# Latin American Independence Movements - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It begins with a paradox that every good answer on this topic has to carry - that between roughly 1808 and 1826 the two largest European empires in the world lost almost the whole of their American territory, and that the societies which emerged from that collapse looked, in their landholding, their labour and their racial ordering, remarkably like the societies that had gone into it. Between those two observations it teaches the colonial order that was being broken: the legal caste hierarchy from peninsular officeholder down through American-born white, mixed-descent, Indigenous and enslaved populations; the land and labour systems that hierarchy existed to protect; and the commercial monopoly that gave a continent's elite a standing economic grievance. It teaches the Bourbon reform programme that tried to make the empire pay and instead manufactured its opposition, and the two great Andean risings of 1780-83 which taught the creole elite that the enemy below was more frightening than the enemy above. It teaches the ideas - the Enlightenment, the American Revolution of 1776, the French Revolution of 1789 and the Haitian Revolution of 1791-1804 - under a discipline that is itself examinable: that inspiration is not causation, and that an answer which explains 1810 by 1776 has not explained why the intervening thirty-four years produced nothing. It teaches Haiti in its own right and not as a prologue, because it is the single case in which independence and social revolution were the same event. It teaches the imperial crisis of 1808 - Bayonne, the abdications, the juntas and the constitutional question they raised; the Cortes of Cadiz and the Constitution of 1812, which offered a liberal empire and were refused from both directions; the Mexican sequence from Hidalgo's rising of 1810 through Morelos to Iturbide's conservative settlement of 1821, which is the sharpest political reversal in the region; the northern campaigns of Simon Bolivar from the lost republics of 1811-14 to Boyaca in 1819, Gran Colombia, Carabobo in 1821 and Pichincha in 1822; the southern campaigns of Jose de San Martin from the Army of the Andes and Chacabuco in 1817 to Chile under O'Higgins, the Peruvian expedition of 1820-22 and the meeting at Guayaquil; the final royalist collapse at Junin and Ayacucho in 1824 and the creation of Bolivia in 1825; and the entirely separate Brazilian path, in which a European court crossed the Atlantic in 1807-08, governed an empire from its own colony, and produced a monarchical separation in 1822 under Pedro I with slavery intact. It teaches the participants in full - creole elites, Indigenous communities, enslaved and free people of colour, women, the clergy, merchants and the popular armed forces on both sides - and it insists that royalist America was as American as patriot America. It teaches what outside powers did and did not do: British commercial and naval interest and recognition, United States recognition and the Monroe message of 1823, and the Holy Alliance intervention that was discussed and never launched. It teaches the outcomes: fragmentation into separate states, the personalist military rule that the wars made possible, the abolition of slavery by instalments across half a century, the survival and in places the worsening of Indigenous land pressure, the export economies and the dependency argument built on them. And it ends with the comparisons, the historiography, the permitted counterfactuals and the answer architecture. The American and French revolutions belong to their own topics and are used here only as inspiration and boundary; the Congress of Vienna and the Concert of Europe belong to the Vienna and Concert of Europe topic, which is where the Holy Alliance and the diplomacy of intervention are taught; the late-nineteenth-century partition of Africa belongs to the New Imperialism owner; twentieth-century Latin America, its United States relationship and its debt crises belong to the twentieth-century Latin America topic; and post-1945 Asian and African decolonisation belongs to the post-1945 decolonisation topic. Here you learn why two empires collapsed in the Americas inside a single generation, who made that happen, what they were fighting for, and why the word "independence" and the word "revolution" cannot be used interchangeably about the result.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is deliberately not the order of a narrative. A narrative account of this topic begins with Bolivar, adds San Martin, mentions Mexico, appends Brazil and concludes that independence was achieved - and that structure produces the single commonest failure in the examination hall, which is a campaign chronicle with a moral at the end. Here the colonial social and economic order is taught before any cause, because an answer that cannot describe what the hierarchy was cannot explain why its beneficiaries led the rebellion or why its victims often did not. The Bourbon reforms are taught before the ideas, because the grievance was material before it was philosophical. Haiti is taught before 1808, because it is chronologically first and because every later actor in the region made decisions with Haiti in mind. The imperial crisis is taught before any campaign, because until 1808 there was no opening and after 1808 there was one everywhere at once. The five theatres are taught separately and then compared, because they had different leaders, different social bases and different outcomes, and the comparison is where the marks are. And the outcomes - abolition, land, fragmentation, personal rule and export dependence - are taught as consequences of the method by which independence was won, not as a list of subsequent misfortunes.

Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Two labels run through every lesson, and keeping them apart is the single habit that separates a confident answer from an over-confident one.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | A verified historical statement: a recorded date, name, instrument, provision, sequence or finding | Assert it. Dates, names, instruments and recorded sequences can be stated flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |

One further habit belongs with those labels, and it is a teaching discipline rather than a caveat. Where a standard connecting name, date or instrument is needed to hold a sequence together, this session gives it instead of retreating into vague formulations such as "an alliance was made", "a parliament met" or "a plan was proclaimed". Precision of that kind is not decoration: an answer that can write *Plan of Iguala, 24 February 1821* is doing something an answer that writes *a settlement was reached* cannot do. Such facts are ordinary verified history and are asserted as ✅ **Fact** like any other; where a point genuinely cannot be settled, it is marked as unresolved instead of being smoothed over.

Five further disciplines are specific to this topic, and each exists because this is a part of the syllabus where a fluent but wrong answer is unusually easy to write.

**The count discipline.** There are **five distinct paths** - northern South America, southern South America, Mexico, Brazil and Haiti - and **one common trigger, the Iberian crisis of 1808, which opened four of them**. Haiti is the exception and must always be marked as one: its revolution began in 1791 from an entirely different trigger, seventeen years before the Spanish monarchy fell, and it is counted among the five paths without being counted among the four the crisis opened. Do not describe the paths as four by folding Haiti into the Caribbean background, and do not inflate them to six by promoting a sub-theatre such as the River Plate or Upper Peru to the status of a separate path. When a lesson treats a sub-theatre, it says so.

**The agency discipline.** The popular version of this topic contains two or three great men and a continent of extras. That grammar is false to the evidence and expensive in the examination. Indigenous communities, enslaved and free people of colour, mixed-descent militias, women, parish clergy and merchant houses made choices here, and many of them chose the king. ⚠️ Royalist forces in Spanish America drew very largely on American manpower in most theatres, although the balance varied by region, by period and by arm, and no figure for the composition of any force is asserted in this session; the qualified point is enough, because an answer that treats royalism as a Spanish import cannot explain why the wars lasted fifteen years. Where the surviving record cannot supply the voices of the poor, the absence is named as a property of the record rather than treated as a property of the past.

**The evidentiary discipline for violence and for numbers.** These were long wars with mass mortality, and no casualty figure for any campaign, any massacre or any repression in the region is asserted here. This session therefore states what was done, by whom, under which policy and with what recorded consequence, and refuses every number it cannot verify. No death toll, army size, population figure, vote share or output series appears anywhere in this session. It also refuses collective attribution: cruelty is attributed to identified commanders, decrees and policies, never to a nation or a people as such.

**The sensitive-identity discipline.** This topic requires constant use of colonial racial categories - peninsular, creole, mestizo, mulatto, pardo, Indigenous, enslaved, free coloured - and those categories are the historical object, not the session's own classification of human beings. They are used here as legal and fiscal statuses created and enforced by a colonial state, they are defined on first use, and claims about what any such group "wanted" are made only where a recorded action, decree or petition supports them. Where a group's position varied by region, the variation is stated instead of being averaged into a single motive.

**The boundary discipline.** Six neighbouring topics own material this one constantly touches. The American Revolution and the French Revolution belong to their own topics; this session uses only their recorded status as examples and their exported constitutional and political vocabulary, and narrates neither. The Congress of Vienna and the Concert of Europe belong to the Vienna and Concert of Europe topic; the Holy Alliance, the congress system and the diplomacy of intervention are that topic's material, and this session uses only the recorded consequence for the Americas. The New Imperialism and the partition of Africa belong to their own topic; the comparison appears here only where it clarifies what kind of decolonisation this was. Twentieth-century Latin America - United States intervention, the Cold War, the debt crisis and the left turn - belongs to its own topic, and this session establishes the inheritance and hands over. Post-1945 Asian and African decolonisation belongs to the post-1945 decolonisation topic and is used here as a bounded comparison only.

*Read the diagram downward once for sequence and then across once for argument. The horizontal pairings are where the marks are. The left-hand column alone produces a story of liberation; the right-hand column alone produces a story of betrayal. The topic is the relation between them: the same social group that had the resources, the literacy, the credit and the officer class to win a fifteen-year war was the social group with the least interest in changing the order that had made it rich. That is not a moral discovery; it is a structural explanation, and it is what an examiner is asking for when a question pairs the word "independence" with the word "society".*

**Scope of this session.** The American Revolution and the French Revolution belong to their own topics; this session uses their recorded status as examples and their exported vocabulary, and narrates neither. The Congress of Vienna, the Concert of Europe and the congress system belong to the Vienna and Concert of Europe topic; Lesson 16 uses only the recorded consequence for the Americas and hands the European diplomacy over. The New Imperialism and the partition of Africa belong to their own topic and appear here only as a controlled comparison in Lesson 20. Twentieth-century Latin America - United States intervention, the Cold War, authoritarian rule and the debt crisis - belongs to its own topic; Lessons 18 and 19 establish the inheritance and stop. Post-1945 Asian and African decolonisation belongs to the post-1945 decolonisation topic and is used only as a bounded comparison. Indian nationalism appears once, in Lesson 20, as a disciplined contrast and never as an analogy.

## Lesson 1 - What "Latin American independence" names, and why one label covers five revolutions

Progress: 1/22 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the region defined; the chronological span; five distinct paths and the one trigger that opened four of them; independence distinguished from revolution; the four things the phrase is wrongly made to mean; what the label licenses you to assert

CA search: not sought - one current linkage only, placed in Lesson 18
CA found: none carried for this lesson

### Visual: one phrase, five separate operations

```text

   INSIDE IT ARE FIVE DIFFERENT EVENTS WITH DIFFERENT

   THE COMMON TRIGGER (shared by four of the five)
   1808: Napoleon invades Spain; the Portuguese court sails for Brazil;
   the legitimacy of the imperial centre collapses before it is attacked

   +-----------------+-----------------+-----------------+-----------------+
   | (1) HAITI       | (2) MEXICO      | (3) NORTHERN    | (4) SOUTHERN    |
   | 1791-1804       | 1810-1821       | SOUTH AMERICA   | SOUTH AMERICA   |
   | begins BEFORE   | popular and     | 1810-1825       | 1810-1824       |
   | the trigger     | Indigenous      | Bolivar         | San Martin      |
   |                 | rising, ends    |                 |                 |
   | made by the     | as a royalist   | continental     | regular armies  |
   | ENSLAVED        | officer's       | republican      | projected from  |
   |                 | conservative    | project         | an external     |
   | slavery ENDS    | settlement      | Gran Colombia   | base            |
   | immediately     |                 |                 |                 |
   +-----------------+-----------------+-----------------+-----------------+
                     | (5) BRAZIL 1807-1822                                |
                     | a European court relocates to its own colony and    |
                     | the heir declares independence as EMPEROR;          |
                     | monarchy retained; slavery retained                 |
                     +-----------------------------------------------------+

   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   THE TOPIC IS NOT "HOW DID LATIN AMERICA BECOME FREE".
   IT IS: WHY DID FIVE DIFFERENT SOCIETIES, USING FOUR DIFFERENT
   METHODS, ARRIVE AT FOUR DIFFERENT SOCIAL OUTCOMES FROM ONE
   COMMON IMPERIAL COLLAPSE?
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
```

*The diagram is the reason "Latin American independence was inspired by the American and French revolutions and led by Bolivar and San Martin" is a weak answer rather than a compressed one. It is true of part of path (3) and part of path (4). It is false of (1), which preceded the trigger and was made by people whom Bolivar's own class feared; it is false of (2), whose independence was finally declared by the royalist commander sent to suppress it; and it is false of (5), which produced a slaveholding monarchy. Five paths, one label.*

### 1. The container word, and the five events inside it

Imagine you are a wealthy landowner in Caracas or Buenos Aires in 1800. You are white, American-born, Catholic and rich. You own estates and possibly enslaved people. You are also, in law, a second-class subject: the top posts in your own city's government, courts and church are reserved in practice for men born in Spain, many of whom arrived last year and will leave in five. You resent this deeply. You have read some Enlightenment political writing. You have heard about a British colony in North America that made itself into a republic.

And you have done nothing about any of it, because you can count. Below you are people you rule and fear: the mixed-descent artisans and cattle-hands of your own city, the Indigenous villages that pay tribute and supply labour to your estate, and the enslaved Africans on the coast. In 1780 an Andean rising led by a claimant to Inca descent shook the whole southern viceroyalty. In 1791 the richest colony in the Caribbean was taken over by the people who had been enslaved on it. You have concluded that any general appeal to liberty is a weapon that can be picked up by more than one pair of hands.

Now move forward to 1808. The king of Spain has been forced off his throne by a French army and is a prisoner in France. There is no legitimate authority in Madrid to obey. The question is not whether you want independence; it is a narrower and more urgent constitutional question - **who governs in the king's absence?** Everything in this topic runs through that question, and the answers people gave to it in 1808 hardened into the wars they fought for the next fifteen years.

### 2. The region, defined exactly

✅ **Fact:** **the area known as Latin America consists of the countries of South America, Central America including Mexico, and islands in the Caribbean Sea such as Cuba, Jamaica and Hispaniola**, the last **divided into two states - Haiti and the Dominican Republic**. ✅ **These states gained their independence from Spain - in the case of Brazil, from Portugal - in the early nineteenth century.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three clarifications make the definition usable in an answer.

| Point | Precise statement | Why it matters in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| "Latin" is a later label | The term describes the Romance-language colonial inheritance of Spain, Portugal and France in the Americas | It is a nineteenth-century and later coinage applied backwards; the people in this session did not call themselves Latin Americans |
| The region is not one polity | It was administered as separate viceroyalties, captaincies and audiencias under two crowns, with a French colony in between | This is the structural reason why one collapse produced many states rather than one |
| Not all of it became independent in this period | ✅ Cuba is named by the source as part of the region, and Spanish rule there did not end in this period | An answer that says "Spain lost the Americas by 1826" must add "on the mainland" |

### 3. The chronological span and the ten markers that fix it

✅ **Fact:** the subject's master chronology dates **Latin American Independence to c. 1808-1826**, records **Bolivar and San Martin campaigns**, and states that **most mainland Spanish colonies were independent by the mid-1820s**. ✅ The subject's revision chart dates the same era **c. 1808-1826**, summarises it as the retreat of the Spanish and Portuguese empires from the mainland Americas, and records its distinctive feature as **creole-led states that retain major social inequalities** and **independence without complete social revolution**.

✅ **Fact:** ten markers fix the sequence, and they are the chronological spine used throughout.

| Marker | ✅ Date | ✅ What it fixes |
|---|---|---|
| Haitian Revolution begins | 1791 | The first major anti-slavery revolutionary break in the region |
| Haiti becomes independent | 1804 | The first free Black republic of the modern era |
| Napoleon invades Spain; the Portuguese court shifts to Brazil | 1808 | The Iberian imperial crisis opens the door to colonial autonomy |
| Revolts begin in Spanish America; Hidalgo issues the Grito de Dolores in Mexico | 1810 | The independence phase turns mass and regional |
| San Martin crosses the Andes into Chile | 1817 | Southern liberation takes military shape |
| Bolivar secures New Granada and builds Gran Colombia | 1819 | Northern liberation gains momentum |
| Mexico becomes independent | 1821 | Spanish rule weakens decisively in North America |
| Brazil becomes independent under Pedro I | 1822 | Portuguese America follows a different path |
| Ayacucho breaks major royalist power in South America | 1824 | The Spanish military position collapses |
| Bolivia emerges as an independent state | 1825 | Bolivar's northern campaign reaches symbolic completion |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice what the spine already tells you before any explanation is offered. The revolution that abolished slavery is the earliest and is over before the mainland wars begin. The trigger is external to the region. Independence arrives in Mexico and Brazil in consecutive years by opposite methods. And the last royalist army is broken two years after the first independent monarchy is proclaimed. A chronology that is merely memorised is decoration; a chronology whose shape carries an argument is evidence.

### 4. Independence and revolution: the distinction the whole topic turns on

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four words are used loosely in weak answers and must be separated here, because the examiner's favourite question on this topic lives exactly in the gap between them.

| Term | Precise meaning | What it does *not* mean |
|---|---|---|
| **Independence** | The transfer of sovereignty: the end of an external state's authority to govern a territory | It says nothing whatever about who governs afterwards, or on what social terms |
| **Revolution** | A transformation of the structure of social and political power, not merely of its external source | It is not a synonym for a war, a rising or a change of flag |
| **Secession** | The withdrawal of a territory and its existing ruling group from a larger polity, keeping its internal order | This is the closest single word for what happened on most of the Spanish American mainland |
| **Decolonisation** | The general process by which colonial rule ends | It covers a settler-elite separation in 1821 and an anti-colonial mass movement in 1960, which is why the word alone explains nothing |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the standard formulation of the outcome is that **political decolonisation outran social democratisation**. That sentence is the topic in eight words, and the thesis templates in Lesson 22 are all developments of it. Haiti is the control case that proves the distinction is real rather than rhetorical: there, independence and revolution were the same event, and the difference in outcome is visible immediately.

### 5. What the phrase is wrongly made to mean - four overstatements

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** each of the following is a marked error, and each is committed routinely.

1. **That all the movements were the same kind of event.** ✅ Haiti, Mexico, Spanish America and Brazil followed different social and political paths, and treating them as identical republican-national revolts is the first error to avoid.
2. **That independence created equality.** ✅ The owners record the correction directly: social and racial hierarchies often survived. Power frequently remained with creole elites.
3. **That Bolivar and San Martin were doing the same thing in the same place.** ✅ The correction is recorded: their theatres overlapped only partly and their political roles differed. Lesson 13 shows exactly where and how they met.
4. **That Brazil followed the Spanish American route.** ✅ Brazil became independent in **1822** under **Pedro I** by a monarchical path, and the contrast with the Spanish American republics is explicit.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a fifth error is less often noticed and more damaging. It is the assumption that the wars were fought between Spaniards and Americans. They were not. They were, to a very large extent, civil wars inside American society, in which American troops fought on both sides, and the royalist side frequently held the loyalty of Indigenous communities, mixed-descent militias and whole regions. Lesson 15 builds this out; it is introduced here because an answer that imagines two neatly national armies cannot explain a fifteen-year war.

### 6. What this lesson licenses you to say, and what it does not

| You may assert | You may not assert |
|---|---|
| ✅ That the region as defined includes South America, Central America with Mexico, and Caribbean islands including Hispaniola, divided into Haiti and the Dominican Republic | That the whole region became independent in this period; Cuba did not |
| ✅ That the span is c. 1808-1826 and that most mainland Spanish colonies were independent by the mid-1820s | A precise count of new states; no such count is held here |
| ✅ That there was one common trigger and five distinct paths | That there were four paths or six, or that the trigger of 1808 also opened the Haitian revolution |
| ✅ That political decolonisation outran social democratisation | That independence was therefore a failure or a fraud; that is a verdict, not a finding, and Lesson 21 tests it |
| ⚠️ That the wars were substantially civil wars within American society | Any figure for the composition of any army; none is verified here |

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Insisting on five paths is pedantry. Every historical process contains internal variation; if you subdivide any revolution far enough it stops being one thing. The category "Latin American independence" is useful precisely because it names a single regional outcome - the collapse of Iberian rule on the American mainland within one generation - and an answer that spends its opening insisting on heterogeneity will have no room left to explain the outcome.

**Reply:** The objection is right that heterogeneity is not by itself interesting, and an answer that only catalogues difference deserves the marks it gets. But the five-path distinction is not subdivision for its own sake; it is the mechanism by which the topic's central question is answered. The question an examiner sets is almost never "what happened"; it is "why did independence not produce social transformation", or "compare the paths", or "assess Haiti's significance". Each of those demands requires the variation, because the variation *is* the evidence. ✅ Haiti abolished slavery immediately and is the region's only case of independence as social revolution; ✅ Mexico began as a popular and Indigenous rising and ended in a conservative settlement led by a royalist officer, the sharpest reversal in the region; ✅ Brazil separated monarchically and comparatively without violence, and slavery persisted long after independence. Those three facts, held together, explain the outcome that the objection wants explained: the social result tracked the social composition of the winning coalition. Strip the variation out and you are left with an assertion that elites betrayed a revolution, which is a sentiment rather than an argument.

**Residual:** What the distinction cannot do is fix the boundaries of the region or the period with any precision. ✅ The owners date the era c. 1808-1826 while beginning their own chronology at 1791, which is a deliberate inconsistency reflecting Haiti's status as both inside the region and outside the Spanish imperial crisis. Nothing here resolves whether the Haitian Revolution is best taught as the first event of this topic or as a separate Caribbean revolution that this topic must account for. This session teaches it as both - Lesson 5 treats it in its own right, and every later lesson treats it as a factor in other people's calculations - and records that the classification is a historian's convenience.

### UPSC application

The syllabus clause that owns this topic names **colonization** and **decolonization** and **their forms and effect on the society**, and Latin American independence fits that frame directly. Read the clause carefully: "forms" licenses a question about the *type* of decolonisation, and "effect on the society" licenses a question about what changed below the level of the state. Those two words are why the social-outcome question is the most probable Mains framing on this topic, and why an opening paragraph should establish the paradox rather than the chronology. Build it from this lesson: the span, the common trigger, the count of paths, and a thesis clause stating that sovereignty was transferred faster than social power. That is four sentences, and it pre-empts the commonest opening failure, which is to begin with a description of how vast and resource-rich South America is.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Treating "Latin American independence" as one event with one cause and one outcome.
- Using "independence" and "revolution" as synonyms, and therefore being unable to answer the question that pairs them.
- Describing the paths as four by absorbing Haiti into background, or as six by promoting a sub-theatre.
- Asserting that Spain lost the Americas by 1826 without the qualifier "mainland".
- Giving a number for the new states created; none is verified here.
- Imagining the wars as Spaniards against Americans rather than as civil wars inside American society.
- Beginning the answer at 1810 or at Bolivar, which loses both the trigger and the control case.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Region: South America, Central America including Mexico, and Caribbean islands including Hispaniola - Haiti and the Dominican Republic.
2. ✅ Independence from Spain; from Portugal in the case of Brazil; early nineteenth century.
3. ✅ Span: c. 1808-1826; most mainland Spanish colonies independent by the mid-1820s.
4. ✅ Ten markers: 1791 Haiti begins; 1804 Haiti independent; 1808 Iberian crisis; 1810 Spanish American revolts and the Grito de Dolores; 1817 Andes crossing; 1819 New Granada and Gran Colombia; 1821 Mexico; 1822 Brazil; 1824 Ayacucho; 1825 Bolivia.
5. ⚠️ **Five paths** - Haiti, Mexico, northern South America, southern South America, Brazil - and **one trigger, 1808, which opened four of them**. ⚠️ Haiti is the exception: its revolution began in 1791 from a different trigger. Never four; never six.
6. ⚠️ Independence = transfer of sovereignty. Revolution = transformation of social power. The topic lives in the gap.
7. ✅ Owners' verdict: political decolonisation outran social democratisation; creole-led states retained major social inequalities.
8. ✅ Four recorded corrections: the movements were not identical; independence did not create equality; Bolivar and San Martin did not work identically in one theatre; Brazil did not follow the Spanish American route.
9. ⚠️ Fifth correction, carried by this session: these were substantially civil wars within American society.
10. ✅ Cuba is inside the region and outside this period's independence.
11. ⚠️ Syllabus hooks: "colonization", "decolonization", "forms", "effect on the society".
12. ⚠️ Opening architecture: span, trigger, five paths with the four it opened, paradox thesis - four sentences.

### Concept check

**Question:** A script opens: "Inspired by the American and French Revolutions, the people of Latin America rose under Bolivar and San Martin and won their freedom by 1826." Identify the three separate errors in that sentence and rewrite it as an opening that would earn marks.

**Model answer:** The first error is causal: inspiration is asserted as cause, when the ideas were available for decades before anything happened and the actual opening was the collapse of the Spanish monarchy in 1808. The second is social: "the people" conceals that the movements were led by American-born white elites whose position depended on the existing hierarchy, and that large parts of the Indigenous, mixed-descent and enslaved populations fought for the king or did not fight at all. The third is descriptive: Bolivar and San Martin account for two of five paths, and the sentence therefore erases Haiti, which abolished slavery and was the only case where independence was social revolution; Mexico, which began as a popular rising and ended in a conservative settlement under a royalist officer; and Brazil, which separated as a slaveholding monarchy in 1822. A marks-earning opening would read: between about 1808 and 1826 the Spanish and Portuguese empires lost almost all of mainland America, but the five paths to that outcome differed so sharply in leadership and social base that they produced opposite social results - immediate abolition in Haiti, a conservative settlement in Mexico, creole republics in Spanish South America, and a slaveholding empire in Brazil - which is why political decolonisation in this region outran social democratisation.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that naming the variation is a digression from the question. The variation is the evidence for the answer; an answer that treats the region as homogeneous cannot explain its own conclusion, because the only thing that explains why social outcomes differed is that the winning coalitions differed.

*The next lesson supplies what this one assumed. It has been said that the creole elite had a grievance, that it feared those below it, and that its position depended on an order worth preserving. None of that has yet been described. Before any cause can be weighed, the colonial order itself - who held office, who owed labour, who owned land and who was permitted to trade - has to be put on the table, because it is the thing the wars did and did not change.*

## Lesson 2 - The order that had to be broken: office, caste, land, labour and trade

Progress: 2/22 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the office hierarchy from peninsular to creole; the legal caste order and its fiscal logic; Indigenous tribute and community land; enslaved and free coloured populations; hacienda and plantation; the commercial monopoly; how the four grievances distribute across groups

Book context: queried - the colonial social table of peninsulares, creoles, mestizos, Indigenous peoples and enslaved Africans, the land and labour institutions, and the long-run agrarian structure
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage sits in Lesson 18
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four separate hierarchies, stacked

```text
   THE COLONIAL ORDER WAS NOT ONE LADDER. IT WAS FOUR,
   AND THEY DID NOT LINE UP NEATLY.

   (A) OFFICE                (B) LEGAL CASTE           (C) LAND AND LABOUR
   ------------------        -----------------         ---------------------
   PENINSULARES              Spaniards (peninsular     GREAT ESTATE / HACIENDA
   born in Spain or          and American) - full      owner: creole or
   Portugal; hold the        legal standing, tax       peninsular
   top offices               exemptions                     |
        |                          |                   resident and debt-bound
   CREOLES                   MESTIZO / PARDO /         workers; seasonal
   American-born whites;     MULATTO - intermediate    Indigenous labour
   WEALTHY but barred in     legal status, subject           |
   practice from the top     to restrictions          PLANTATION (coast,
        |                          |                  Caribbean, Brazil)
   everyone else:            INDIGENOUS - legally      enslaved African labour
   excluded from             separate; pay TRIBUTE;         |
   high office               hold COMMUNITY LAND      INDIGENOUS COMMUNITY LAND
   altogether                       |                 held collectively; the
                             ENSLAVED AFRICANS -      state protects it because
                             property in law          it protects the tribute

   (D) TRADE
   -------------------------------------------------------------------
   Colonial commerce routed, taxed and licensed through the imperial
   system. Producing for a distant monopoly is profitable; being
   prevented from selling to the highest bidder is a permanent
   grievance for exactly the people who own the most to sell.
   -------------------------------------------------------------------

   WHY FOUR LADDERS AND NOT ONE:
   a creole is at the TOP of (B), near the top of (C), and BLOCKED in (A).
   That single mismatch - social supremacy plus political exclusion -
   is the engine of the whole topic.
```

*The diagram explains why the revolt came from the second rung rather than the bottom. A group that is at the base of every ladder has no instrument. A group that is at the top of every ladder has no grievance. The creole elite was at the top of the social and economic ladders and blocked on the political one, which gave it both a motive to act and the resources to act with - and, equally important, a powerful reason to make sure that when it acted, ladders (B) and (C) were not disturbed.*

### 1. A landowner's position, looked at from the inside

Colonial rule in Spanish America was not primarily a matter of soldiers. It was a matter of law, office and revenue. A small number of men sent from Spain held the senior positions in the viceregal administration, the high courts and the senior church posts. Around them was a large, rich, educated, American-born white population that ran the local economy, sat on city councils, owned the estates and mines, supplied the priests and lawyers, and was permanently reminded that the top of its own society was reserved for men from somewhere else.

Underneath that white population was a legally graded society. The law did not treat everyone in the same way and did not pretend to. It assigned different tax obligations, different labour obligations, different rights to bear arms, to enter universities, to hold office and to marry, according to categories of descent. And at the bottom were two populations whose labour paid for everything: Indigenous communities, taxed directly and bound to supply work, and enslaved Africans, who were property in law.

The question to hold onto is this: **if you are the creole elite, what exactly do you want to change?** You want the offices. You want the trade restrictions gone. You do not want the tribute system dismantled, because you benefit from the cheap labour it organises. You certainly do not want a general emancipation, because you own people. That is not a moral failing peculiar to Spanish America; it is a description of an interest, and it predicts the outcome of the wars better than any amount of quotation from political philosophy.

### 2. The office hierarchy, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** the top of the structure is unambiguous. **Peninsulares** - Europeans born in Spain or Portugal - **held the top colonial offices**, and they were **resented by creoles**. ✅ **Creoles** were **American-born whites: wealthy but politically restricted**, and they **became the leading independence elites**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the precise content of the grievance matters, because a vague version of it is worth nothing in an answer. It was not that creoles were poor; many were far richer than the officials placed above them. It was not that they were excluded from all public life; they sat on municipal councils and dominated local society. It was that the senior, decision-making, revenue-controlling and appeal-hearing posts were, in practice, closed to them, and that the men who held those posts were transients with careers elsewhere. ✅ **Fact:** the standard designation for this structure is that the highest colonial offices - viceroy, captain-general, and the judgeships of the high courts known as *audiencias* - were filled overwhelmingly from Spain, and that eighteenth-century policy deliberately reduced the creole share of *audiencia* appointments that had grown in the previous century.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two consequences follow, and both are examinable. First, the grievance was about *status and access*, which is why it produced demands for self-government rather than for social change. Second, because it was a grievance about who holds office rather than about how society is arranged, satisfying it did not require disturbing anything below the white population - and in the event, it did not.

### 3. The legal caste order, and the fiscal logic underneath it

✅ **Fact:** further down that structure, **mestizos, Indigenous peoples and enslaved Africans** were **socially and politically subordinate**, and while a **mass base existed**, the **benefits of independence were uneven**.

✅ **Fact:** the colonial legal vocabulary for this graded order is standard and should be used precisely.

| Category | What the term names | Legal and fiscal position |
|---|---|---|
| **Peninsular** | A person born in Spain or Portugal | Full standing; the pool from which senior office was filled |
| **Creole** (*criollo*) | American-born person of Spanish or Portuguese descent | Full legal standing in most respects; excluded in practice from the highest office |
| **Mestizo** | A person of mixed Indigenous and European descent | Intermediate standing; not tribute-paying as Indigenous, not privileged as Spanish |
| **Mulatto / pardo** | A person of mixed African and European descent; *pardo* is the usual term in northern South America | Intermediate and restricted; barred from many offices, guilds and institutions; often liable to a head tax |
| **Indigenous** | Members of the legally distinct "republic of Indians" | Separate jurisdiction; liable to **tribute**; entitled to protected **community land**; subject to labour drafts |
| **Enslaved African** | Property in law | No civil standing; concentrated in plantation zones, mines, ports and urban service |
| **Free person of colour** | Manumitted or free-born people of African descent | Legally free, socially and legally restricted; a large and militarily important group in the Caribbean and coastal South America |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the system is usually taught as social prejudice with a legal veneer. That gets the causation backwards. The categories were maintained because they were **fiscally and administratively productive**. Indigenous status generated tribute revenue and labour drafts; separate jurisdiction made those obligations enforceable; restrictions on mixed-descent groups protected the guild, office and military privileges of those above them. A state that abolished the categories would have abolished a revenue stream and a labour supply at the same time. Hold that thought, because it explains a later puzzle: why republics that abolished caste distinctions on paper in the 1810s and 1820s so often found reasons to keep collecting Indigenous tribute afterwards.

⚠️ **Analytical inference, stated as a discipline:** these categories are the historical object of study, not a classification this session endorses. They were legal and fiscal statuses created and enforced by a colonial state, they varied in meaning between regions, and individuals moved between them through wealth, marriage, military service and documentary reclassification. Where this session says that a group "supported" or "opposed" something, it is reporting a recorded action, petition or decree in a stated region - never an assumption about what people of a given descent naturally wanted.

### 4. Land and labour: what the hierarchy was protecting

✅ **Fact:** the long-run agrarian structure of the region persisted for a stated reason: **agriculture remained backward because peasant labour was so plentiful and cheap that wealthy estate owners had no need to go to the trouble of modernizing**, with whole countries **dominated by huge estates whose owners were all-powerful, and who ruled their peasants like feudal monarchs**, and with **states dominated by groups of wealthy landowners**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that passage describes a later period, and this session does not import its dates. What it establishes for the present lesson is the *shape* of the structure and its self-reproducing logic: abundant cheap labour removes the incentive to change anything, which is precisely why a change of sovereignty could leave the agrarian order untouched.

✅ **Fact:** the standard terminology for the colonial land and labour systems is as follows, and an answer should use it exactly.

| Institution | What it was |
|---|---|
| **Encomienda** | An early grant of the right to extract labour and tribute from specified Indigenous communities; in decline by this period but the origin of much later landholding |
| **Hacienda** | The large private estate that became the dominant rural form, worked by resident labourers, tenants and seasonal Indigenous workers, frequently bound by debt |
| **Mita** (Andes) | A rotational compulsory labour draft on Indigenous communities, most infamously for the silver mines |
| **Tribute** | The head tax owed by Indigenous adults, collected through community authorities, and a principal source of colonial revenue |
| **Community land** | Land held collectively by Indigenous communities, legally protected because the community had to remain viable in order to pay tribute and supply labour |
| **Plantation** | Export agriculture worked by enslaved African labour - sugar above all - dominant in the Caribbean, coastal Brazil and coastal New Granada and Venezuela |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the paradox inside colonial protection of Indigenous land. The crown's legal protection of community holdings was not benevolence; it was revenue policy. That has a consequence for Lesson 17 that must be flagged now rather than sprung later: when independent republics adopted liberal doctrines of individual property and legal equality, the legal shield around community land was weakened at the same time as the caste categories were abolished - so a single reform could deliver formal equality and material dispossession together.

### 5. Trade: the grievance of the people with the most to sell

✅ **Fact:** the commercial position is equally direct: **colonial monopoly and regulation bred resentment**, and **economic grievance mattered**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the structure of the grievance is what makes it politically explosive rather than merely irritating. A colonial commercial system routes trade through designated ports, licensed merchants and imperial taxation. That arrangement transfers part of the value of American production to peninsular merchant houses and to the treasury. The people who feel it most sharply are the owners of exportable production - estate owners, mine owners, cattle ranchers and the port merchants who serve them - which is to say, exactly the creole elite already blocked on the office ladder. Economic exclusion and political exclusion therefore fell on the same shoulders, and that coincidence is the reason the grievance became a programme.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two qualifications keep this honest. First, the monopoly was porous: contraband, licensed neutral trade in wartime and periodic imperial relaxations meant that the practical experience of restriction varied by region and by decade. Second, and importantly for Lesson 19, the free-trade demand was not a demand for economic independence. It was a demand to sell to whoever paid most - which in practice meant Britain. A region that won free trade in the 1820s had swapped a Spanish commercial monopoly for a British commercial preponderance, and an answer that celebrates the first without noticing the second has only read half the ledger.

### 6. The grievance map: who wanted what

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this table is the most useful single object in the lesson, because it converts a static social description into a prediction about behaviour - and the prediction is what an answer needs.

| Group | Principal grievance | What it therefore wanted | What it did *not* want |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Creoles** | ✅ Wealthy but politically restricted; ✅ trade monopoly | Access to office; commercial freedom; self-government | Any disturbance of caste, land or slavery |
| **Mestizos, pardos and free people of colour** | ✅ Legally and socially subordinate | Removal of caste restrictions; access to office, guilds, militia rank | Not a unified programme; in several regions the crown was a more reliable protector than the local creole elite |
| **Indigenous communities** | ✅ Subordinate; tribute; labour obligations | Relief from tribute and labour; protection of community land | Not necessarily a new republic, which offered no guarantee on land and had removed the crown's protective jurisdiction |
| **Enslaved Africans** | ✅ Subordinate; property in law | Freedom | Nothing in the creole programme offered it - which is why the decisive enslaved intervention in this topic occurs in Haiti |
| **Peninsular officials and merchants** | Had the offices and the commerce | Continuity of the imperial system | Any devolution of office or opening of trade |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read the table's second, third and fourth rows together and a central fact of the topic falls out. The creole programme answered the creole grievance and nobody else's. Therefore the creole leadership, in order to raise armies, would have to *purchase* the participation of those below it with promises - of freedom, of tribute relief, of rank - and the shape of post-independence society depends heavily on which of those promises were kept. Lesson 15 traces the bidding war; Lesson 17 audits the payments.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This lesson reduces politics to material interest. People are not calculators. Creoles read Enlightenment writers and meant what they wrote; Bolivar freed his own enslaved workers; several republics legislated against slavery and caste within months of declaring themselves. Explaining every action by structural position is a cynicism that the evidence does not require, and it makes the genuine radicalism of parts of the movement invisible.

**Reply:** The objection is a necessary corrective and should be conceded in an answer rather than ignored, because the radical measures were real and are themselves evidence. But the concession has to be exact. What the structural account claims is not that individuals were insincere; it is that *programmes* which threatened the material base of the leading coalition were the ones that failed, were postponed, or were passed and then not enforced. That claim is testable, and the record supports it. ✅ Slavery was abolished immediately only in Haiti, where the enslaved themselves made the revolution; elsewhere ✅ emancipation came piecemeal and later. ⚠️ Caste distinctions were abolished in law while social hierarchy persisted in practice. ✅ Power remained with creole elites. So both things are true: individual radicalism existed and is historically important, and the structural constraint determined which radicalism survived contact with the post-war settlement. An answer that holds both is a better answer than one that chooses.

**Residual:** What the record available here cannot supply is the internal experience or the collective intention of the subordinated groups. No petition series, vote, census, landholding table or wage series is held here. This session therefore reports recorded actions - who joined which army, which decrees were issued, which obligations continued - and refuses to convert those into statements about what "the Indigenous population" or "the pardos" believed. The silence is a property of the record available here, and it is stated rather than filled.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies the second paragraph of almost every answer on this topic, and it is the paragraph that separates a script which asserts that hierarchy survived from one that explains why it survived. Use the four-ladder structure rather than a caste list: the decisive fact is the *mismatch* between social supremacy and political exclusion in one group. For a 10-mark answer, two grievances are enough - office exclusion and trade restriction - provided each is named precisely. For 15 and 20 marks, add the fiscal logic of the caste order and the land and labour systems, because those are what make the continuity argument in the final paragraph evidential rather than rhetorical. Where a question asks about "effect on the society", this lesson is the baseline against which the effect is measured; you cannot show that something did not change without first describing it.

### Where answers on the colonial order go wrong

- Writing that creoles were "oppressed", which is imprecise and invites the reply that they owned the country. They were socially dominant and politically excluded, and the precision is the point.
- Presenting the caste order as prejudice alone, which loses the tribute and labour revenue that made it durable.
- Treating Indigenous communities as a single actor with a single interest across a continent.
- Assuming the enslaved population's interests were represented anywhere in the creole programme.
- Forgetting that colonial protection of Indigenous community land was fiscal, so that liberal individual-property reform could dispossess while equalising.
- Describing the trade grievance as a demand for economic independence rather than for the right to sell in a wider market.
- Supplying population, revenue, landholding or output figures; none is verified here.
- Treating the four hierarchies as a single ladder, which hides the mismatch - social supremacy with political exclusion - that made the creole elite act at all.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Peninsulares: Europeans born in Spain or Portugal; held top colonial offices; resented by creoles.
2. ✅ Creoles: American-born whites; wealthy but politically restricted; became the leading independence elites.
3. ✅ Mestizos, Indigenous peoples and enslaved Africans: socially and politically subordinate; mass base existed; benefits uneven.
4. ⚠️ Four ladders - office, legal caste, land and labour, trade - which do not align; the creole mismatch is the engine.
5. ✅ Viceroy, captain-general and *audiencia* judgeships filled overwhelmingly from Spain; eighteenth-century policy cut the creole share.
6. ✅ Vocabulary: peninsular, creole, mestizo, mulatto/pardo, Indigenous, enslaved, free person of colour.
7. ✅ Institutions: encomienda, hacienda, mita, tribute, community land, plantation.
8. ⚠️ The caste order was fiscally productive: tribute and labour drafts depended on separate Indigenous legal status.
9. ⚠️ Colonial protection of community land was revenue policy, not benevolence - which is why liberal reform could equalise and dispossess simultaneously.
10. ✅ Trade restriction: monopoly and regulation bred resentment; economic grievance mattered.
11. ⚠️ Office exclusion and commercial exclusion fell on the same group, which is why a grievance became a programme.
12. ⚠️ The creole programme answered only the creole grievance; participation from below therefore had to be purchased with promises.
13. ✅ Long-run structure: cheap abundant labour removed the incentive to modernise; states dominated by wealthy landowners; no tradition of democracy except in Chile.
14. ⚠️ Discipline: caste terms are the historical object, defined on first use, never used to impute motives without a recorded action.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the leadership of the independence movements came from the group that was second in the colonial hierarchy rather than from the groups at the bottom, and state the consequence of that fact for the social outcome.

**Model answer:** Leadership came from the creole elite because it was the only group that combined a serious grievance with the means to act on it. Creoles were American-born whites who were wealthy but politically restricted: they were excluded in practice from the viceregal, judicial and senior ecclesiastical posts that were filled from Spain, and they bore the cost of a commercial system that routed and taxed their exports through the imperial monopoly. At the same time they held the estates, the mines, the credit, the literacy, the municipal councils and the militia commissions, so they could finance and officer a war. Groups below them had a far deeper grievance and almost no instrument: Indigenous communities were bound into tribute and labour obligations under a separate jurisdiction, mixed-descent populations were legally restricted, and enslaved Africans were property in law. The consequence for the social outcome follows directly. The creole grievance was about who holds office and who may trade, and satisfying it required no change to caste, land or slavery - all of which underpinned creole wealth. So when the wars ended, power remained with creole elites, social and racial hierarchies survived, and emancipation came piecemeal and late except in Haiti, where the enslaved made the revolution themselves. The social result tracked the social composition of the winning coalition.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the creole grievance as a mild or unreal one because creoles were rich. Exclusion from the senior offices of one's own society, by men who are transient outsiders, is a political grievance of the first order, and dismissing it makes the whole topic incomprehensible. The correct formulation is not that the grievance was trivial but that it was *narrow*: real, deeply felt, and satisfiable without changing anything below the white population.

*The next lesson supplies the change that turned a standing grievance into an active opposition. Colonial hierarchies of this kind had existed for two centuries without producing a continental war. Something made the empire more intrusive, more expensive and more closed to creoles in exactly the decades before 1808, and it was not a failure of imperial energy - it was a burst of it.*

### Visual: a reform loop that feeds back into revolt

```text

   an empire that produces enormous wealth and delivers
   too little of it to the treasury, defended by too few

                          v

   | new intendancies;    | higher and better-   | comercio libre:      |
   | professional         | collected taxes;     | more imperial ports  |
   | officials sent from  | state monopolies on  | opened to one        |
   | Spain; creole share  | tobacco and spirits; | another; more trade, |
   | of high office CUT   | more revenue remitted| more revenue, more   |
   | new viceroyalties    | to Spain             | competition          |
   +----------------------+----------------------+----------------------+

          +---------------+---------------+---------------+
          |                               |               |
   CREOLE ELITES              INDIGENOUS AND            REGIONAL
   lose office; lose          RURAL POPULATIONS         MERCHANTS
   the informal               face harder tax           of old monopoly
   accommodation with         collection and            ports lose their
   transient officials        forced purchase           privileged position
          |                          |                        |
          |                    1780-83: ANDEAN RISINGS         |
          |                    1781: COMUNEROS OF NEW GRANADA  |
          |                          |                        |
          +---------------+----------+------------------------+
                          |
   THE LESSON EACH SIDE DREW - AND THEY DREW OPPOSITE ONES
   MADRID: the empire needs more control, not less
   CREOLES: our exclusion is now policy, not accident
   CREOLES, SECOND LESSON: mass mobilisation is DANGEROUS TO US
                          |
                          v
   A GRIEVANCE THAT IS ORGANISED, ARTICULATE AND ARMED-AVERSE
   waiting for an opportunity it cannot itself create
```

*The diagram is the answer to the question "why 1808 and not 1750 or 1850?" The reforms did not cause independence; ✅ the recorded trigger is the Iberian crisis. What they did was convert a diffuse resentment into a specific, documented, continent-wide political grievance held by the only group capable of acting, while simultaneously teaching that group to be terrified of the only force large enough to overthrow an empire quickly.*

### 1. Why reform is the dangerous phase of an empire

Empires that are going badly get reformed. By the middle of the eighteenth century the Spanish monarchy had concluded, correctly, that it was extracting far less from its American possessions than the possessions were worth, that its defences there were inadequate against a British navy, and that its administration had been quietly captured by local interests - partly because offices had been sold, and partly because officials who spent decades in one place married into the families they were supposed to regulate.

The remedy was a programme of administrative, fiscal and commercial modernisation. Judged by its own objectives it worked: revenue rose substantially, administration became more direct, and trade within the empire expanded. Judged by its political consequences it was a catastrophe, because every one of its successes took something away from somebody who had previously had it, and the people it took the most from were the people who owned the colonies' economy.

This is a general pattern worth carrying to other topics: **the dangerous moment for an empire is not its neglectful phase but its reforming phase**, because reform withdraws privileges that habit has turned into rights.

### 2. The reform programme, named exactly

✅ **Fact:** the eighteenth-century Spanish programme is standardly called the **Bourbon reforms**, after the dynasty that ruled Spain from the start of that century, and it reached its most intensive phase under Charles III in the second half of the century. Its principal components are the following.

| Component | What it did | Who it affected |
|---|---|---|
| **New viceroyalties** | New Granada, and later the River Plate, were separated from existing jurisdictions to make administration and defence more effective | Shifted regional centres of gravity; created new capitals and new rivalries - the River Plate capital's rise is directly relevant to Lesson 12 |
| **The intendancy system** | Professional officials with wide fiscal and administrative powers were placed over districts, bypassing older office-holders | Reduced local and creole influence over revenue and justice |
| **Reduced creole officeholding** | Deliberate policy cut the creole share of *audiencia* and senior appointments that had grown when offices were sold | The precise, documentable form of the grievance in Lesson 2 |
| **Fiscal intensification** | Higher and better-collected sales and customs duties; expanded state monopolies, notably in tobacco and spirits | Merchants, producers, urban consumers and rural taxpayers |
| **Military reform** | Expanded colonial militias, with commissions and legal privileges attached | Created armed, organised creole and mixed-descent units - the officer corps of the later wars |
| **Expulsion of the Jesuits: 1759 in the Portuguese empire, 1767 in the Spanish empire** | The order was expelled and its extensive properties and missions taken over - Portugal acting first, Spain following eight years later | Removed an institution deeply embedded in creole education and in Indigenous mission communities; a long-remembered grievance |
| **Comercio libre, culminating in the decree of 1778** | Opened many more ports in Spain and America to trade with one another, ending the old restriction of legal traffic to a very few designated ports | Expanded imperial trade, but on imperial terms; benefited some regions and damaged the monopoly position of others |

✅ **Fact:** the Portuguese empire ran an analogous programme in the same decades, associated with the minister usually known as Pombal, involving administrative centralisation, the earlier expulsion of the Jesuits from Portuguese territories in **1759** - eight years before Spain followed - chartered monopoly companies and a shift of the Brazilian capital southward to Rio de Janeiro - which matters directly for Lesson 14, because it is the reason the relocated court in 1808 arrived at a capital that was already the empire's American centre.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice that **comercio libre** is a false friend. It does not mean free trade; it means freer trade *inside* the empire. The monopoly was loosened, not abolished, and the destination remained Spain. ✅ The owners record the grievance as **colonial monopoly and regulation**, and an answer that translates *comercio libre* as free trade converts a partial concession into a solution and then cannot explain why the grievance persisted.

### 3. What the reforms did to each group

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the political effect of the programme is best seen group by group, because it did not produce a single united opposition - and the absence of unity is as important as the presence of grievance.

| Group | Effect of the reforms | Political consequence |
|---|---|---|
| **Creole elites** | Offices withdrawn; tax burden raised; commercial position disturbed; a treasured religious order expelled | Grievance becomes specific and documented; but they had no wish for a general upheaval |
| **Peninsular officials and merchants** | Positions strengthened; new appointments flowed to them | Their interest was now openly opposed to the creoles', sharpening a division that had been social and became political |
| **Indigenous communities** | Harder tribute collection; continued forced-purchase arrangements imposed by district officials; heavier labour and fiscal pressure | The population most immediately provoked - and the one that rose first |
| **Mixed-descent and free coloured populations** | Militia service offered rank and legal privileges; simultaneous social restrictions continued | An ambiguous position: real opportunities inside the imperial system, real humiliations outside it |
| **Regional economies** | Some gained from wider imperial trade; some lost protected positions | Regional divergence - a structural reason why the later revolts were regional rather than continental |

### 4. The Andean risings of 1780-83 and the Comuneros of 1781

✅ **Fact:** the reform decades produced two mass revolts of the first importance, and both preceded the independence wars by a generation.

- In the Andes in **1780**, a rising began under **Jose Gabriel Condorcanqui**, who took the name **Tupac Amaru II** as a claimant of Inca descent, directed initially against the abuses of district officials, the forced purchase system and fiscal exactions. It spread very widely across the southern Andes, developed a parallel movement in Upper Peru associated with **Tupac Katari**, and was suppressed with extreme severity by **1783**.
- In New Granada in **1781**, the **Comuneros** revolt arose against new fiscal impositions, drew in a broad provincial coalition including creoles, mobilised in large numbers, extracted concessions by negotiation - and then saw those concessions repudiated and its leadership punished once the authorities were in a position to do so.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these two episodes do far more work in an answer than their length suggests, and they do it in three ways.

1. **They prove that the grievance was not confined to the elite.** Fiscal intensification hurt the rural and Indigenous populations most, and they responded first and hardest.
2. **They explain the creole fear that shapes everything afterwards.** ⚠️ A creole landowner in Cuzco or Bogota who lived through the early 1780s learned that an appeal to justice against colonial officials could, within weeks, become an assault on the whole structure of white property and authority. That memory is a principal reason why, when the opportunity came in 1808, creole elites acted in the name of the captive king, insisted on legality, and tried to keep mobilisation within controllable limits.
3. **They demonstrate a mechanism that recurs.** In both cases, negotiation was used to buy time and the settlement was repudiated afterwards. That pattern - concession under pressure, repudiation once strong - reappears in the imperial response to the juntas of 1808-10, and it is one of the strongest reasons why autonomy demands hardened into independence demands.

✅ **Fact:** the Andean rising is also the historical reference that the region's later Indigenous movements have most often invoked. It is recorded here because it is factually standard, not to import twentieth-century politics into the nineteenth; the later use belongs to another topic.

⚠️ **Evidentiary discipline:** no casualty figure, participant number or area measurement for either rising appears in this session. The scale of the Andean rising is described as very large in every standard account, and that qualitative statement is all that can responsibly be asserted.

### 5. Reform as a cause: stated carefully, then limited

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the reforms are best classified as a **predisposing cause** - something that makes a system vulnerable - rather than as a **precipitant**, which is what actually triggers the break. The distinction is worth making explicitly in an answer because it is the difference between a monocausal script and a graded one.

| Causal role | What belongs in it here | Test |
|---|---|---|
| **Background condition** | The colonial hierarchy, land and labour systems and commercial monopoly of Lesson 2 | Present for two centuries without producing continental war |
| **Predisposing cause** | The Bourbon and Pombaline reform programmes; the sharpened creole exclusion; the fiscal burden; the revolts of 1780-83 and their memory | Present for decades; produced revolts and grievances but no independence movement |
| **Enabling or permissive condition** | The availability of a political vocabulary and of foreign examples - Lesson 4 | Necessary for the form the demands took; insufficient for the demands to be made |
| **Precipitant** | ✅ The Iberian crisis of 1808 | Independence movements begin immediately after it and not before |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the honest limit on the reform explanation is that its effects were not uniform and that many of its beneficiaries fought for the king. Militia reform armed and promoted mixed-descent populations who frequently stayed royalist. Comercio libre enriched some creole regions. A model in which reform simply manufactured rebels cannot account for royalist America, and Lesson 15 will show that royalist America was large.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** The Bourbon reforms are given more weight here than the compact standard summaries of this topic will carry. Those summaries record trade restriction and creole political restriction as grievances; they do not narrate a reform programme, do not name intendancies or the Jesuit expulsion, and do not mention the Andean risings at all. The central claim of this lesson - that reform organised the opposition - is therefore a reconstruction rather than a quotation.

**Reply:** The objection correctly identifies the evidential status of the lesson, and that status is stated rather than hidden. But three points defend the lesson's inclusion. First, the grievance itself requires it: trade restriction and creole political restriction are recorded grievances, and a grievance has to have a history, or the answer is left asserting that creoles were dissatisfied without being able to say since when or why it worsened. Second, the material carried is standard and uncontroversial - the reform programme, the 1778 commercial decree, the expulsion of the Jesuits, the intendancies, the risings of 1780-83 - and teaching that refuses such connecting essentials is forced into vague formulations that no examiner can reward. Third, the lesson is deliberately careful about causal weight: it classifies the reforms as predisposing rather than precipitating and states the trigger as 1808, which is where the record places it. What is added is the *mechanism* by which a standing hierarchy became an active grievance, and the fact that this mechanism is reconstructed rather than quoted is itself part of the teaching.

**Residual:** The relative weight of fiscal, administrative and commercial grievance within the creole position cannot be settled from anything held here, and no attempt is made to settle it. Nor can the record available here establish how far the memory of the 1780-83 risings actually governed creole decisions in 1808-10; that the memory existed is certain, that it disposed the elite toward legality and caution is an inference stated as one, and the alternative view - that creole caution in 1808 was simply a realistic assessment of Spanish power - is equally available on the evidence held here.

### UPSC application

This lesson is what converts a causes paragraph from a list into an argument. The structure to use is the four-row causal ladder of section 5: background condition, predisposing cause, enabling condition, precipitant. It takes one sentence per row, it demonstrates causal grading without using the word "multi-causal", and it protects you from the commonest causes-answer failure, which is to place the Enlightenment, the colonial hierarchy, Bourbon taxation and Napoleon side by side as though they were four items of the same kind. If a question asks specifically why independence came when it did, the ladder *is* the answer: everything above the last row had been present for decades.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Translating *comercio libre* as free trade, and thereby dissolving the grievance the reforms were supposed to have caused.
- Treating the reforms as a cause of independence rather than as a predisposing cause, and then being unable to explain the timing.
- Omitting the Andean and Comuneros revolts, which removes both the evidence that grievance reached below the elite and the explanation of creole caution.
- Presenting the reforms as uniformly resented, which cannot account for royalist America - and treating militia reform as merely military, when it created the officer class that later fought on both sides.
- Supplying figures for revolt participation, casualties or revenue increases; none is verified here.
- Forgetting the Portuguese parallel, which is the reason Rio de Janeiro was ready to receive a court in 1808.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ The dangerous phase for an empire is the reforming phase, because reform withdraws privileges that habit has made into rights.
2. ✅ Bourbon reforms; most intensive under Charles III; new viceroyalties of New Granada and the River Plate.
3. ✅ Intendancy system: professional officials with fiscal and administrative powers, bypassing older officeholders.
4. ✅ Deliberate reduction of the creole share of senior and *audiencia* appointments.
5. ✅ Fiscal intensification; state monopolies including tobacco and spirits.
6. ✅ Militia expansion: commissions and privileges; the future officer corps of both sides.
7. ✅ Expulsion of the Jesuits: **1759** from Portuguese territories, **1767** from the Spanish empire. Portugal acted first.
8. ✅ *Comercio libre*, decree of 1778 - freer trade **inside** the empire, not free trade.
9. ✅ Pombaline counterpart in the Portuguese empire; capital shifted to Rio de Janeiro - the precondition for 1808.
10. ✅ 1780-83: Andean rising under Jose Gabriel Condorcanqui, taking the name Tupac Amaru II; parallel Upper Peru movement under Tupac Katari; suppressed with great severity.
11. ✅ 1781: Comuneros of New Granada; fiscal grievance; broad coalition; concessions granted and then repudiated.
12. ⚠️ Three uses of the revolts: grievance reached below the elite; creole fear of mass mobilisation; concession-then-repudiation as a recurring imperial method.
13. ⚠️ Causal ladder: background condition -> predisposing cause -> enabling condition -> precipitant (✅ 1808).
14. ⚠️ Honest limit: reform effects were uneven; many beneficiaries remained royalist.
15. ⚠️ No casualty, participation or revenue figure is held for any of this.

### Concept check

**Question:** A question asks why the Spanish American independence movements began when they did. Explain why an answer that starts with the Bourbon reforms is only half right, and show how to structure the causal claim correctly.

**Model answer:** It is half right because the reforms explain the *depth* and *specificity* of the grievance but not its *timing*. The reform programme - new viceroyalties, intendancies, fiscal intensification, state monopolies, the expulsion of the Jesuits in 1767, and the deliberate reduction of the creole share of senior office - made exclusion a matter of policy rather than of custom, and it did so in the same decades in which commercial regulation was loosened only within the empire by the decree of 1778 rather than genuinely opened. But those measures were in place for a generation before any independence movement began, and the mass revolts they provoked in the Andes in 1780-83 and in New Granada in 1781 were suppressed without producing one. The correct structure grades the causes. Background condition: a colonial hierarchy of office, caste, land and trade, present for two centuries. Predisposing cause: the reform programme, which converted resentment into an articulate and documented grievance held by the group with the means to act, while also teaching that group to fear mass mobilisation. Enabling condition: an available political vocabulary and foreign examples, which shaped the form of the demands. Precipitant: the Iberian crisis of 1808, which removed the legitimate authority everyone had been obeying and created the opening in every territory at once. The reforms explain why the powder was dry; 1808 explains the spark.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading *comercio libre* as free trade and therefore concluding that the commercial grievance had been settled before 1808. The 1778 decree opened more imperial ports to one another; it did not permit Americans to trade with whoever paid most, which is what they actually wanted and what Britain was offering informally throughout the period.

*The next lesson takes up the enabling condition named in the causal ladder. The creole elite did not invent its political language. It borrowed one - from European political writing, from a successful North American secession and from a French revolution - and it also watched, with horror rather than admiration, what that language did in the hands of the enslaved population of a Caribbean sugar colony. The question is how much of the outcome those borrowings actually explain.*

## Lesson 4 - Ideas and examples: what inspiration explains, and what it cannot

Progress: 4/22 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the Enlightenment vocabulary; the American example of 1776; the French example of 1789 and its two faces; Haiti as the example that frightened; the inspiration-is-not-causation discipline; the boundary with the American and French Revolution owners

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the recorded influences on the region - Enlightenment, 1776, 1789 and Haiti - and Britannica on the Haitian Revolution
CA search: not sought - the one current linkage is held back for Lesson 18
CA found: none carried for this lesson
