━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four examples, four different messages

```text
   THE SAME CREOLE ELITE READ ALL FOUR. THEY DID NOT
   ALL SAY THE SAME THING, AND TWO OF THEM SAID
   OPPOSITE THINGS TO THE SAME READER.

   ENLIGHTENMENT POLITICAL WRITING
   supplies a VOCABULARY: natural rights, the social contract,
   sovereignty residing in the nation, separation of powers,
   representative government, equality before the law
   -> tells you HOW TO ARGUE; does not tell you WHEN TO ACT
                          |
   1776 - THE NORTH AMERICAN EXAMPLE
   a settler elite separates from a European metropolis,
   writes a constitution, becomes a viable republic
   -> MESSAGE: "it can be done, and by people like us"
                          |
   1789 - THE FRENCH EXAMPLE, WITH TWO FACES
   FACE ONE: rights of man; abolition of legal privilege;
             popular sovereignty -> A PROGRAMME
   FACE TWO: mass mobilisation; terror; the destruction of a
             propertied order; war -> A WARNING
                          |
   1791-1804 - THE HAITIAN EXAMPLE
   the enslaved apply the same principles to themselves,
   destroy the plantation order and found a state
   -> MESSAGE TO CREOLE READERS: NOT "it can be done"
      but "THIS IS WHAT HAPPENS IF WE LOSE CONTROL"

   ------------------------------------------------------------
   NET EFFECT ON CREOLE ELITES:
   a full vocabulary of liberty
   + a demonstration that separation is feasible
   + a demonstration that liberty is contagious
   = A MOVEMENT THAT SPEAKS RADICALLY AND ACTS CAUTIOUSLY
   ------------------------------------------------------------
```

*The diagram explains a feature of the topic that puzzles students: why the documents of Spanish American independence are so much more radical than its social outcomes. The vocabulary was borrowed whole, and it was genuinely believed by many of the men who used it. The caution was learned from two of the same examples. A declaration can be generous and its author can still be determined that the generosity stop at the boundary of his own estate.*

### 1. The gap that inspiration cannot fill

There is a standard and slightly lazy sentence in answers on this topic: "the Latin American revolutions were inspired by the American and French revolutions." It is not false - ✅ the Enlightenment, the American Revolution, the French Revolution and Haiti did influence the region, and that influence was intellectual and political inspiration. The trouble is that the sentence, on its own, proves nothing and predicts nothing.

Consider the dates. The North American declaration is of 1776. The French Revolution begins in 1789. The Haitian Revolution begins in 1791 and produces an independent state in 1804. ✅ The first mainland revolts begin in 1810. If ideas were doing the causal work, the gap of thirty-four years from the first example, and of twenty-one from the second, needs an explanation - and the only explanation available is that something else had to happen first.

So the question for this lesson is not whether the ideas mattered. They did. The question is *what kind* of causal work they performed, and the answer is that they supplied **form** rather than **impulse**: they determined what the movements said, how they justified themselves, what institutions they built and what language their constitutions used, without determining that they would occur or when.

### 2. The Enlightenment: what was actually borrowed

✅ **Fact:** the **Enlightenment** stands among the ideas and examples that **influenced the region** and provided **intellectual and political inspiration**; the movements are accurately described as **anti-colonial struggles shaped by Iberian crisis and Enlightenment-era ideas**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the useful move in an answer is to say what specifically was taken, because "Enlightenment ideas" as a phrase is worth almost nothing. Four transfers are visible in the documents and institutions of the period.

| What was borrowed | What it was used to do |
|---|---|
| **Sovereignty residing in the nation or the people rather than in the monarch's person** | This is the decisive one. It is the doctrine that answers the question of 1808 - if the king is gone, authority reverts to the people who conferred it - and it converts a caretaker junta into a constituent authority |
| **Natural rights and equality before the law** | Justified the abolition of formal caste distinction, and supplied the standard against which the later failure to deliver equality would itself be measured |
| **Constitutionalism and separation of powers** | Determined the *form* of the new states: written constitutions, elected congresses, separated powers, declared rights |
| **Political economy favouring open commerce** | Gave the commercial grievance of Lesson 2 a theoretical language and a programme |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sovereignty doctrine deserves particular attention because it is the hinge between the ideas and the trigger. Spanish political tradition itself contained an older version of the same claim - that in the absence of a legitimate king, authority returns to the kingdoms and to their communities. That is why the juntas of 1808 could be simultaneously traditionalist and revolutionary, and why the men who formed them could insist, with complete sincerity, that they were defending the king rather than rebelling. Lesson 6 develops this; it is flagged here because it shows how a borrowed doctrine took hold - by fusing with something already present rather than by displacing it.

### 3. The American example: the demonstration effect, stated and bounded

✅ **Fact:** the **American Revolution** stands among the recorded influences on the region.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** what the North American case supplied was a **demonstration effect** of a precise kind. It showed that a settler-descended colonial elite, without a professional army of its own at the outset, could separate from a European power, survive the war, and construct a durable federal republic. For a Caracas or Buenos Aires landowner, the relevance was social as well as political: the successful rebels were people of his own kind, defending property and self-government, not a rising of the poor.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three limits must be attached, and attaching them is what distinguishes an argued comparison from a gestured one.

1. **The institutional starting point was different.** The North American colonies had long-established representative assemblies and a tradition of colonial self-government; Spanish America had municipal councils and no comparable tradition of legislative self-rule. ⚠️ The owners record this as the reason independence in Spanish America created states with armies but without an established civilian political class. That is a very large difference and it explains much of the divergence in outcome.
2. **The social structure was different.** Spanish American societies were majority non-white, legally stratified and in places heavily dependent on enslaved labour, which made any general appeal to rights far more dangerous to the people making it.
3. **The demonstration cut both ways in Madrid too.** Spain had assisted the North American rebels against Britain; the example was therefore not a foreign curiosity but a recent imperial policy whose implications were understood on both sides.

**Boundary.** The American Revolution's causes, course, constitutional settlement and its own social limits belong to its owner. This session uses only its recorded status as an example and the exported constitutional vocabulary, and does not narrate it.

### 4. The French example, and its two faces

✅ **Fact:** the **French Revolution** stands among the recorded influences on the region.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the French case is the one that most rewards careful treatment, because it operated on the region in two opposite directions at the same time.

- **As a programme**, it supplied the most forceful available statement of popular sovereignty, the abolition of legal privilege and equality before the law - and it was, unlike the North American example, explicitly universal in its claims, which made it directly applicable to colonial caste society.
- **As a warning**, it supplied the image of a propertied order destroyed by mass mobilisation, of a revolution that could not be stopped at the point its originators intended, and of a decade of war. ⚠️ Creole elites drew both lessons, and the second is why so many of them, in 1808-10, went to considerable lengths to keep their movement legal, monarchical in form and socially closed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** there is also a hard material link that is often missed. The French Revolution's colonial consequences produced Haiti. The abolition decreed in the French colonies during the revolutionary decade, and the subsequent attempt to reverse it, are the immediate context of the Haitian war of independence. For a Spanish American reader, therefore, "the French Revolution" and "the destruction of the richest plantation colony in the Caribbean" were not two separate items of news; they were one continuous story.

**Boundary.** The French Revolution's causes, phases, terror, Napoleonic outcome and European consequences belong to its owner. This session uses its exported vocabulary, its two-faced demonstration effect and its role in producing the Haitian crisis, and narrates none of its internal history.

### 5. Haiti as an example: the one that changed behaviour most

✅ **Fact:** **Haiti** stands among those influences too, and it showed that **anti-colonial and anti-slavery revolution could be more socially radical than creole-led movements elsewhere**. ✅ Britannica describes the Haitian Revolution of **1791-1804** as a struggle that **ended French rule and created the first state founded by former slaves**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Haiti's influence on the mainland elites was overwhelmingly **deterrent**, and saying so is not a cynical reading but the one that explains the evidence. It supplied an answer to the question every creole planter asked himself about revolutionary language - what happens if the people I own take this seriously? - and the answer was the destruction of his class. The consequences run right through the topic:

- it is one reason why mainland movements avoided general emancipation and preferred conditional, military and gradual routes to freedom;
- it is a reason why royalist recruitment among enslaved and free coloured populations was often successful, since the crown could plausibly present itself as the protector of the socially subordinate against a slaveholding creole elite;
- it is why the new republics were slow to recognise Haiti diplomatically, and why Haiti remained, in the region's official imagination, a warning rather than a precedent.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and yet the deterrent reading is not the whole of it. Haiti also gave material assistance to the mainland struggle at a critical moment, on terms that were explicitly abolitionist - a point developed in Lesson 10, where it belongs chronologically. The disciplined formulation is that **Haiti was an inspiration to the enslaved and a warning to the enslavers, and both effects operated on the same events**.

### 6. The discipline: inspiration is not causation

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most portable single control in the session, and it applies to several other World History topics.

| Claim | Status | Why |
|---|---|---|
| "The Enlightenment and the Atlantic revolutions influenced the region" | ✅ Recorded and assertable | The owners state it directly |
| "They supplied the vocabulary, the constitutional forms and the justification" | ⚠️ Strong inference, defensible | Visible in the institutions and documents produced |
| "They caused the independence movements" | ⚠️ Not supportable | Present for decades without effect; ✅ the recorded trigger is 1808 |
| "Without them the movements would not have happened" | ⚠️ Counterfactual, not assertable | Untestable here; the most that can be said is that the movements would have taken a different political form |
| "The movements were imitations of the American or French revolutions" | ⚠️ False | Different social base, different trigger, different outcome; and the Mexican and Brazilian paths are not imitations of anything |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sentence to memorise, because it converts the whole lesson into one line of an answer: **the Atlantic revolutions gave Spanish America its political language and its constitutional models; the collapse of the Spanish monarchy in 1808 gave it its opportunity; and the colonial social structure gave it its limits.** Three clauses, three different kinds of cause, and no single-factor claim anywhere.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This lesson demotes ideas too far. The men who led these movements were not merely borrowing a convenient vocabulary; they wrote constitutions, abolished caste distinction in law, legislated against the slave trade, founded republics rather than kingdoms, and several of them acted directly against their own material interest in doing so. To classify ideas as "form rather than impulse" is to assume that material interest is real and conviction is decorative, which is a theoretical prejudice rather than an empirical finding.

**Reply:** The objection is right that conviction was real and that treating it as ornament would be both unhistorical and unfair, and an answer should say so explicitly. But the lesson's claim is narrower than the objection supposes. It is not that ideas were insincere; it is that ideas cannot explain *timing*, and that timing is precisely what a "why did it happen when it did" question asks about. The evidence for the narrower claim is simply the chronology: the vocabulary was available and admired for decades, and ✅ the revolts begin in 1810, immediately after the imperial collapse of 1808. Where the objection lands successfully is on outcome rather than cause: the ideas explain why the new states were republics with written constitutions and declared rights rather than, say, independent monarchies on the Brazilian model, and they explain why formal caste abolition happened so quickly and so widely. That is a large and genuine causal contribution. The disciplined position is therefore a division of labour - ideas explain form and much of the institutional outcome; the imperial crisis explains timing; social structure explains limits - and that division is stronger than either the idealist or the materialist version alone.

**Residual:** What cannot be established from the record available here is the depth and distribution of Enlightenment reading among the creole population, or how far the leadership's public language reflected private conviction. No readership, subscription, library or education data is held here. The session therefore treats the ideas as demonstrably present in the documents and institutions, and makes no claim about how widely they were held.

### UPSC application

Three uses. First, this is the paragraph that prevents the commonest causes-answer error, which is to open with inspiration and never reach the trigger; the three-clause sentence in section 6 does the whole job in one line and leaves room for evidence. Second, when a question directly names the American or French Revolution and asks about its influence or legacy - which is a recurring shape in this subject's verified routing - this topic's contribution is exactly one or two sentences on the exported vocabulary and the demonstration effect, followed by a hand-over; do not annex that question. Third, when a question asks how far Latin American independence was a revolution, section 5's treatment of Haiti is the hinge, because Haiti is the case that makes the distinction between the two words visible.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Asserting that the Atlantic revolutions caused the Latin American movements, and thereby leaving the thirty-four-year gap unexplained.
- Writing "Enlightenment ideas" without saying which ideas did what work.
- Treating the French Revolution's influence as purely encouraging, and so missing the deterrent effect that shaped creole caution.
- Treating Haiti as an inspiration to the creole leadership; it was overwhelmingly a warning to them, and an inspiration to those they ruled.
- Narrating the American or French Revolution at length, which trespasses on another topic and spends marks on material that is not being examined.
- Claiming that the movements were imitations, which cannot accommodate Mexico or Brazil.
- Quoting Enlightenment authors or revolutionary documents; no verbatim text is held here.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The owners record Enlightenment, the American Revolution, the French Revolution and Haiti as influences supplying intellectual and political inspiration.
2. ⚠️ Ideas supplied form, not impulse: vocabulary, justification, constitutional models. ⚠️ The decisive borrowed doctrine is sovereignty residing in the nation rather than in the monarch's person - the doctrine that answers 1808.
3. ⚠️ It fused with an older Spanish tradition that authority reverts to the kingdoms when the king is absent, which is why juntas could be traditionalist and revolutionary at once.
4. ⚠️ American example = demonstration effect: a settler elite can separate and build a durable republic.
5. ⚠️ Three limits on that comparison: no comparable tradition of legislative self-government; a legally stratified, majority non-white society; Spain's own earlier support for the North American rebels.
6. ⚠️ French example has two faces: a universal programme and a warning about uncontrollable mobilisation.
7. ⚠️ The French revolutionary decade produced the Haitian crisis; for creole readers these were one story, not two.
8. ✅ Haiti 1791-1804 ended French rule and created the first state founded by former slaves; ✅ it showed that anti-colonial revolution could be more socially radical than creole-led movements.
9. ⚠️ Haiti's effect on mainland elites was chiefly deterrent; it was an inspiration to the enslaved and a warning to the enslavers.
10. ⚠️ Consequences of the deterrent effect: avoidance of general emancipation; successful royalist recruitment among the socially subordinate; slow recognition of Haiti.
11. ⚠️ The memorisable line: the Atlantic revolutions gave the region its political language and constitutional models; 1808 gave it its opportunity; colonial social structure gave it its limits. ⚠️ Boundary: the American and French revolutions are narrated by their own topics, not here.
12. ⚠️ No quotation from any Enlightenment author or revolutionary document is held here.

### Concept check

**Question:** "The Latin American revolutions were the children of the American and French revolutions." Assess this claim, distinguishing clearly between what it gets right and what it cannot explain.

**Model answer:** It gets the intellectual genealogy right and the causation wrong. What is right is that the Enlightenment, the American Revolution and the French Revolution are recorded influences on the region, and that their contribution was substantial and specific: the doctrine that sovereignty resides in the nation rather than in the monarch's person, which supplied the answer to the constitutional question of 1808; natural rights and equality before the law, which justified the rapid abolition of formal caste distinction; constitutionalism and separation of powers, which determined that the new states would be republics with written constitutions, elected congresses and declared rights; and a political economy of open commerce that gave the trade grievance a programme. The North American case added a demonstration effect of a socially reassuring kind, since the successful rebels were a settler-descended propertied elite. What the claim cannot explain is timing, and timing is usually the question. The vocabulary and the examples were available from 1776 and 1789, and nothing happened on the mainland until 1810, immediately after the Iberian crisis of 1808 destroyed the legitimacy of the imperial centre. The claim also cannot accommodate the fourth influence. Haiti's revolution of 1791-1804, which ended French rule and created the first state founded by former slaves, operated on creole elites as a warning and not as a model, and it is one reason why the mainland movements spoke a radical language while avoiding general emancipation. The disciplined formulation divides the labour: the Atlantic revolutions explain the form and much of the institutional outcome, the imperial collapse explains the timing, and the colonial social structure explains the limits.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that a revolution's influence is always encouraging. Two of the four examples here were read by the decisive actors primarily as warnings, and a candidate who cannot see that will be unable to explain why movements that used such generous language produced such guarded settlements.

*The next lesson stops treating Haiti as a factor in other people's calculations and takes it on its own terms. It is the earliest event in this topic, the only one in which the people at the bottom of the colonial order made the revolution themselves, and the only one in which independence and social transformation were the same act - which makes it the control case against which every later claim in this session is tested.*

## Lesson 5 - Haiti: independence when the enslaved rather than the creoles made it

Progress: 5/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Saint-Domingue as the richest plantation colony; the four-sided conflict of 1791; Toussaint Louverture; the French expedition and the war of independence; 1 January 1804; Dessalines; what Haiti proves and what it cost; the 1825 indemnity; why Haiti is the control case

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the Haitian dates of 1791 and 1804, the named leaders, and Britannica on the first state founded by former slaves
CA search: not sought - Haiti carries no current anchor in this session
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a four-sided conflict, not a two-sided one

```text
   SAINT-DOMINGUE, 1789-1791: FOUR GROUPS, FOUR PROGRAMMES,
   AND NO POSSIBLE COMPROMISE AMONG THEM

   GRANDS BLANCS                     PETITS BLANCS
   wealthy white planters            poorer whites: overseers,
   want: commercial autonomy         artisans, small traders
   from France; slavery              want: racial supremacy
   untouched; NO rights for          preserved; NO rights for
   free people of colour             free people of colour
              \                            /
               \                          /
                \      FREE PEOPLE OF COLOUR
                 \     (free, often propertied, some
                  \     slaveholding, legally restricted)
                   \    want: the political equality the
                    \   revolutionary language promises them
                     \  -> the FIRST group to act on 1789
                      \        |
                       \       |
   ==================== THE ENSLAVED MAJORITY ====================
   the overwhelming demographic majority of the colony
   want: FREEDOM - which none of the three groups above offers
   -> the northern plain rises in AUGUST 1791
   ===============================================================
                              |
   PLUS TWO EXTERNAL ARMIES ENTERING THE VACUUM
   Spain (from the eastern side of the island) and Britain (by sea),
   each hoping to take the richest colony in the Caribbean
                              |
                              v
   A WAR IN WHICH EVERY SIDE AT SOME POINT ALLIES WITH EVERY OTHER,
   AND ONLY ONE DEMAND IS NEVER NEGOTIABLE: FREEDOM
```

*The diagram is the reason Haiti cannot be summarised as "slaves revolted and won". Four internal parties with irreconcilable aims, two invading powers and a metropolitan revolution changing its own policy repeatedly produced a conflict in which alliances reversed several times. What survived every reversal was the enslaved population's refusal to return to the plantation on the old terms - and because that was the one demand no settlement could contain, it determined the outcome.*

### 1. One announcement, four incompatible readings

Take the most profitable colony in the world. Its wealth comes from sugar and coffee produced by enslaved people who are, by a very large margin, the majority of its population. Its white population is itself divided between wealthy planters and a poorer white population whose only asset is legal whiteness. Between them sits a substantial group of free people of African descent - legally free, often prosperous, sometimes themselves slaveholders, and systematically excluded from political rights.

Now let a revolution in the metropolis announce that men are born free and equal in rights, and let the announcement arrive in a colony where the majority of men are owned. Every group reads the announcement differently. The planters read it as a licence for colonial self-government. The poorer whites read it as an assertion of their own equality with the planters and emphatically not with anyone darker. The free people of colour read it as their claim to the civil rights they have been denied. And the enslaved majority read it exactly as it is written.

Three of those four readings can be argued about in an assembly. The fourth cannot, because it is not a claim for a better position within the order; it is the abolition of the order. That is why Haiti is a different kind of event from everything else in this session.

### 2. Haiti in the record: dates, leaders and verdict

✅ **Fact:** the **Haitian Revolution begins in 1791**, and its significance is that it is the **first major anti-slavery revolutionary break in the region**.

✅ **Fact:** **Haiti becomes independent in 1804** and is recorded as the **first free Black republic in the modern era**.

✅ **Fact:** its leaders were **Toussaint Louverture, Dessalines and others**, and the path is the **most radical social revolution in the region**.

✅ **Fact:** Britannica describes the **Haitian Revolution (1791-1804)** as a struggle that **ended French rule and created the first state founded by former slaves**.

✅ **Fact:** the analytical position is equally clear: Haiti **showed that anti-colonial and anti-slavery revolution could be more socially radical than creole-led movements elsewhere**; it is **the only case where independence *was* social revolution**; and in answer writing it is **the best device for showing that "Latin American independence" was not one single social model**.

✅ **Fact:** one factual control governs every answer here: **do not date Haitian independence to 1791**. The **revolution begins 1791**; **independence is 1804**.

### 3. Saint-Domingue to Haiti: the sequence in full

✅ **Fact:** the colony's French name was **Saint-Domingue**, occupying the western third of the island of Hispaniola, the eastern part of which was Spanish. ✅ Hispaniola **is divided into two states - Haiti and the Dominican Republic**, which is the modern form of that same division.

✅ **Fact:** the standard connecting sequence is as follows, and an answer that needs more than the two owner dates should use it in this form.

| Stage | ✅ Date | What happened |
|---|---|---|
| Free-coloured claim for political rights, and its violent suppression | 1790-91 | The revolutionary language is first turned against the colonial order by people who are already free |
| Rising of the enslaved population in the northern plain | August **1791** | The dated beginning of the revolution |
| Spain and Britain intervene, each seeking the colony | 1793 onward | The conflict becomes an international war fought on Haitian soil |
| French revolutionary abolition of slavery in the colonies | 1793-94 | Emancipation is decreed by the metropolis, converting former insurgents into French commanders |
| **Toussaint Louverture** becomes the dominant authority in the colony | later 1790s | A formerly enslaved man governs the colony, drives out the invaders, and restores production without restoring bondage |
| Autonomous constitution promulgated in the colony | 1801 | Self-government under French sovereignty, with slavery prohibited |
| French expeditionary force sent to restore the earlier order | 1802 | Napoleonic France attempts reversal; Toussaint is seized and deported |
| **Toussaint Louverture dies in captivity in France** | 1803 | Leadership passes to his lieutenants; the war becomes explicitly a war of independence |
| Final defeat of the French force; **independence proclaimed** | 1 January **1804** | ✅ Haiti independent; **Jean-Jacques Dessalines** leads the new state |
| The name | 1804 | The new state took an Indigenous Taino name for the island rather than the colonial one |
| French recognition purchased | **1825** | France recognised Haitian independence by royal ordinance in return for an indemnity of **150 million francs**, reduced in **1838** to **90 million** |

⚠️ **Evidentiary discipline:** **no casualty figure** is asserted for any phase of this war - not for the rising, not for the repression, not for the French expedition, not for the disease mortality that is standardly held to have devastated it. Mortality was very heavy on all sides; that qualitative statement is what is asserted here, and no number is given, estimated or implied.

⚠️ **Sensitive-evidence discipline:** the enslaved population of this colony is described here by what it did and what was done to it under named policies, and not by any characterisation of what enslaved people as a category were like. Where this session says "the enslaved population rose", it is reporting a recorded event in a named region in a named month, not a collective psychology.

### 4. Why the reversal failed, and what that establishes

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the decisive analytical point in Haiti is not that a rising occurred - risings occurred in every slave society - but that a **restoration was attempted and defeated**. That sequence is what converts a revolt into a revolution, and it establishes four things that the rest of this session uses.

1. **Emancipation, once general, could not be reversed by force.** The French expedition of 1802 had the resources of the strongest land power in Europe and a competent command, and it failed. ⚠️ The reason usually given is a combination of universal resistance and catastrophic disease mortality, and this session records both without weighting them numerically.
2. **Independence became necessary only after reversal was attempted.** Before 1802 the colony's leadership sought autonomy within the French empire with slavery abolished. Independence was the response to the attempt to restore bondage. ⚠️ This is an important structural parallel with the mainland, where autonomy demands hardened into independence demands only after Spain refused them - and the parallel is worth making in an answer because it shows the pattern is general.
3. **A social revolution can produce a defensive and militarised state.** The new state was founded under conditions of invasion, embargo and the certainty of further attack, and its institutions reflected that. ⚠️ Any assessment of Haiti's subsequent difficulties that ignores the circumstances of its founding is an assessment of the wrong thing.
4. **The international system punished the outcome.** ✅ Recognition by the former metropolis came only in **1825** and only in exchange for a very large indemnity, later reduced. ⚠️ That transaction - a state compelled to compensate its former owners for its own freedom - is the single most economical piece of evidence available for the argument that the post-independence international order was not neutral between social outcomes.

### 5. The comparison that makes Haiti the control case

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the following table is the most valuable object in this lesson for answer writing, because it isolates the variable. Haiti and the mainland faced comparable imperial situations; the difference is who made the revolution.

| Axis | ✅ Haiti | ✅ Mainland Spanish America |
|---|---|---|
| Who made it | The enslaved majority, with free people of colour | Creole elites, with mixed-descent and Indigenous forces recruited by both sides |
| What was demanded | Freedom from bondage, then independence | Office, commercial freedom, self-government |
| Effect on slavery | Ended immediately and irreversibly | Piecemeal and later; ✅ emancipation was immediate only in Haiti |
| Effect on landholding | The plantation order was destroyed as a system of ownership | The hacienda and the plantation survived |
| Effect on the ruling group | Replaced entirely | Confirmed: ✅ power remained with creole elites |
| External response | Diplomatic isolation; ✅ recognition bought in 1825 for an indemnity | Recognition by Britain and the United States in the 1820s; capital and trade followed |
| Classification | Independence **and** social revolution | Independence **without** social revolution |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** one sentence is worth memorising as the topic's sharpest single line: Haiti is the region's control case, showing what independence looked like when the enslaved rather than the creoles made it - and why the creole-led movements elsewhere took care that it would not.

### 6. What Haiti is wrongly made to mean

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three errors recur, and each is expensive.

1. **Treating Haiti as a footnote or a Caribbean preamble.** ✅ It was **historically central as the first successful slave revolution**, and an answer that omits it cannot make the revolution-versus-independence distinction at all.
2. **Dating independence to 1791.** ✅ The owners prohibit this directly. 1791 is the revolution's beginning; 1804 is independence.
3. **Reading Haiti's later poverty backwards as a verdict on social revolution.** ⚠️ This is the most tempting and the least defensible. The new state faced invasion, an attempted restoration, diplomatic isolation and an indemnity demanded as the price of recognition. An answer that attributes subsequent difficulty to the radicalism of the revolution, while ignoring the penalties imposed on it, has assumed its conclusion. The disciplined formulation is that Haiti demonstrates both the possibility of genuine social revolution in this period and the cost the international order attached to it.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Haiti does not belong in this topic at all. It was a French colony, not an Iberian one; its revolution began seventeen years before the trigger that produced every other movement here; its causes were the specific dynamics of a sugar colony with an overwhelming enslaved majority; and it was not part of the Spanish imperial crisis in any respect. Including it inflates the topic and imports a Caribbean revolution into a discussion of Iberian imperial collapse.

**Reply:** The objection is strong on classification and weak on function, and the reply should concede the first to win the second. It is entirely true that Haiti is chronologically and imperially separate, and this session says so rather than smoothing it over. But three considerations make its inclusion necessary rather than decorative. First, it is one of the five paths and stands first in the chronology as the region's opening event. Second, it is causally connected to the others in both directions - it shaped creole calculation continuously, as Lesson 4 showed, and it materially assisted the mainland struggle at a critical moment, as Lesson 10 will show. Third, and decisively, it is the only available control. Every claim this session makes about why independence did not produce social transformation depends on being able to point to a case where it did, made by different people, and to isolate the variable. Remove Haiti and the central argument of the topic becomes an assertion about elite motives that cannot be tested. The disciplined position is therefore that Haiti is not a Spanish American independence movement and is an indispensable part of the analysis of Spanish American independence.

**Residual:** The relative weight of resistance and disease in defeating the French expedition cannot be settled from anything held here, and no numerical claim about either is made. Nor can the record available here establish the internal social settlement of the new state - its labour arrangements, its landholding pattern or the position of its own cultivators after independence - and the session therefore stops at the destruction of the plantation order as a system of ownership and does not describe what replaced it.

### UPSC application

Haiti is the highest-value single paragraph available on this topic, and it should appear in any 15- or 20-mark answer about social outcomes, about whether independence was revolutionary, or about comparison within the region. The construction is four sentences: the dates 1791 and 1804 correctly distinguished; who made it; what it did to slavery and to the ruling group; and the 1825 indemnity as the price of recognition. Deployed at the end of a paragraph arguing that mainland independence left hierarchy intact, it converts an assertion into a controlled comparison, because it shows that the alternative outcome was historically available and not merely imaginable. Where a question asks you to assess Haiti's significance directly, the framing - the only case where independence was social revolution - is the thesis, and the two sections above supply the body.

### Traps peculiar to Haiti

- Dating independence to 1791 - an explicitly prohibited error.
- Treating the revolution as a two-sided conflict between enslaved people and French forces, which loses the free-coloured claim, the internal white division and the British and Spanish interventions.
- Omitting the attempted restoration of 1802, which is the step that makes the event a revolution rather than a revolt.
- Supplying casualty figures, population figures or production figures; none is verified here.
- Explaining Haiti's later difficulties by the radicalism of its revolution while omitting the isolation and the indemnity.
- Treating Haiti as an inspiration to mainland creole leaders rather than as a warning to them.
- Calling Haiti a footnote, which makes the central distinction of the topic unavailable.
- Stopping at the revolutionary abolition decree of the 1790s, which erases the attempted restoration of 1802 and with it the reason the event counts as a revolution.
- Implying that independence in 1804 brought recognition; the former metropolis recognised Haiti only in 1825, and only on payment.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Revolution begins **1791**; independence **1804**. Never conflate the two dates.
2. ✅ First major anti-slavery revolutionary break in the region; ✅ first free Black republic of the modern era; ✅ first state founded by former slaves.
3. ✅ Leaders recorded: Toussaint Louverture, Dessalines and others.
4. ✅ The colony was Saint-Domingue, the western third of Hispaniola; ✅ the island is today divided into Haiti and the Dominican Republic.
5. ⚠️ Four internal parties: wealthy white planters, poorer whites, free people of colour, the enslaved majority - with irreconcilable aims.
6. ✅ Sequence: free-coloured claim 1790-91; northern rising August 1791; Spanish and British intervention from 1793; French revolutionary abolition 1793-94; Toussaint dominant in the later 1790s; autonomous constitution 1801; French expedition 1802; Toussaint dies in captivity 1803; independence 1 January 1804 under Dessalines.
7. ⚠️ Independence became the demand only after reversal was attempted - the same hardening pattern as on the mainland.
8. ✅ French recognition 1825 in exchange for an indemnity of 150 million francs, reduced to 90 million in 1838.
9. ⚠️ Four things Haiti establishes: general emancipation could not be reversed by force; independence followed attempted reversal; social revolution produced a besieged militarised state; the international order penalised the outcome.
10. ✅ Control-case comparison: who made it, what was demanded, what happened to slavery, land, the ruling group and recognition.
11. ⚠️ Owners' line: Haiti shows what independence looked like when the enslaved rather than the creoles made it - and why creole-led movements took care that it would not.
12. ⚠️ Three prohibited moves: footnoting Haiti; dating independence to 1791; reading later poverty backwards as a verdict on radicalism.
13. ⚠️ No casualty, population or production figure is held for any phase of this war.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain precisely why Haiti is described as the only case in the region where independence *was* social revolution, and show how that description is used to analyse the mainland outcomes.

**Model answer:** It is described that way because in Haiti the same event that ended external rule also destroyed the internal social order, whereas everywhere else the two came apart. The revolution began in 1791 with a rising of the enslaved majority, was made by the people at the very bottom of the colonial hierarchy together with free people of colour, and produced independence in 1804 under Dessalines after a French expedition sent in 1802 failed to restore the earlier order. Slavery ended immediately and irreversibly; the plantation order was destroyed as a system of ownership; and the ruling group was not reformed but replaced. Britannica records the outcome as the first state founded by former slaves, and it was the first free Black republic of the modern era. On the mainland the sequence separated: creole elites who were wealthy but politically restricted led the movements, sovereignty was transferred to them, and slavery was ended piecemeal and later while landholding and racial hierarchy survived. The analytical use follows from isolating the variable. Haiti and the mainland faced comparable imperial situations; what differed was who made the revolution, and the social outcome tracked that difference exactly. That is why the mainland result cannot be explained by saying that social transformation was impossible in this period - it demonstrably was not - and must instead be explained by the composition of the coalitions that won. Haiti also shows the price: France recognised independence only in 1825 and only in return for an indemnity, so the international order penalised the one social revolution it saw.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding from Haiti's later difficulties that radical social revolution was self-defeating. The new state was founded under invasion, faced diplomatic isolation, and had to purchase recognition with an indemnity; attributing its subsequent condition to the radicalism of its revolution, rather than to the penalties imposed on it, assumes the conclusion the evidence is supposed to test.

*The next lesson returns to the mainland and to the event that opened everything. Everything so far has described conditions: a hierarchy, a reform programme, a vocabulary and a warning. None of them could produce a break on its own, because the empire's legitimacy was intact and no American elite was willing to attack it. In 1808 that legitimacy collapsed - not in America, but in Europe, and not by rebellion, but by a French army.*

## Lesson 6 - 1808: who holds sovereignty when the king has been removed?

Progress: 6/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Napoleonic invasion and the Bayonne abdications; the Spanish response and the juntas; the transfer of the Portuguese court; the constitutional question and the two answers to it; why 1810 rather than 1808; the hardening from autonomy to independence

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - Britannica on Napoleon's intervention in Spain, the formation of juntas, and the 1808 transfer of the Portuguese court
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage sits in Lesson 18
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the legitimacy chain, and where it broke

```text
   BEFORE 1808 - THE CHAIN NOBODY IN AMERICA WAS ATTACKING

   GOD -> THE KING OF SPAIN -> viceroys, audiencias, captains-general
                            -> municipal councils -> subjects

   Creole grievance ran AGAINST OFFICIALS, never against the king.
   The standard formula of protest: "long live the king, down with
   bad government." A grievance with no constitutional exit.

   ================= 1808: THE LINK IS CUT IN EUROPE =================

   French armies enter Spain -> the royal family is taken to Bayonne
   -> ABDICATIONS extracted -> a French king is placed on the throne
   -> THERE IS NO LEGITIMATE AUTHORITY IN MADRID TO OBEY

   ===================================================================
                              |
              THE QUESTION THAT NOW HAS TO BE ANSWERED:
              IN WHOSE NAME DOES ANYONE GOVERN?
                              |
        +---------------------+---------------------+
        |                                           |
   SPAIN'S ANSWER                            AMERICA'S ANSWER
   provincial JUNTAS form in the king's      if authority reverts to the
   name; then a central junta; then a        kingdoms, it reverts HERE TOO:
   regency; then a parliament at Cadiz       American cities form their own
   -> "we are the government; the            juntas in the king's name
      Americas are our possessions"          -> "we are kingdoms, not
                              |                 colonies; our juntas are
                              |                 as legitimate as yours"
        +---------------------+---------------------+
                              |
              THE COLLISION: TWO IDENTICAL ARGUMENTS,
              ONE CONCLUSION EACH SIDE REFUSES
                              |
   1810: American juntas multiply -> ✅ revolts begin
   Spain refuses equality -> autonomy claims HARDEN INTO INDEPENDENCE

   ===================== MEANWHILE, PORTUGAL =========================
   the court does NOT fall - it SAILS. Lisbon's monarchy relocates to
   Rio de Janeiro. Portuguese America therefore has NO legitimacy gap,
   NO junta crisis and NO war of independence. SAME TRIGGER,
   OPPOSITE MECHANISM, OPPOSITE OUTCOME (Lesson 14).
   ===================================================================
```

*The diagram carries the single most important structural insight in the topic. The Spanish American movements did not begin by claiming a right to leave; they began by claiming the same right that Spaniards were simultaneously exercising. That is why the early documents are royalist in form, why the wars are so bitter (both sides claim legitimacy), and why the Portuguese case - where the monarch was present rather than absent - produced no war at all.*

### 1. Authority that ran through a single person

Authority in this empire ran through the person of the king. Every official held his post by royal commission; every court gave judgment in the royal name; every protest that wished to remain lawful had to be directed against bad officials rather than against the crown. This is why two centuries of grievance produced revolts against specific taxes and specific officials and never a general political programme: there was nowhere for such a programme to go.

In 1808 a French army occupied Spain and the royal family was removed to French territory, where abdications were extracted and a French monarch installed. At that moment every official in the Spanish empire held a commission from a person who was no longer in power, in the name of a monarchy whose legitimate occupant was a prisoner.

Notice how narrow the question is at this stage, and how quickly it becomes enormous. Nobody at first is asking "should we be independent?" Everybody is asking "who may lawfully give orders?" - and both the Spanish and the American answers to that question turn out to be the same answer: in the absence of the king, authority reverts to the communities that originally conferred it, which may form emergency governing bodies. The Spanish provinces did exactly that. When American cities did exactly the same thing, they were told they could not, because they were possessions rather than kingdoms. The war grew out of that refusal.

### 2. The crisis stated in one line

✅ **Fact:** **Britannica links Latin American independence directly to the shock created by Napoleon's intervention in Spain**.

✅ **Fact:** **the colonies began to form juntas and challenge old authority after the Spanish monarchy was destabilized**.

✅ **Fact:** **San Martin's career also turns decisively after Napoleon's occupation of Spain in 1808**.

✅ **Fact:** the chronology records for **1808**: **Napoleon invades Spain; Portuguese court shifts to Brazil**, with the recorded significance that the **Iberian imperial crisis opens the door to colonial autonomy**.

✅ **Fact:** the trigger is recorded in one line: **Napoleon invades Spain, 1808; Portuguese court shifts to Brazil; juntas form and challenge old authority**, with the distinctive feature that **legitimacy collapsed at the centre before it was attacked in the colonies**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that last clause is the whole lesson in eight words and should be quoted-in-substance in any causes answer. It establishes the direction of causation. The colonies did not bring down the empire's legitimacy; the empire's legitimacy fell and the colonies then had to decide what to do about it.

### 3. From Bayonne to Riego: the metropolitan sequence

✅ **Fact:** the standard connecting sequence for the Spanish crisis is as follows.

| Stage | ✅ Date | What it did |
|---|---|---|
| French troops enter Spain under the cover of a joint operation against Portugal | 1807-08 | Converts an ally into an occupier |
| The royal family is summoned to **Bayonne**; abdications are extracted; Napoleon's brother **Joseph** is placed on the Spanish throne | 1808 | Destroys the legitimate chain of command throughout the empire |
| Popular rising in Spain against the French; provincial **juntas** form in the name of the captive **Ferdinand VII** | 1808 | Establishes the doctrine that authority reverts to the communities in the king's absence - the doctrine America will use |
| A **Junta Central** is formed to coordinate resistance, and invites American representation | 1808-09 | Concedes in principle that the American territories are part of the monarchy rather than possessions - but on unequal terms |
| The Junta Central collapses under French pressure; a **Regency** succeeds it | 1810 | Removes the last body with plausible imperial authority and precipitates the American juntas |
| **Juntas are formed in American capitals**; ✅ revolts begin in Spanish America | **1810** | The dated turn: the independence phase becomes mass and regional |
| A parliament - the **Cortes** - is convened at **Cadiz** | 1810 | The subject of Lesson 7 |
| **Ferdinand VII** is restored and repudiates the constitutional settlement | 1814 | Converts American autonomy claims into independence claims |
| A military revolt in Spain under **Riego** forces the restoration of the constitution | 1820 | Reopens everything; directly produces the Mexican outcome of 1821 |

✅ **Fact:** the parallel Portuguese sequence is short and decisive. Facing the same French advance, the Portuguese court left Lisbon under British naval escort in late **1807** and arrived at **Rio de Janeiro** in **1808**, whereupon Brazilian ports were opened to friendly nations and the seat of the monarchy was established in America. ✅ The owners record the same event as **the Portuguese court shifts to Brazil**.

### 4. The juntas: what they claimed, and why it was not yet independence

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the point at which most answers go wrong, by reading 1810 backwards from 1824. The American juntas of 1808-10 were not, in the main, independence movements. Their characteristic claim had four parts:

1. sovereignty has reverted, in the king's absence, to the communities of the monarchy;
2. the American territories are **kingdoms** of that monarchy, not possessions of Spain;
3. they may therefore form juntas exactly as Seville, Valencia or Oviedo did; and
4. they hold their authority **for Ferdinand VII** until he is restored.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three consequences follow, and each is examinable.

- **The claim was legally conservative and politically revolutionary at once.** It used a traditional doctrine to reach a conclusion that destroyed the colonial relationship, which is why the men making it could sincerely deny that they were rebels.
- **It split American society rather than uniting it.** Peninsular officials, and many Americans, held that the Regency and later the Cortes were the legitimate government and that local juntas were usurpation. ⚠️ This is the origin of royalist America: it was a constitutional position, not merely a Spanish garrison.
- **It was refused.** Spain's successive governments would concede representation but not equality, and would treat American juntas as sedition. ⚠️ The refusal is what converted a constitutional dispute into a war, and the war is what converted autonomy into independence.

⚠️ **Analytical inference, stated as the topic's central mechanism:** the movement to independence in Spanish America was **reactive and cumulative**. It ran: crisis -> autonomy claim -> refusal -> armed conflict -> hardening -> independence. Very few of the men who formed juntas in 1810 had declared for independence; most of those who survived to 1820 had. An answer that presents independence as the aim from the start cannot explain the documents, the oaths or the composition of the early governments.

### 5. Why 1810 and not 1808, and why the mechanism is general

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two-year gap is a good test of whether a candidate understands the mechanism. In 1808 there was still a plausible central authority in Spain claiming to act for the king, and American cities overwhelmingly recognised it. ✅ The collapse of that authority and its replacement by a Regency in **1810** removed the last body whose claim was stronger than a local junta's - and the American juntas followed immediately. ✅ The revolts are dated to **1810** for exactly this reason.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the same mechanism explains three later turning points, which is why it is worth stating generally.

| Metropolitan event | ✅ Date | American consequence |
|---|---|---|
| Regency replaces the Junta Central | 1810 | Juntas formed; revolts begin |
| Ferdinand VII restored; constitutional settlement repudiated | 1814 | Autonomy option closed; reconquest attempted; independence becomes the only remaining position |
| Riego's revolt restores the constitution | 1820 | Spanish forces in America are weakened and divided; in Mexico, conservative opinion switches to independence to escape a liberal metropolis (Lesson 9) |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the asymmetry in that table. Every entry is a *Spanish* event. That is not an argument that Americans lacked agency; it is the specific finding that the openings in this conflict were created in Europe and used in America, and it is the most defensible form of the "external trigger" claim.

### 6. The Portuguese contrast, previewed

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the same shock produced the opposite mechanism in Portuguese America, and the contrast is the cleanest natural experiment in the topic.

| Element | Spanish America | Portuguese America |
|---|---|---|
| What happened to the monarch | ✅ Removed; abdications extracted at Bayonne | ✅ Relocated to the colony |
| Legitimacy gap | Total | None - the sovereign was physically present |
| Constitutional crisis | Acute: in whose name does anyone govern? | Absent: in the king's name, from Rio |
| Institutional consequence | Juntas, refusal, war | Colony raised to the centre of the empire; ports opened |
| Path to independence | Fifteen years of war | A separation in 1822 led by the heir to the throne |
| Social outcome | Republics; caste abolished in law; slavery ended piecemeal | ✅ Monarchy retained; slavery persisted long after independence |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** hold this table for Lesson 14 and deploy it in any comparative answer. It shows that the trigger was common and the outcome was not, which forces the explanation onto the *mechanism* - the presence or absence of legitimate authority - rather than onto vague appeals to Iberian culture or colonial temperament.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This account makes Latin American independence an accident of European war. On this telling the decisive events are French troop movements, Spanish abdications and a Spanish military revolt in 1820, and the Americans merely respond. That is a Eurocentric explanation of an American process, and it cannot account for the fifteen years of American fighting, the American political creativity in constitution-making, or the fact that the wars were won by American armies commanded by American officers.

**Reply:** The objection is the most important one in the lesson and the reply must concede a great deal of it. It is correct that an explanation which stops at the trigger has explained the opening and nothing else. The disciplined position distinguishes **opportunity** from **outcome**. The opportunity was indeed created in Europe: ✅ legitimacy collapsed at the centre before it was attacked in the colonies, and that revolts begin in 1810 after the crisis rather than before it. But what was done with that opportunity was entirely American and is not derivable from the trigger at all. The choice to claim kingdom status rather than colonial subordination; the decision to fight on after 1814 when the autonomy option closed; the construction of armies, revenues and constitutions; the recruitment bargains struck with enslaved, Indigenous and mixed-descent populations; the decision in Mexico in 1821 to seek independence in order to *avoid* metropolitan liberalism; and the whole of the social outcome - none of that follows from Napoleon's decisions. Moreover the opportunity was equally available everywhere and was used very differently in each of the five paths, which by itself proves that the trigger does not determine the result. The correct sentence is that Europe supplied the opening and America supplied the war, the settlement and the society that followed.

**Residual:** What cannot be established here is the distribution of opinion inside American societies in 1808-10 - how many people in any city supported the junta, the Regency or neither. No electoral, petition or membership evidence is held. The session therefore describes the *positions* that were taken and the institutions that were created, and makes no claim about their popularity.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies the pivot sentence of any causes answer and the whole of any "why then" answer. The construction is: the grievances of Lessons 2 and 3 had been present for decades; ✅ in 1808 Napoleon's intervention destroyed the legitimacy of the imperial centre; ✅ juntas formed in the king's name and challenged old authority; ✅ Spain refused to concede equality; and the autonomy claim hardened into independence. Five clauses, each carrying a recorded fact, and the causal grading is visible without a single abstract noun. For a question on the nature of the movements, section 4's four-part junta claim is the evidence that early Spanish American independence was constitutionally conservative in form - which is one of the two facts that make the "transfer of power rather than revolution" reading defensible.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing that the colonies rebelled in 1808, when ✅ the revolts are dated 1810 and the two-year gap is itself evidence of the mechanism - or presenting the juntas as independence movements from the outset, which makes the oaths to Ferdinand VII inexplicable.
- Treating royalism as a Spanish imposition rather than as an American constitutional position held by Americans.
- Making the topic an accident of European war and thereby losing fifteen years of American decisions.
- Forgetting 1814 and 1820, which are the two later metropolitan events that determine Mexican and southern outcomes.
- Conflating the Spanish and Portuguese mechanisms, which destroys the best comparison in the topic.
- Supplying figures for troop numbers, populations or the composition of juntas; none is verified here.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Britannica links Latin American independence directly to the shock created by Napoleon's intervention in Spain. ✅ The colonies began to form juntas and challenge old authority after the Spanish monarchy was destabilised.
2. ✅ 1808: Napoleon invades Spain; the Portuguese court shifts to Brazil; the Iberian imperial crisis opens the door to colonial autonomy.
3. ✅ Owners' formulation: legitimacy collapsed at the centre before it was attacked in the colonies.
4. ✅ Bayonne abdications; Joseph placed on the Spanish throne; Spanish provincial juntas in the name of Ferdinand VII; Junta Central 1808-09; Regency 1810; Cortes at Cadiz 1810; restoration 1814; Riego's revolt 1820.
5. ✅ The Portuguese court left Lisbon under British escort in late 1807 and reached Rio de Janeiro in 1808; Brazilian ports were opened to friendly nations. ⚠️ The four-part junta claim: sovereignty reverts to the communities; the Americas are kingdoms not possessions; they may form juntas as Spanish provinces did; they hold authority for Ferdinand VII.
6. ⚠️ The claim was legally conservative and politically revolutionary at once - which is why its authors denied being rebels.
7. ⚠️ Royalist America originates as a constitutional position, not as a garrison.
8. ⚠️ Central mechanism: crisis -> autonomy claim -> refusal -> war -> hardening -> independence.
9. ✅ Why 1810 and not 1808: the Junta Central's collapse and the Regency removed the last authority with a stronger claim than a local junta's. ✅ 1814 closed the autonomy option; ✅ 1820 reopened the war on new terms and produced the Mexican reversal.
10. ⚠️ Spanish and Portuguese mechanisms compared: absence versus presence of the sovereign; war versus separation; republics versus monarchy.
11. ⚠️ The disciplined sentence: Europe supplied the opening; America supplied the war, the settlement and the society that followed.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Spanish American movements began by swearing loyalty to Ferdinand VII, and show how that starting point helps explain both the length of the wars and the eventual social outcome.

**Model answer:** They began that way because the crisis of 1808 was in the first instance a constitutional crisis rather than a national one. The removal of the royal family and the extraction of abdications at Bayonne destroyed the chain of authority through which every official in the empire held his commission, and the Spanish response was to form provincial juntas in the name of the captive king on the doctrine that in the absence of the monarch authority reverts to the communities that conferred it. American cities used the identical doctrine, claiming that their territories were kingdoms of the monarchy rather than possessions of Spain and that their juntas were therefore as legitimate as Seville's - and they governed in Ferdinand's name. That starting point explains the length of the wars in two ways. First, it meant that both sides claimed legitimacy: royalist America was a constitutional position held by large numbers of Americans, not a Spanish garrison, so the conflicts were civil wars fought within American society. Second, it meant that independence was reached by hardening rather than by declaration: Spain conceded representation but not equality, the restoration of 1814 repudiated the constitutional settlement and attempted reconquest, and only then did autonomy claims become independence claims. It explains the social outcome because a movement whose founding claim was that existing American elites should exercise the authority formerly exercised from Madrid had no reason to disturb anything below those elites. The demand was for the offices and the commerce, not for a new social order, which is why sovereignty was transferred while landholding, caste practice and slavery largely survived.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading 1810 backwards from 1824 and assuming that everyone who formed a junta intended a republic. Most did not; the republican and independent positions were arrived at through a decade of refusal and war, and an answer that assumes them at the start cannot explain the oaths, the early institutions or the existence of a large American royalist party.

*The next lesson examines the road not taken. Between 1810 and 1814 Spain attempted something genuinely remarkable: a written constitution for the whole monarchy, with American deputies sitting in a single parliament and colonial status formally abolished. If it had worked, this topic would not exist. Understanding exactly why it failed - and it was refused from both directions - is the best available test of whether independence was inevitable.*

## Lesson 7 - Cadiz: the liberal empire that was offered, and refused from both directions

Progress: 7/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Cortes of Cadiz and the Constitution of 1812; American deputies and the representation question; the exclusion of castas of African descent; what was actually conceded and what was withheld; the restoration of 1814; the Riego revolt of 1820; Cadiz as the test of inevitability

CA search: not sought - no current anchor belongs on the Cadiz settlement
