### Visual: the offer, the arithmetic and the refusal

```text
   CADIZ 1810-1814: A PARLIAMENT FOR THE WHOLE MONARCHY,
   MEETING IN THE ONE CITY THE FRENCH COULD NOT TAKE

   WHAT WAS OFFERED TO AMERICA               WHAT WAS WITHHELD
   ---------------------------               -----------------
   * the Americas declared an                * EQUAL representation:
     integral part of the                      American deputies were
     Spanish nation, not colonies              seated in numbers far
   * American deputies seated in               below the American share
     the Cortes                                of the monarchy's people
   * a written constitution (1812)           * FREE TRADE with foreign
     with national sovereignty,                nations - refused, because
     a single legislature, limited             peninsular commerce
     monarchy, rights and an end               depended on the monopoly
     to seigneurial jurisdiction             * CITIZENSHIP for castas of
   * legal abolition of Indigenous             African descent - withheld,
     tribute and of the labour                 which was ALSO the device
     draft                                     that kept American numbers
   * press freedom, equality before            down in the representation
     the law for Spaniards of both             calculation
     hemispheres

   THE ARITHMETIC TRAP
   if people of African descent count as citizens -> America outnumbers Spain
   if they do not                                -> Spain keeps the majority
   => the RACIAL exclusion and the REPRESENTATION question were ONE question

   ================== AND THEN IT WAS ALL CANCELLED ==================
   1814: Ferdinand VII returns, repudiates the constitution,
   dissolves the Cortes, and orders reconquest
   -> THE AUTONOMY OPTION IS CLOSED. INDEPENDENCE IS WHAT IS LEFT.
   1820: Riego's revolt forces the constitution back
   -> reopens everything, and pushes MEXICAN CONSERVATIVES into
      independence to ESCAPE a liberal Spain (Lesson 9)
   ===================================================================
```

*The diagram shows why Cadiz is the best available test of whether independence was inevitable. A genuine constitutional alternative existed, was legislated, and failed - not because nobody proposed it, but because conceding it fully would have meant a monarchy in which America outvoted Spain, and no peninsular majority was prepared to legislate itself into a minority.*

### 1. A parliament in the last corner of a besieged country

By 1810 the Spanish resistance had been pushed back to a single defensible corner of the peninsula, and the men governing it had to answer a hard question. They were fighting a French occupation in the name of liberty and of a captive king. They needed American money, American loyalty and American soldiers. And America had just begun forming its own juntas on precisely the argument the Spanish juntas had used.

So they did something genuinely radical: they summoned a parliament for the entire monarchy, seated American deputies in it, declared that the American territories were not colonies but an integral part of a single nation, and wrote a constitution. This was not a cynical manoeuvre by everyone involved; many of the men at Cadiz meant it.

But two things they could not concede. They could not concede trade with foreign nations, because the peninsular commercial interest was one of the pillars of the regime they were defending. And they could not concede equal representation, because on any honest count of the monarchy's inhabitants the American territories were more populous than Spain - which meant that a genuinely equal parliament would be an American parliament. So they conceded the principle and engineered the arithmetic, and the American deputies understood exactly what had been done.

### 2. The institution, named exactly

✅ **Fact:** the parliament summoned in 1810 met at **Cadiz** and is standardly called the **Cortes of Cadiz**. It promulgated the **Constitution of 1812**, whose principal provisions were national sovereignty, a limited hereditary monarchy, a single elected legislature, the abolition of seigneurial jurisdiction, freedom of the press, and the declaration that the Spanish nation comprised **Spaniards of both hemispheres**. ✅ American deputies sat in it. ✅ The Cortes legislated the abolition of **Indigenous tribute** and of compulsory labour drafts.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features of this list deserve emphasis because they are routinely missed.

- **It formally abolished colonial status.** The phrase "of both hemispheres" is not decorative; it is a constitutional claim that there were no colonies, only provinces of one nation. That is a more radical position than anything Britain offered its American colonies before 1776, and it is why the failure of Cadiz is so analytically important.
- **It abolished tribute.** ✅ The head tax on Indigenous adults - one of the pillars of the colonial fiscal order described in Lesson 2 - was legislated away by the metropolis, not by the republics. ⚠️ Several independent republics subsequently reintroduced it or an equivalent, which is one of the sharpest single pieces of evidence in the whole topic that the social content of independence was not determined by the vocabulary of liberty.
- **It was written under siege.** The parliament's radicalism is partly a function of its isolation: the men sitting in Cadiz were a self-selected wartime liberal minority, and the constitution they produced had very little support among the institutions - crown, church hierarchy, army command - that would have had to implement it.

### 3. The representation question, and the racial exclusion inside it

✅ **Fact:** the Cortes refused to grant American territories representation proportionate to their inhabitants. The device by which the peninsular majority was preserved was the **exclusion from citizenship of those of African descent**, who were denied the vote and excluded from the population base on which representation was calculated, while Indigenous inhabitants were counted.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the single most instructive episode in the entire constitutional history of the crisis, and it should be in any answer that discusses whether reform could have prevented independence. Observe what it proves.

1. **The representation question and the race question were the same question.** A liberal assembly that had just declared equality before the law and the unity of the nation manufactured a racial exclusion for the arithmetical purpose of retaining control. That is not hypocrisy in the loose sense; it is a demonstration that the constitutional settlement had a purpose - the preservation of peninsular primacy - which overrode its own stated principles when the two collided.
2. **It gave American autonomists their strongest possible argument.** After 1812 they could say, with documentary support, that even Spanish liberals would not treat Americans as equals, and that autonomy within the monarchy was therefore not available on honourable terms.
3. **It complicated American radicalism too.** ⚠️ American deputies who demanded that people of African descent be counted were arguing for a racial inclusion that many of their own constituents opposed; and when the mainland republics later abolished caste categories in law, they did so partly because the alternative had been used against them at Cadiz. The interaction of principle and arithmetic runs in both directions.

⚠️ **Sensitive-evidence discipline:** no population figure for any territory or group is given here, and none is verified here. The claim asserted is the *structure* of the exclusion and its purpose, which is documentary, not any demographic quantity.

### 4. What Cadiz conceded, what it refused, and why each mattered

| Demand | Cadiz response | ⚠️ Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| End of colonial status | ✅ Conceded in principle: provinces of one nation | Removed the vocabulary of colonialism without removing the substance; sharpened rather than satisfied the grievance |
| Seats in the national parliament | ✅ Conceded | Created a forum in which the inequality could be documented and publicised |
| Proportionate representation | ✅ Refused, via the exclusion of those of African descent from the citizen base | The decisive refusal; converted reformers into autonomists |
| Access to office | Partially addressed by formal equality of Spaniards of both hemispheres | Not enforced in practice while the war continued |
| Free trade with foreign nations | ✅ Refused | Left the central economic grievance of the creole elite untouched, and pushed it toward Britain |
| Abolition of Indigenous tribute and labour draft | ✅ Conceded | Genuinely radical; later reversed in several republics |
| Autonomy for American juntas | ✅ Refused; juntas treated as sedition | Guaranteed that the constitutional dispute would be settled by war |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read the table's shape rather than its rows. Everything that cost Spain nothing was conceded; everything that would have transferred power or revenue was refused. That is not a moral observation - it is the normal behaviour of a metropolitan legislature - but it is the evidential basis for the claim that reform failed structurally rather than accidentally.

### 5. 1814 and 1820: the two reversals that decided the war

✅ **Fact:** **Ferdinand VII** was restored in **1814**, immediately annulled the Constitution of 1812, dissolved the Cortes, restored the earlier order and dispatched forces to reconquer the American territories.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** 1814 is the hinge of the whole political sequence and deserves a sentence of its own in any answer about the nature of the movements. Up to 1814 a large part of American opinion was autonomist rather than separatist: it wanted self-government inside the monarchy. The annulment destroyed that position, because it demonstrated that even a constitution signed by a Spanish parliament could be cancelled by a single royal decree. After 1814 there was no available guarantee short of sovereignty - and it is from that point, not from 1810, that the movements become unambiguously independence movements.

✅ **Fact:** in **1820** a military revolt in Spain led by **Riego**, among forces assembled for service in America, compelled Ferdinand VII to restore the Constitution of 1812.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 1820 revolt has three effects that the rest of this session depends on.

- It diverted and demoralised the forces intended for reconquest, at the moment when Bolivar and San Martin were completing their campaigns.
- It produced negotiations and armistices in several theatres, which the patriot side used to consolidate.
- Most strikingly, it **reversed the political valence of independence in Mexico**: conservative, clerical and landholding interests that had fought the popular insurgency for a decade now preferred separation from a liberal Spain to obedience to it. Lesson 9 traces that inversion, which is the sharpest reversal in the region and is unintelligible without the 1820 revolt.

### 6. Cadiz as the test of inevitability

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the section to use whenever a question asks whether independence was inevitable, or whether Spain could have retained its empire.

| Position | Case for | Case against |
|---|---|---|
| **Independence was inevitable** | The refusals at Cadiz followed from the structure: a monarchy in which America was more populous could not concede equal representation and remain Spanish-controlled; and the commercial concession would have destroyed the peninsular interest that sustained the regime | This treats a set of political decisions as if they were laws of nature; different decisions were argued for at Cadiz and were defeated by majorities, not by necessity |
| **A liberal empire was possible** | Cadiz shows the constitutional materials existed: colonial status abolished, deputies seated, tribute abolished, rights declared; Brazil shows that a transatlantic monarchy could hold together when the centre moved | The Brazilian analogy fails, because the Portuguese monarch was physically present in America; no Spanish equivalent was even attempted |
| ⚠️ **The disciplined position** | Independence was not inevitable in 1810 and was close to inevitable after 1814. The variable is not sentiment but guarantee: once a constitutional settlement had been cancelled by decree, no arrangement short of sovereignty could bind a future Spanish government | This still leaves open whether an earlier, more generous settlement could have succeeded; that question cannot be answered from the record available here |

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This lesson gives a failed Spanish parliament a whole lesson while the campaigns that actually produced independence are compressed. Cadiz changed nothing: its constitution operated for barely two years, was cancelled by a royal decree, and was restored only when a mutiny forced it. Giving it this weight mistakes constitutional documents for historical causes.

**Reply:** The objection would be decisive if the claim were that Cadiz shaped events directly, and it is not. Three defences hold. First, Cadiz is the **counterfactual test**: it is the only occasion on which the alternative to independence was actually constructed and put to the vote, so the reason it failed is the best evidence available about whether independence could have been avoided. An answer that asserts independence was inevitable without examining the one serious attempt at an alternative has asserted rather than argued. Second, its refusals are the **documentary origin of the hardening**: ✅ the recorded American demands - political access and commercial freedom - are precisely what Cadiz declined, and the racial-arithmetic device by which representation was withheld is the strongest single piece of evidence that the refusal was structural. Third, its two reversals are **directly causal**: 1814 closed the autonomy option and converted autonomists into separatists, and 1820 diverted the reconquest and produced the Mexican inversion of 1821. Without the second of those, the Mexican path - the sharpest social reversal in the region - is inexplicable.

**Residual:** Nothing held here settles how far the Constitution of 1812 was actually applied in American territories under royalist control, how the elections it prescribed worked in practice, or how much support it commanded among American populations. Those are precisely the questions that would decide whether a liberal empire was ever a live possibility, and the session records the gap rather than filling it.

### UPSC application

Two uses, and one boundary. The first use is in any answer arguing about the nature of the movements: Cadiz is where you prove that the early demand was for equality inside the monarchy rather than for separation, and 1814 is where you date the change. The second is in any answer on inevitability or on whether reform could have preserved the empire; the three-position table of section 6 is a complete answer structure in itself, and the disciplined position - not inevitable in 1810, close to inevitable after 1814 - is the kind of dated, qualified judgement that scores. The boundary is that the Peninsular War, Spanish liberalism as a movement and the European settlement that followed belong to other owners; use the American consequences and hand the European narrative over.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Omitting Cadiz entirely, which leaves the answer unable to say why autonomy failed and forces it to assert inevitability.
- Presenting Cadiz as a purely cynical manoeuvre, which cannot explain the abolition of tribute or the declaration of Spaniards of both hemispheres.
- Missing the link between the racial exclusion and the representation arithmetic, which is the strongest evidence in the lesson.
- Dating the shift to independence at 1810 rather than 1814.
- Forgetting the 1820 revolt, and therefore being unable to explain Mexico in 1821.
- Supplying population or deputy figures; none is verified here.
- Treating the Constitution of 1812 as effective in America, which is not established here.
- Reading the Constitution of 1812 as a purely Spanish domestic document, which loses its explicit claim to cover Spaniards of both hemispheres.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Cortes of Cadiz summoned 1810; **Constitution of 1812**; American deputies seated.
2. ✅ Provisions: national sovereignty; limited hereditary monarchy; single legislature; press freedom; abolition of seigneurial jurisdiction; the nation comprises **Spaniards of both hemispheres**.
3. ✅ Indigenous tribute and compulsory labour drafts abolished by the Cortes - and later reintroduced in several republics.
4. ✅ Proportionate representation refused; those of African descent excluded from citizenship and from the population base used to calculate representation.
5. ⚠️ The representation question and the race question were one question; the exclusion preserved a peninsular majority.
6. ⚠️ Pattern of the settlement: everything costless conceded; everything that transferred power or revenue refused.
7. ✅ Free trade with foreign nations refused.
8. ✅ **1814**: Ferdinand VII restored; constitution annulled; Cortes dissolved; reconquest ordered.
9. ⚠️ 1814 is the hinge: after a constitution has been cancelled by decree, nothing short of sovereignty is a guarantee.
10. ✅ **1820**: Riego's revolt among troops assembled for America forces restoration of the constitution.
11. ⚠️ Three effects of 1820: reconquest forces diverted; armistices exploited by patriots; Mexican conservatives switched to independence to escape a liberal Spain.
12. ⚠️ Inevitability verdict: not inevitable in 1810; close to inevitable after 1814; the variable is guarantee, not sentiment.
13. ⚠️ Cadiz abolished colonial status in law - a more radical formal position than anything offered before 1776 elsewhere - and still failed.
14. ⚠️ No population, deputy or vote figure is held here.

### Concept check

**Question:** "Spain was never willing to make the concessions that would have preserved its American empire." Test this claim against the record of the Cortes of Cadiz.

**Model answer:** The claim is too absolute and should be refined rather than accepted or rejected. Spain conceded a great deal at Cadiz and did so in writing. The Cortes summoned in 1810 seated American deputies, declared the Spanish nation to comprise Spaniards of both hemispheres and thereby abolished colonial status in principle, promulgated the Constitution of 1812 with national sovereignty, a limited monarchy, a single legislature, press freedom and equality before the law, and legislated away Indigenous tribute and compulsory labour drafts - measures more radical than several of the independent republics later sustained. So the claim fails as a statement about willingness in general. What it identifies correctly is the pattern of the refusals. Everything that cost nothing was granted, and the two concessions that would have transferred power or revenue were withheld: trade with foreign nations, because peninsular commerce depended on the monopoly, and proportionate representation, because on any honest count America was the more populous part of the monarchy. The device used to withhold the second was to exclude those of African descent from citizenship and from the population base used to calculate seats, which shows that the representation question and the race question were a single question and that the refusal was structural rather than incidental. The disciplined verdict is therefore that Spain conceded the vocabulary of equality and refused its arithmetic - and that when the restoration of 1814 annulled even the vocabulary by decree, it proved that no Spanish concession could bind a future Spanish government, which is the point at which autonomy ceased to be a rational objective.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Cadiz as irrelevant because it was short-lived. Its importance is not what it achieved but what it tested: it is the only case in which the alternative to independence was actually constructed, so the reasons it failed are the best available evidence on whether independence was avoidable.

*The next lesson turns to the first of the mainland theatres, and to the one that shows most starkly what the creole elite had been afraid of since the 1780s. In Mexico the rising began not among the great landowners but in a parish, and within weeks it had become a mass movement of Indigenous and mixed-descent people attacking property as well as authority. What happened to that movement, and what its suppression did to Mexican politics, explains why Mexican independence eleven years later looked nothing like its beginning.*

```text
             |                               the peninsular officials
             |                                        |
   BLOCKED IN 1808 by a pre-emptive          SEPTEMBER 1810: the GRITO
   strike: peninsular merchants and          DE DOLORES. Indigenous and
   officials deposed the viceroy who         mixed-descent people join in
   had flirted with autonomy                 very large numbers
             |                                        |
             +-------------------+--------------------+
                                 |
        THE CRUCIAL DIVERGENCE: THE CROWD ATTACKS PROPERTY,
        NOT ONLY OFFICE. Estates are seized; the mining city of
        Guanajuato is stormed; peninsular civilians are killed.
                                 |
        +------------------------+------------------------+
        |                                                 |
   CREOLE ELITES RECOIL                          THE INSURGENCY RADICALISES
   the group that leads independence              under MORELOS it acquires a
   everywhere else here joins the                 PROGRAMME: independence,
   ROYALIST side to defend property               abolition of slavery and of
        |                                         caste, end of tribute,
        |                                         a congress, a constitution
        |                                                 |
        +------------------------+------------------------+
                                 |
   RESULT BY 1815-16: the insurgency is broken militarily,
   Hidalgo and Morelos are executed, and Mexico is the ONE
   major theatre where the creole elite is fighting FOR the king.
   THE REVERSAL OF 1821 (Lesson 9) BEGINS FROM THIS POSITION.
```

*The diagram explains the apparent paradox of Mexican independence: it is the theatre where the popular rising was largest and earliest, and the theatre whose independence settlement was the most conservative. Those two facts are not in tension; the second is a consequence of the first. A creole class that has watched its estates burned does not lead a social revolution eleven years later - it negotiates one that guarantees its property.*

### 1. New Spain in 1810, and the route closed first

New Spain in 1810 was the wealthiest territory of the Spanish empire, with the largest silver production, a very large Indigenous population living in communities that owed tribute and labour, an extensive mixed-descent population working in mines, estates and towns, and a creole elite that was both prosperous and, as everywhere, excluded from the top offices.

When the imperial crisis arrived, the creole autonomist option was closed early and violently: peninsular interests in the capital pre-empted a move toward a local junta by removing the viceroy who had entertained it. That mattered enormously. It meant that in New Spain the constitutional, city-council route that elites took everywhere else was blocked at the start, and that anyone who wanted change had to look outside the institutions.

What followed is the event this lesson is about. A rising began in a provincial parish, spread with extraordinary speed among Indigenous and mixed-descent populations, and immediately became something broader than a constitutional dispute - a movement against the people who owned the land and the mines as well as against the officials who governed. And at that point the creole elite made the choice that determined Mexican history for a decade: it sided with the crown.

### 2. The Mexican sequence as the record fixes it

✅ **Fact:** **Hidalgo** issued the **Grito de Dolores** in **1810**.

✅ **Fact:** the Mexican path is recorded as a sequence: **Hidalgo, then Morelos, then Iturbide** - **a longer sequence from popular revolt to conservative settlement**.

✅ **Fact:** the path runs **Hidalgo's Grito de Dolores, 1810 -> Morelos -> Iturbide**, with **independence 1821**, and gives its distinctive feature as: it **began as a popular/indigenous rising and ended in a conservative settlement led by a royalist officer - the sharpest reversal in the region**.

✅ **Fact:** **Indigenous peoples** were **mobilised in Mexico's popular phase under Hidalgo**, and that afterwards **land pressure often continued or worsened**.

✅ **Fact:** the chronology records **1810** as the year in which **revolts begin in Spanish America** and the **independence phase turns mass and regional**.

### 3. The rising of 1810, from Dolores to Guanajuato

✅ **Fact:** **Miguel Hidalgo y Costilla** was a parish priest at the town of **Dolores**. On **16 September 1810** he issued the call known as the **Grito de Dolores**, which invoked religion, denounced the peninsular authorities and proclaimed loyalty to **Ferdinand VII**. The rising drew very large numbers of Indigenous and mixed-descent participants, took the mining city of **Guanajuato**, and advanced toward the capital before turning away. Hidalgo was defeated, captured and **executed in 1811**.

✅ **Fact:** Hidalgo issued decrees during the rising **abolishing slavery** and **abolishing Indigenous tribute**, and ordered the restitution of land to Indigenous communities.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features of this opening phase carry the analytical weight.

1. **The form was traditional and the content was not.** The call was made in the name of religion and of the captive king, which is the standard legitimist formula of the period. What made it revolutionary was who answered it and what they did: the participants were overwhelmingly the people at the base of the colonial order, and their targets included property as well as office.
2. **The decrees were the most radical social programme issued anywhere on the mainland at that date.** Abolition of slavery, abolition of tribute and restitution of community land together address every grievance identified in Lesson 2 except the commercial one - which is precisely the grievance that belonged to the creoles.
3. **The violence at Guanajuato was decisive politically.** ⚠️ The storming of the city and the killing of peninsular civilians sheltering there is the moment at which creole opinion consolidated against the insurgency. ⚠️ **Evidentiary discipline:** no casualty figure for Guanajuato or for any other episode in this war is verified here, and none is given. What is asserted is the event and its recorded political effect.

### 4. Morelos: the insurgency acquires a programme

✅ **Fact:** after Hidalgo's execution the insurgency was led principally by **Jose Maria Morelos**, also a priest, who reorganised it into more disciplined and mobile forces operating mainly in the south. He convened a **congress at Chilpancingo in 1813**, which declared independence from Spain, and a constitution was promulgated at **Apatzingan in 1814**. Morelos was captured and **executed in 1815**. The insurgency thereafter survived only as scattered guerrilla resistance, associated above all with **Vicente Guerrero** in the south.

✅ **Fact:** the programme associated with Morelos - set out in the document conventionally known as the *Sentiments of the Nation* - included independence, popular sovereignty, the abolition of slavery, the abolition of the caste distinctions, the abolition of tribute, and a moderate but real redistributive intent through limits on great estates and taxation of luxury.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most important single fact about the Mexican first phase, and it should be in any answer that discusses whether social revolution was possible in the region. **The Mexican insurgency is the only mainland movement that combined independence with an explicit programme of social transformation and put it into constitutional form.** It therefore functions as a second control case alongside Haiti - and unlike Haiti, it lost.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** why it lost is the examinable part, and four reasons can be stated without speculation.

| Reason | Content |
|---|---|
| **Elite opposition** | The programme's social content guaranteed that creole landowners, mine owners and the church hierarchy would fund and officer the other side |
| **Resource asymmetry** | The royalist side commanded the viceregal treasury, the silver revenue, the regular army and the militia structure created by the Bourbon military reforms |
| **Geographical confinement** | The insurgency operated mainly in the south and centre-south and never took the capital, the treasury or the principal ports |
| **No external support** | ⚠️ Unlike the northern and southern theatres, which drew on foreign volunteers, arms purchases, external bases and eventually British commercial interest, the Mexican insurgency had no comparable outside supply |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice that the first reason is the decisive one and that it is structural rather than accidental. A movement that promises to abolish slavery, caste and tribute and to limit great estates has made an enemy of every group capable of sustaining a war in New Spain. That is the price of a genuine social programme in a society organised as Lesson 2 described - and it is the clearest available demonstration of why the movements that succeeded were the ones that did not make such promises.

### 5. What survived the defeat

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the first phase is usually described as a failure, and militarily it was. But three things survived it, and all three shape what follows.

1. **A surviving guerrilla presence in the south**, which meant that the royalist command still needed field operations in 1820 - and therefore still needed a commander with troops, which is the opening Iturbide used.
2. **A constitutional and programmatic legacy**: an independence declaration, a written charter, and a documented social programme that later Mexican politics repeatedly returned to. ⚠️ The insurgent leaders became, retrospectively, the founding figures of the Mexican republic, which is why the conservative settlement of 1821 had to accommodate their memory even while reversing their programme.
3. **A political lesson learned by the creole elite**, which is the one that mattered most in the short run: that independence obtained through mass mobilisation was a mortal threat to property, and that if independence was to be had at all it must be obtained by arrangement among elites and by the existing army.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that third point is the bridge to the next lesson, and it is also the general proposition this topic exists to establish. In Mexico the two things were tried in sequence rather than in different places: social revolution with independence, which failed, and then independence without social revolution, which succeeded within months. The comparison is internal to one country, which makes it unusually clean evidence.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Treating Hidalgo's rising as a social revolution over-reads it. Its stated aims were religious and legitimist - it marched under a religious banner and proclaimed loyalty to Ferdinand VII - and its mass following was driven by immediate grievances over tribute, food prices and local officials rather than by any programme. The radical decrees were issued in the course of a campaign that was already collapsing, and reading them as the movement's purpose imposes a coherence that was not there.

**Reply:** The objection is a useful corrective against romanticising the rising, and part of it should be conceded: the participants' motives were local and material, the movement's early form was legitimist, and it was not led by a social programme in its first weeks. But three points hold the analysis. First, the classification does not depend on stated aims; it depends on what was done and to whom. A movement whose participants seized estates and attacked the property of the dominant class was acting against the social order whatever banner it carried. Second, the decrees are not incidental: ✅ abolition of slavery, abolition of tribute and restitution of community land address precisely the grievances of the groups that had joined, which indicates responsiveness rather than accident. Third, and decisively, the programme was made explicit, deliberate and constitutional under Morelos, with an independence declaration at Chilpancingo in 1813 and a charter in 1814 - so if the first phase was inchoate, the second was not. The recorded description settles the classification: Mexico **began as a popular and Indigenous rising**. What the objection correctly warns against is the further claim that the rising was a coordinated social revolution from the start, and this session does not make that claim.

**Residual:** Nothing is established here on the internal composition of the insurgent forces, the extent of Indigenous community participation by region, or the degree to which the Morelos programme was implemented in areas under insurgent control. The session therefore reports the decrees, the congress and the charter as documents, and records that their practical application cannot be assessed from what is held here.

### UPSC application

Mexico's first phase is the strongest single piece of evidence available for the argument that the social limits of Latin American independence were chosen rather than imposed by circumstance. The construction, in three sentences: in 1810 a mass Indigenous and mixed-descent rising under Hidalgo issued decrees abolishing slavery and tribute and restoring community land; under Morelos it produced an independence declaration in 1813 and a constitution in 1814 with a programme of caste abolition and limits on great estates; and it was defeated by a coalition in which the creole elite fought on the royalist side. Deployed inside an answer about why independence did not produce social transformation, that sequence is worth more than any amount of general reflection on elite self-interest, because it shows the alternative being proposed, supported and beaten.

### The errors this lesson exists to prevent

- Describing Hidalgo's rising as a creole independence movement, when it is the one major case where creoles opposed the rising - or treating the insurgents' legitimist language as proof that the rising was not socially radical.
- Omitting Morelos, which removes the programme, the congress of 1813 and the charter of 1814, and leaves the phase looking like a jacquerie.
- Dating Mexican independence to 1810; ✅ 1810 is the Grito, ✅ 1821 is independence.
- Supplying casualty figures for Guanajuato or any other episode; none is verified here.
- Presenting the defeat as inevitable rather than as the outcome of a specific coalition with specific resources, or assuming that the Morelos programme was implemented in insurgent-held areas, which is not established.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Hidalgo** issued the **Grito de Dolores** in **1810**; ✅ the sequence is Hidalgo -> Morelos -> Iturbide; ✅ independence 1821.
2. ✅ Owners' description: began as a popular/Indigenous rising, ended in a conservative settlement led by a royalist officer - the sharpest reversal in the region.
3. ✅ Hidalgo was a parish priest at Dolores; the call was issued on 16 September 1810 invoking religion and loyalty to Ferdinand VII. ✅ Guanajuato was stormed; Hidalgo was defeated, captured and executed in 1811.
4. ✅ Hidalgo's decrees abolished slavery and tribute and ordered restitution of community land.
5. ⚠️ Traditional form, revolutionary content: the participants were the base of the colonial order and the targets included property.
6. ✅ **Morelos** reorganised the insurgency; **congress of Chilpancingo, 1813** declared independence; **charter of Apatzingan, 1814**; Morelos executed **1815**; guerrilla resistance continued under **Guerrero**.
7. ✅ The Morelos programme: independence, popular sovereignty, abolition of slavery, abolition of caste, abolition of tribute, limits on great estates.
8. ⚠️ The Mexican insurgency is the only mainland movement to combine independence with an explicit constitutional programme of social transformation - a second control case beside Haiti, which lost.
9. ⚠️ Four reasons it lost: elite opposition produced by its own programme; royalist command of treasury, silver and regular forces; geographical confinement; no external supply.
10. ⚠️ Three survivals: southern guerrilla presence; constitutional and programmatic legacy; the creole lesson that independence must not come through mass mobilisation.
11. ⚠️ Internal comparison: Mexico tried social revolution with independence, and then independence without social revolution, within one decade and one country.
12. ✅ Indigenous peoples were mobilised under Hidalgo, and land pressure often continued or worsened afterwards. ⚠️ No casualty, participation or composition figure is held for this war.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the creole elite of New Spain fought on the royalist side between 1810 and 1815, and state what that fact contributes to the general argument of this topic.

**Model answer:** It fought on the royalist side because the rising that began in 1810 threatened it more directly than the imperial government did. The creole grievance in New Spain was the standard one - wealth without access to the highest office, under a commercial system that taxed and routed its exports - and the constitutional route to redress had been closed early when peninsular interests in the capital pre-empted a move toward a local junta. But when Hidalgo's call of September 1810 raised the countryside, the movement that resulted was not a constitutional one. Its participants were overwhelmingly Indigenous and mixed-descent people whose grievances were tribute, labour, land and the conduct of local officials; its targets included estates and the mining city of Guanajuato; and its decrees abolished slavery and tribute and ordered the restitution of community land. Under Morelos this became an explicit programme, declared at Chilpancingo in 1813 and constitutionalised at Apatzingan in 1814, including caste abolition and limits on great estates. Every element of that programme took something from the creole elite. It therefore funded and officered the royalist side, and the insurgency was broken by 1815-16. The contribution to the general argument is that it isolates the variable. Elsewhere one can argue that creole leaders simply did not think of social transformation; in Mexico it was proposed, written into a constitution and fought for, and the creole elite chose the crown over it. That establishes that the social limits of independence in this region were a choice made by the group with the means to fight, not an absence of alternatives.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because the insurgents used religious imagery and swore loyalty to the captive king, the rising was socially conservative. Legitimist language was the normal idiom of protest in this empire; the social character of a movement is established by who joins it, what it attacks and what it decrees, and on all three counts this was the most socially radical movement on the mainland.

*The next lesson completes the Mexican sequence and delivers the reversal. In 1820 a liberal revolt in Spain restored a constitution that Mexican conservatives regarded as a threat to the church, to property and to the established order. Within eighteen months the royalist officer who had spent a decade hunting the insurgents had declared independence, allied with the last insurgent commander still in the field, and had himself crowned emperor.*

## Lesson 9 - Mexico, second phase: Iturbide, and independence achieved as a conservative settlement

Progress: 9/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: how the 1820 revolt in Spain inverted Mexican politics; the Plan of Iguala and its three guarantees; the alliance of royalist commander and insurgent survivor; the Treaty of Cordoba and independence in 1821; the empire of 1822 and the republic of 1824; Central America; what the reversal proves

### Visual: the inversion, drawn as a switch of sides

```text
   POSITION IN 1815                         POSITION IN 1821
   ----------------                         ----------------
   CROWN + CREOLE ELITE + CHURCH            CREOLE ELITE + CHURCH +
   + ROYALIST ARMY                          ROYALIST ARMY + SURVIVING
        vs                                  INSURGENTS
   INSURGENTS (popular, Indigenous,              vs
   mixed-descent, socially radical)         A LIBERAL SPANISH GOVERNMENT

   WHAT MOVED? NOT THE PEOPLE. THE METROPOLIS.

   1820: Riego's revolt forces Ferdinand VII to restore the
   Constitution of 1812. Spain is now governed by liberals who
   legislate against clerical privilege and corporate property.
                          |
                          v
   THE CALCULATION IN MEXICO CITY FLIPS
   for a decade, loyalty to Spain PROTECTED the established order
   after 1820, loyalty to Spain THREATENS it
   => independence becomes the CONSERVATIVE option
                          |
                          v
   THE PLAN OF IGUALA, 24 FEBRUARY 1821 - THREE GUARANTEES
   +----------------+----------------+----------------------------+
   | RELIGION       | INDEPENDENCE   | UNION                      |
   | Roman          | a sovereign    | peninsulares and Americans |
   | Catholicism    | Mexican        | equal; caste distinctions  |
   | as the sole    | monarchy,      | abolished; NO land reform, |
   | established    | crown offered  | NO attack on property,     |
   | religion,      | to a Bourbon   | NO purge of officeholders  |
   | privileges     | prince         |                            |
   | protected      |                |                            |
   +----------------+----------------+----------------------------+
   ENFORCED BY: the Army of the Three Guarantees - the royalist
   commander ITURBIDE joined by the insurgent survivor GUERRERO
                          |
   TREATY OF CORDOBA, AUGUST 1821 -> ✅ INDEPENDENCE 1821
   1822 Iturbide crowned EMPEROR -> 1823 deposed -> 1824 REPUBLIC
   Central America separates; briefly annexed; then separate again
```

*The diagram makes the reversal intelligible rather than merely surprising. Nobody in Mexico changed their view about society. What changed was which government threatened the established order - and when the metropolis became more radical than the colony, the colonial elite discovered a national vocation. This is the clearest case in the topic of independence used as an instrument of social conservation.*

### 1. A pacified colony, and the news from Spain

Between 1816 and 1820 Mexico was, to outward appearances, pacified. The insurgency survived only in the southern hills. The viceregal government functioned, the mines were being restored, and the creole elite had spent a decade helping to defeat a movement that had threatened its estates.

Then, in 1820, a mutiny in Spain among troops being assembled to sail for America forced the king to restore the Constitution of 1812. Spain now had a liberal government that was legislating against clerical privileges and corporate property and that intended to apply its measures throughout the monarchy.

Put yourself in the position of a Mexican bishop, a great landowner, or a senior royalist officer. For ten years the imperial connection has been the guarantee of the social order and the insurgents have been the threat to it. Suddenly the imperial connection is the threat. The question answers itself: if Spain is going to become a liberal state, Mexico must leave it - and the way to leave without disorder is for the army that defeated the insurgency to declare independence itself, on terms that guarantee religion and property.

That is exactly what happened, and it happened fast.

### 2. Independence in 1821, and the phrase that describes it

✅ **Fact:** **Mexico becomes independent** in **1821**, with the recorded significance that **Spanish rule weakens decisively in North America**.

✅ **Fact:** the sequence is **Hidalgo, then Morelos, then Iturbide**, described as **a longer sequence from popular revolt to conservative settlement**.

✅ **Fact:** the distinctive feature recorded for the Mexican path is that it **began as a popular/indigenous rising and ended in a conservative settlement led by a royalist officer - the sharpest reversal in the region**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "the sharpest reversal in the region" is the standard assessment and can be used as the thesis of any answer on Mexico. The work of this lesson is to supply the mechanism that makes the reversal explicable, because an unexplained reversal reads as an anecdote.

### 3. Iguala and Cordoba: the instruments of separation

✅ **Fact:** **Agustin de Iturbide** was a creole officer who had fought against the insurgency with distinction. Given command in **1820** of operations against the surviving insurgent force in the south, he instead negotiated with its commander **Vicente Guerrero** and on **24 February 1821** proclaimed the **Plan of Iguala**. Its three guarantees were **religion** - Roman Catholicism as the sole established religion with clerical privileges protected; **independence** - a sovereign Mexican monarchy, the crown to be offered to a Bourbon prince; and **union** - legal equality of peninsular-born and American-born inhabitants, with caste distinctions abolished. The combined force was called the **Army of the Three Guarantees**.

✅ **Fact:** the newly arrived Spanish chief authority, **O'Donoju**, signed the **Treaty of Cordoba** in **August 1821**, accepting Mexican independence on the basis of the Plan; Spain subsequently repudiated the treaty, but the fact of independence was not reversed. ✅ Independence is dated **1821**.

✅ **Fact:** no Bourbon prince accepted the throne. **Iturbide was proclaimed emperor in 1822**, was **deposed in 1823**, and Mexico became a **federal republic under the constitution of 1824**. ✅ The provinces of Central America separated from Spain in 1821, were briefly annexed to the Mexican empire, and after its fall formed a separate federation which subsequently fragmented into individual states.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the speed and the sequence. Independence was declared in February 1821, conceded by treaty in August 1821, converted into a monarchy in 1822, into a republic by 1824, and the associated Central American union, federated in 1823, broke up between 1838 and 1841. ⚠️ That is the fragmentation mechanism of Lesson 18 appearing in miniature, and it appears immediately, which is evidence that fragmentation was structural rather than the result of later mismanagement.

### 4. Reading the three guarantees as a political settlement

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Plan of Iguala is the most economical document in the topic for showing what a conservative independence looks like, because each of its guarantees is aimed at a specific constituency and each carries a specific silence.

| Guarantee | Constituency addressed | What it promises | ⚠️ What it conspicuously omits |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Religion** | Church hierarchy; devout landowners; rural society | Catholicism established and clerical privilege protected against Spanish liberal legislation | Any reform of ecclesiastical landholding or of the tithe |
| **Independence** | Creole elite; the officer corps | Sovereignty, and a monarchy rather than a republic - continuity of form | Any popular constituent process; the crown is offered to a European prince |
| **Union** | Peninsular residents; the insurgent remnant; mixed-descent populations | Legal equality of peninsulares and Americans; abolition of caste distinctions | Land; tribute arrangements in practice; enforcement machinery; and any provision for those still enslaved |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two conclusions follow, and both are examinable.

1. **Caste abolition was cheap and land reform was not.** The Plan granted formal legal equality precisely because it cost the propertied nothing, while omitting every measure that would have transferred assets. ✅ This is the mechanism behind the general finding that independence did not create equality and that social and racial hierarchies often survived - visible here in a single document.
2. **The union guarantee was a peace treaty, not a social programme.** Its function was to bring the insurgent remnant and the peninsular residents into the same settlement so that independence could be achieved without a renewed civil war. It worked; and its success is precisely why nothing below the level of legal status changed.

### 5. What the reversal proves

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Mexican sequence is the single most valuable case in this session for answering the question the syllabus actually licenses - what were the effects on society. It proves four things, each with evidence inside one country.

| Proposition | Evidence from the Mexican sequence |
|---|---|
| Independence and social revolution are separable | The socially radical movement of 1810-15 was defeated; independence arrived in 1821 without it |
| The social content of independence follows the composition of the winning coalition | ✅ A royalist officer, the church and the creole elite made the 1821 settlement, and it protected religion, property and office |
| External events can invert internal alignments without changing anyone's interests | ✅ The 1820 Spanish revolt turned loyalty into a threat; the same people made the opposite choice for the same reason |
| Formal equality is the cheapest concession available | Caste distinctions abolished in the same document that guarantees property and clerical privilege |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and it supplies the sharpest available formulation for an essay or a 20-mark conclusion: **in Mexico, independence was not the victory of the revolution but the terms on which the revolution was ended.**

### 6. The Mexican path against the others, in one table

| Axis | Mexico | Northern and southern South America | Brazil | Haiti |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Who began it | ✅ A parish priest leading a popular and Indigenous rising | Creole city elites forming juntas | The Portuguese heir and the Brazilian elite | The enslaved majority |
| Creole elite's initial position | ✅ Royalist | Patriot leadership | Separatist under the monarchy | Opposed |
| How independence was achieved | ✅ By the royalist army, against a liberal metropolis | By fifteen years of war | ✅ By declaration under a monarchy | By defeating an attempted restoration |
| Constitutional form adopted | Monarchy 1822, republic 1824 | Republics | ✅ Monarchy retained | Republic |
| Social outcome | Caste abolished in law; property, church and hierarchy protected | ✅ Hierarchy largely preserved | ✅ Slavery persisted long after independence | ✅ Slavery ended immediately |

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Calling 1821 a conservative settlement is a judgement smuggled in as a description. The Plan of Iguala abolished the caste distinctions, declared the legal equality of peninsulares and Americans, and was accepted by the last insurgent commander in the field, who would hardly have joined a counter-revolution. Within three years Mexico was a federal republic with an elected congress. Measured against the colonial order it replaced, that is a substantial advance, and describing it as conservative reflects disappointment that it was not more radical rather than an assessment of what it did.

**Reply:** The objection is right that the settlement was not reactionary and that measuring it against an ideal rather than against its predecessor is unfair, and an answer should say so. But "conservative" here is a precise description of function, not a term of disapproval, and three pieces of evidence support it. First, the settlement's own origin: ✅ it was produced by the royalist command, the church hierarchy and the creole elite in response to a liberal turn in the metropolis, which means its purpose was to insulate the existing order from reform. Second, its silences: it granted the one thing that cost nothing - legal equality of status - and omitted every measure that would have transferred assets, whereas the defeated movement of 1810-15 had proposed exactly those measures. Third, the recorded outcome: ✅ power remained with creole elites, social and racial hierarchies survived, and Indigenous land pressure often continued or worsened. As for Guerrero's participation, it establishes that the settlement was broad enough to end the civil war rather than that it was socially transformative - and the subsequent history of the insurgent leaders in independent Mexico is not evidence that their programme was adopted. The disciplined formulation is that 1821 was a genuine political rupture and a deliberate social continuity.

**Residual:** It cannot be established here the motives of the individual actors - whether Iturbide's proclamation was primarily a defence of the church and property, a career calculation, or a genuine conversion to independence - and no claim about his intentions is made here. Nor can it establish how the caste abolition of 1821 operated in practice in the following decades; only the general finding that hierarchy survived is secure.

### UPSC application

Mexico is the case to lead with whenever a question pairs independence with social change, because the whole argument can be made inside one country and therefore cannot be dismissed as a comparison between dissimilar places. The construction is four sentences: the rising of 1810-15 proposed independence with social transformation and was defeated by a coalition including the creole elite; the Spanish liberal revolt of 1820 made the imperial connection a threat to the established order; the Plan of Iguala of 1821 delivered independence with guarantees for religion, property and legal equality of status and with no land measure; and ✅ the result is recorded as the sharpest reversal in the region. That sequence is a complete body paragraph and it earns marks because it supplies a mechanism rather than an attitude.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Presenting Mexican independence as the culmination of Hidalgo's movement, which inverts the actual relationship.
- Omitting the 1820 Spanish revolt, which makes the reversal look arbitrary.
- Forgetting that Iturbide had fought against the insurgency, which is the whole point of the reversal.
- Treating the abolition of caste distinctions in the Plan as evidence of social radicalism; it was the cheapest available concession.
- Dating Mexican independence to 1810 or to 1822 rather than ✅ 1821.
- Omitting the monarchy of 1822 and the republic of 1824, which show the settlement's instability.
- Ignoring Central America, whose separation and rapid fragmentation previews Lesson 18.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Mexico becomes independent in **1821**; Spanish rule weakens decisively in North America.
2. ✅ Sequence: Hidalgo -> Morelos -> Iturbide; a longer sequence from popular revolt to conservative settlement.
3. ✅ Owners' assessment: the sharpest reversal in the region - a popular and Indigenous rising ending in a conservative settlement led by a royalist officer.
4. ✅ 1820: Riego's revolt restores the Constitution of 1812; a liberal Spain now threatens clerical privilege and corporate property.
5. ✅ **Iturbide**, royalist commander, negotiates with the insurgent **Guerrero** and proclaims the **Plan of Iguala on 24 February 1821**.
6. ✅ The three guarantees: **religion**, **independence** under a monarchy with the crown offered to a Bourbon prince, **union** with equality of peninsulares and Americans and abolition of caste distinctions; enforced by the **Army of the Three Guarantees**.
7. ✅ **Treaty of Cordoba**, August 1821, signed by O'Donoju; repudiated by Spain; independence not reversed.
8. ✅ No Bourbon prince accepted; **Iturbide emperor 1822**, **deposed 1823**; **federal republic 1824**.
9. ✅ Central America separated in 1821, was briefly annexed to the Mexican empire, formed a federation, and then fragmented.
10. ⚠️ The guarantees read as a settlement: each addresses a constituency; the silences are land, tithe, enforcement and those still enslaved.
11. ⚠️ Caste abolition was cheap; land reform was not - the mechanism behind formal equality with surviving hierarchy.
12. ⚠️ Four propositions proved inside one country: independence and social revolution are separable; social content follows the winning coalition; external events invert alignments without changing interests; formal equality is the cheapest concession.
13. ⚠️ The formulation to memorise: in Mexico, independence was not the victory of the revolution but the terms on which the revolution was ended.
14. ⚠️ Rapid constitutional instability - monarchy to republic in two years, federation to fragments in a few more - previews the general fragmentation mechanism.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how a liberal revolution in Spain produced a conservative independence in Mexico, and state what this episode establishes about the relationship between metropolitan politics and colonial outcomes.

**Model answer:** It produced it by reversing which government threatened the established order. Between 1810 and 1815 the threat in New Spain came from below: the rising under Hidalgo and then Morelos abolished slavery and tribute by decree, ordered the restitution of community land, and at Chilpancingo and Apatzingan constitutionalised caste abolition and limits on great estates, so the creole elite, the church hierarchy and the royalist army fought together to defeat it. After Riego's revolt of 1820 forced Ferdinand VII to restore the Constitution of 1812, the threat came from above: a liberal Spanish government legislating against clerical privilege and corporate property intended to apply its measures throughout the monarchy. The same interests therefore made the opposite choice. Iturbide, the royalist commander sent against the last insurgent force, negotiated instead with Guerrero and proclaimed the Plan of Iguala on 24 February 1821, guaranteeing religion, independence under a monarchy and the legal equality of peninsulares and Americans with caste distinctions abolished - and guaranteeing nothing about land. The Treaty of Cordoba followed in August 1821 and independence is dated to that year. What the episode establishes is that metropolitan politics determined the timing and the political form of colonial outcomes without determining their social content, which was set by whichever domestic coalition was able to seize the opening. Here the coalition was the one that had spent a decade defeating social reform, which is why this is recorded as a popular and Indigenous rising that ended in a conservative settlement led by a royalist officer - the sharpest reversal in the region.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the abolition of caste distinctions in the Plan of Iguala as proof of radical intent. Legal equality of status was the one concession that transferred no assets, which is exactly why a settlement designed to protect property and clerical privilege could include it without difficulty.

*The next lesson crosses to the northern theatre and to the figure whose name the topic carries. It begins where a heroic narrative does not want to begin - with two republics destroyed, a leader in exile twice, and a war in which the majority of the armed population of Venezuela fought against the patriot cause. The reasons for those failures are what produced the strategy, the political programme and the social bargains that eventually worked.*
