### Visual: failure as a curriculum

```text
   WHAT WENT WRONG, AND WHAT EACH FAILURE TAUGHT

   1810-1812  FIRST REPUBLIC (Venezuela)
   a creole junta; a congress; a federal constitution;
   independence declared 1811; narrow social base;
   regional jealousies; an earthquake read as divine judgement
   -> COLLAPSES 1812
         LESSON DRAWN: federalism and legalism cannot
         fight a war. Concentrate authority.
                          |
   1813-1814  SECOND REPUBLIC
   the Admirable Campaign restores Caracas; but the
   royalist cause mobilises the LLANEROS - the mixed-descent
   plainsmen - who have more to fear from the creole
   republic than from the crown
   -> COLLAPSES 1814
         LESSON DRAWN: the war is a CIVIL war and it will be
         won by whoever recruits the interior. The republic
         must OFFER SOMETHING to the people it has ignored.
                          |
   1815      EXILE - JAMAICA and then HAITI
   the analysis is written down; assistance is obtained
   from President Petion of Haiti ON ONE CONDITION:
   EMANCIPATION
         LESSON DRAWN: abolition is not only a principle,
         it is a RECRUITING INSTRUMENT and a price of alliance.
                          |
   1816-1818 RETURN VIA THE ORINOCO
   base the republic in the interior, not on the coast;
   win the llaneros over; accept foreign volunteers;
   build a government at Angostura
         LESSON DRAWN: geography and coalition beat
         proclamations. -> LESSON 11
```

*The diagram is the reason this lesson exists in a session that could have gone straight to 1819. Bolivar's successes are not explicable without his failures, because every element of the winning strategy - concentrated command, a base in the interior, a social bargain with the plainsmen, abolition as policy, foreign volunteers and an external ally - was adopted in direct response to a specific defeat.*

### 1. A junta in Caracas, and the arithmetic beneath it

In 1810 a junta in Caracas did what juntas were doing across Spanish America: it removed the captain-general and assumed authority in the name of the captive king. Within a year it had gone further than most, and a congress had declared independence outright.

It did not last two years. And the reason is the reason the whole northern war was so long: the republic was a project of the creole elite of a handful of cities, in a territory whose interior was populated by people who had no reason to prefer a republic run by the men who owned the cattle to a distant king who occasionally protected them against those same men.

This produced a pattern that recurs throughout Spanish America, and it must be understood before any campaign can be followed. **The royalist armies in this war were largely American, and in Venezuela they were largely non-white.** Mixed-descent plainsmen, enslaved men promised freedom for royal service, and Indigenous communities that had received royal protection against creole encroachment supplied much of the crown's fighting strength. A patriot leadership that could not change that arithmetic could not win, regardless of how many declarations it issued.

The first phase of Bolivar's career is the record of him learning this. The second phase, in the next lesson, is the record of him acting on it.

### 2. Bolivar's place, and the correction attached to it

✅ **Fact:** **Simon Bolivar** is the leading figure of **northern South American independence**, and **Venezuela, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru and Bolivia are tied to his campaigns**.

✅ **Fact:** ✅ the colonies **began to form juntas** and **challenge old authority** after the Spanish monarchy was destabilised.

✅ **Fact:** **Bolivar secured New Granada and built Gran Colombia** in **1819**, with the recorded significance that **northern liberation gains momentum**; ✅ he became **president of Gran Colombia**.

✅ **Fact:** the distinctive feature of the northern path is a **continental-scale republican project**, and one correction is unavoidable: ❌ that he **successfully united Spanish America permanently** is false; ✅ **his integrative vision did not endure politically**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the analytical spine of this lesson is that the northern project was continental in scale and fragile in execution, and everything below is arranged to explain why both halves of that judgement are true.

### 3. Two republics, and how each of them was lost

✅ **Fact:** a junta was formed in **Caracas in April 1810**; a congress declared **Venezuelan independence on 5 July 1811** and adopted a federal constitution. The **First Republic collapsed in 1812** under royalist counter-attack, in a period that included a severe **earthquake** which royalist preaching interpreted as divine judgement on the rebellion.

✅ **Fact:** in exile in New Granada, Bolivar issued the **Cartagena Manifesto** in **1812**, analysing the collapse and attributing it principally to federalism, to the weakness of a volunteer militia system, to fiscal mismanagement and to the failure to concentrate authority in a single command.

✅ **Fact:** in **1813** the **Admirable Campaign** carried Bolivar from New Granada into Venezuela and restored patriot control of **Caracas**, establishing the **Second Republic**. In the same year he proclaimed the policy known as **war to the death**, which distinguished between Spaniards and Americans and offered life to Americans who abandoned the royalist cause while declaring it forfeit for Spaniards who did not join the patriot side.

✅ **Fact:** the **Second Republic collapsed in 1814**, principally under the pressure of royalist forces drawn from the **llaneros**, the mixed-descent plainsmen of the interior grasslands, mobilised under **Jose Tomas Boves**. Their campaign was directed against the creole propertied class as well as against the republic.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the llanero mobilisation of 1814 is one of the two or three most important facts in the entire topic, and it is almost always omitted. It shows, with unusual clarity, that the fighting was not colonisers against colonised. A large mixed-descent population, having weighed a creole republic against a distant monarchy, chose the monarchy - and fought for it with a social animus directed at the patriot elite. ⚠️ Any account of Spanish American independence as a national liberation struggle has to explain that fact, and the only explanations available are social.

⚠️ **Evidentiary discipline:** no figure for the size of any force, and no casualty figure for the war to the death, for the llanero campaigns or for the sieges of this period, is verified here, and none is given. The **war to the death** proclamation is recorded as a policy with a stated content; its application is not quantified here.

### 4. Exile, analysis, and the Haitian condition

✅ **Fact:** in **1815**, in exile, Bolivar wrote the document known as the **Jamaica Letter**, which surveyed the prospects of the Spanish American territories, argued that they could not return to colonial status, predicted that they would form separate states rather than a single one, expressed scepticism about federal and purely democratic forms in societies without political experience, and proposed a strong executive - with a suggestion that the isthmus of Panama might become the seat of a congress of the new states.

✅ **Fact:** in **1816** Bolivar received asylum, arms, ships and supplies in **Haiti** from President **Alexandre Petion**, on the explicit condition that he proclaim the **emancipation of the enslaved** in the territories he liberated. Bolivar accepted, issued emancipation decrees on returning to the mainland, and thereafter made the offer of freedom in return for military service a standing part of patriot recruitment.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this episode does four jobs at once in an answer, and it is worth all four.

1. **It connects the five paths materially.** ✅ Haiti is not only an example in this topic; it is a supplier and an ally, and the only state in the region whose assistance carried an abolitionist condition.
2. **It explains the timing and the limits of northern emancipation.** Abolition entered the patriot programme as a condition of alliance and a means of recruitment. That accounts for both its adoption and its subsequent conditionality: freedom offered for service is not the same as general emancipation, and the difference is precisely what Lesson 17 audits.
3. **It illustrates the recruitment logic that decided the war.** Both sides were bidding for the same populations, and the side that could offer freedom, land, rank or tribute relief could change the military balance without winning a battle.
4. **It supplies the strongest available counter to the claim that mainland leaders were indifferent to slavery.** They were not indifferent; they were constrained, and they acted when a constraint - in this case the price of Haitian assistance - pushed them.

### 5. The four lessons, and why they matter for what follows

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the first phase can be summarised as a curriculum, and doing so converts a sequence of defeats into an explanation of the victories.

| Failure | Diagnosis | Change adopted |
|---|---|---|
| First Republic, 1812 | Federal constitution, dispersed authority, militia weakness, fiscal failure | Concentrated command and executive power; a professional standing force |
| Second Republic, 1814 | The interior population fought for the king; the republic had offered it nothing | A social bargain: emancipation for service, land grants and promotion to the llaneros and to men of colour |
| Coastal vulnerability | Cities on the coast could be retaken by sea | Relocate the base of operations to the interior river system, beyond naval reach |
| Isolation | No external supply, no recognition, no capital | Haitian assistance; foreign volunteers; later, British commercial interest |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and one lesson was drawn that was not military at all, and that shapes Lessons 11 and 18. Bolivar concluded from the collapse of the First Republic that Spanish American societies lacked the political experience for federal or fully representative government, and he therefore built institutions with strong executives and, later, proposed constitutional arrangements of increasing centralisation. ⚠️ That conviction produced the political effectiveness of the second phase and the political failure of the 1820s, and an answer that notices the connection between the two is making an argument rather than narrating a life.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This lesson attributes to structural social causes what was mainly a matter of military capacity. The First and Second Republics fell because they were militarily weaker than the royalist forces opposed to them and because Spain was able to send a substantial expedition after 1815. Explaining the llanero mobilisation by social grievance romanticises what was in large part a war economy - men followed commanders who allowed them to take cattle and plunder - and turns a military history into a sociological one.

**Reply:** The objection is right that material incentive and plunder were real motives and that a purely ideological account of llanero allegiance would be sentimental. But the two explanations are not rivals; they converge. The reason the royalist command could offer cattle, licence and promotion to the plainsmen while the republic could not is that the cattle belonged to the creole families who were the republic's leadership. The republic could not outbid the crown for the interior without attacking the property of the men who financed it, and that constraint is social, not military. The decisive evidence is what happened when the constraint was addressed: ✅ once emancipation was proclaimed and llanero commanders were offered rank, land and a share in the new order, the same population moved to the patriot side and the military balance changed. If allegiance had been determined by capacity alone, that shift would not have been available. The disciplined formulation is that military capacity decided the battles and social bargaining decided who was available to fight in them.

**Residual:** It cannot be established here the scale of enslaved enlistment, the number of emancipations actually effected under the decrees of 1816 onward, or how far promises of land to llanero soldiers were honoured. The session therefore records the decrees and the offers as policy, and records in Lesson 17 that their implementation is exactly where the evidence thins.

### UPSC application

Two uses. First, this lesson is what allows an answer to describe the wars accurately as civil wars within American society rather than as colonial wars between Spaniards and Americans, and that single reframing is worth a great deal because it explains their length and their bitterness in one move. Second, the Haitian assistance of 1816 is the best available single fact for a question on Haiti's significance or on the connections among the five paths: it shows Haiti operating as an actor rather than a symbol, and it ties the abolition question to the military history. For any question on Bolivar, resist the biography; the structure to use is failure, diagnosis, adaptation, which is analysis, and it sets up Lesson 11 to deliver the result.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Beginning the northern story in 1819, which removes the explanation of why the winning strategy took the form it did.
- Treating royalist forces as Spanish, when in Venezuela the decisive royalist mobilisation was of American plainsmen.
- Omitting the llanero shift, which is the single clearest evidence that the wars turned on social bargaining.
- Presenting Bolivar's emancipation decrees as purely ideological, which loses both the Haitian condition and the recruitment function.
- Presenting them as purely cynical, which cannot explain his acceptance of a condition he was free to refuse.
- Supplying force sizes or casualty figures for any engagement; none is verified here.
- Claiming Bolivar permanently united Spanish America - an explicitly recorded prohibition; ✅ his integrative vision did not endure politically.
- Reducing the Cartagena Manifesto to a complaint, when it is the diagnosis from which every element of the winning strategy was built.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Bolivar is the leading figure of northern South American independence; Venezuela, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru and Bolivia are tied to his campaigns.
2. ✅ Caracas junta April 1810; independence declared 5 July 1811; federal constitution; **First Republic collapses 1812**, with an earthquake used by royalist preaching as divine judgement.
3. ✅ **Cartagena Manifesto, 1812**: the collapse attributed to federalism, militia weakness, fiscal failure and dispersed authority.
4. ✅ **Admirable Campaign, 1813**; Caracas restored; **Second Republic**; the **war to the death** policy distinguishing Spaniards from Americans.
5. ✅ **Second Republic collapses 1814** under royalist **llanero** forces mobilised by **Boves**, with a social animus against the creole propertied class.
6. ⚠️ The llanero mobilisation is decisive evidence that these were civil wars: a large mixed-descent population chose the monarchy over a creole republic.
7. ✅ **Jamaica Letter, 1815**: no return to colonial status; separate states rather than one; scepticism about federal and purely democratic forms; a strong executive; a congress at the isthmus of Panama.
8. ✅ **1816**: asylum, arms and supplies from **Petion of Haiti** on the express condition of **emancipation**; Bolivar accepted and issued emancipation decrees.
9. ⚠️ Four uses of the Haitian episode: material connection among the paths; explanation of the timing and conditionality of northern emancipation; the recruitment logic of the war; the answer to the charge of indifference.
10. ⚠️ The curriculum: concentrate command; make a social bargain with the interior; move the base inland; obtain external supply.
11. ⚠️ The non-military lesson: distrust of federal and fully representative forms, which produced both the effectiveness of the second phase and the political failure of the 1820s.
12. ✅ Recorded prohibition: do not write that Bolivar permanently united Spanish America.
13. ⚠️ No force size, casualty or emancipation figure is held here.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the patriot republics in Venezuela failed twice before 1815, and show how the diagnosis of those failures determined the strategy that later succeeded.

**Model answer:** They failed because they were narrow projects in a society whose majority had no stake in them. The Caracas junta of 1810 and the congress that declared independence on 5 July 1811 rested on the creole elite of a few cities, adopted a federal constitution that dispersed authority, relied on militia, and could not command the interior; the First Republic collapsed in 1812, in a period when a severe earthquake was preached as divine judgement on the rebellion. The Second Republic, restored by the Admirable Campaign of 1813, collapsed in 1814 under royalist forces drawn from the llaneros, the mixed-descent plainsmen of the interior mobilised by Boves, whose campaign was directed at the creole propertied class as much as at the republic. The decisive point is that the republic could not outbid the crown for the interior without attacking the property of the families who financed it. The diagnosis determined the strategy in four ways, each traceable to a specific failure. Federal dispersal produced the Cartagena Manifesto of 1812 and a commitment to concentrated command and a standing force. The llanero defeat produced a social bargain: emancipation for military service, land and rank for the plainsmen and for men of colour. Coastal vulnerability produced the relocation of the base to the interior river system beyond naval reach. Isolation produced the search for external support, answered in 1816 when Petion of Haiti supplied arms and asylum on the express condition of emancipation. The campaigns that succeeded from 1819 used all four, which is why the failures are part of the explanation rather than a preface to it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because the royalist side held the interior, it represented colonial authority against a national movement. The forces that destroyed the Second Republic were American and largely non-white, and they fought a creole republic for reasons internal to Venezuelan society. The war was a civil war, and treating it otherwise makes both its length and its eventual resolution impossible to explain.

*The next lesson delivers the result of that curriculum. With a base in the interior, a social bargain with the plainsmen, foreign volunteers and a government at Angostura, the northern campaign took the least expected route available, crossed flooded plains and a high mountain pass, and destroyed a royalist army in the territory that was supposed to be safest.*

## Lesson 11 - Bolivar, second phase: the Orinoco route, Boyaca, Gran Colombia and Carabobo

Progress: 11/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Angostura base and congress; the llanero alliance under Paez; the British Legion; the march of 1819 and Boyaca; the creation of Gran Colombia; Carabobo 1821; Pichincha 1822 and Sucre; why the northern project was continental and why it was fragile

### Visual: the strategic inversion of 1819

```text
   hold the COASTAL CITIES                base the republic on the
   Caracas, Cartagena, the ports          ORINOCO river system at
        |                                 ANGOSTURA - inland, navigable,
   the coast is where Spanish              unreachable by a fleet

        |                                  of fighting them: emancipation
   the interior is hostile:                for service, land, rank, and a
   the llaneros fight FOR the king         commander of their own (PAEZ)
        |                                         |
   result: TWO REPUBLICS LOST              add FOREIGN VOLUNTEERS - the
                                           British Legion - and a
                                           government + congress at
                                           Angostura for legitimacy
                                                  |
                                           1819: DO NOT ATTACK VENEZUELA.
                                           March WEST across flooded
                                           plains and OVER THE ANDES into
                                           NEW GRANADA, where the royalist
                                           command expects nothing
                                                  |
                                           7 AUGUST 1819: BOYACA
                                           -> Bogota falls; ✅ NEW GRANADA
                                              SECURED; its revenue, men
                                              and territory now fund the war
                                                  |
                                           DEC 1819: ✅ GRAN COLOMBIA created
                                           (Venezuela + New Granada + Quito
                                           as a projected union)
                                                  |
                                           24 JUNE 1821: CARABOBO
                                           -> Venezuela decided
                                           24 MAY 1822: PICHINCHA (SUCRE)
                                           -> Quito secured; the northern
                                              army now faces PERU
```

*The diagram shows why 1819 is the turning point of the northern war and why it is a strategic rather than a heroic fact. Bolivar did not win by fighting harder in the place he had twice lost; he changed the base, changed the coalition and changed the target. The march itself is famous; the decision that made it possible - to stop contesting the coast and to buy the interior - is what an answer should reward.*

### 1. Three decisions taken after two defeats

By 1816 the patriot cause in the north had been defeated twice and a substantial Spanish expedition had arrived to complete the reconquest. The obvious moves had all been tried and had all failed.

What Bolivar did instead can be stated as three decisions. First, he stopped trying to hold cities and moved the seat of the republic to the interior, to a river town far up the Orinoco that no fleet could reach and that gave access by water to an enormous hinterland. Second, he stopped treating the plainsmen as enemies and recruited them, which required offering things - freedom, land, rank, plunder, a commander of their own - that the earlier republics had refused to offer. Third, he attacked where he was not expected: not into Venezuela, where the royalist army was concentrated, but westward and over the mountains into New Granada, whose wealth and population would then finance everything else.

The result is that within three years the northern theatre went from near-extinction to the possession of a state stretching from the Caribbean to the equator. And the same three decisions that produced that speed also produced the instability that destroyed the state within a decade - because a republic built on a wartime coalition of regional commanders has to keep paying them afterwards.

### 2. 1819 in the record, and one standing prohibition

✅ **Fact:** **Bolivar secures New Granada and builds Gran Colombia** in **1819**; the recorded significance is that **northern liberation gains momentum**.

✅ **Fact:** the northern path's distinctive feature is a **continental-scale republican project**, and **Gran Colombia's later fragmentation shows how fragile it was**.

✅ **Fact:** **Bolivar** became **president of Gran Colombia**, and ✅ **Venezuela, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru and Bolivia are tied to his campaigns**.

✅ **Fact:** one assessment and one prohibition run together here: ❌ it is false that he **successfully united Spanish America permanently**; ✅ **his integrative vision did not endure politically**.

### 3. From Angostura to Pichincha, stage by stage

✅ **Fact:** the sequence is as follows.

| Stage | ✅ Date | What it did |
|---|---|---|
| Patriot base established in the Orinoco interior at **Angostura** | 1817-18 | Removed the war from naval reach and opened a river supply line |
| Alliance with the llanero commander **Jose Antonio Paez** | 1818 | Converted the force that had destroyed the Second Republic into the patriot cavalry arm |
| Recruitment of foreign volunteers, principally from Britain and Ireland, known as the **British Legion** | 1817-19 | Supplied trained infantry and officers; financed partly by London credit and surplus war material |
| **Congress of Angostura** convened; Bolivar's address on constitutional design | February **1819** | Gave the republic a legislature and a constitutional argument for a strong executive and a moral fourth power |
| March west across the flooded plains and over the eastern Andes into New Granada | mid-**1819** | The operational decision of the war |
| **Battle of Boyaca** | **7 August 1819** | Destroyed the royalist army in New Granada; Bogota fell days later; ✅ New Granada secured |
| **Gran Colombia** proclaimed by the Congress of Angostura | December **1819** | ✅ Union of Venezuela and New Granada, with Quito included as a projected component |
| **Battle of Carabobo** | **24 June 1821** | Decided Venezuela; Caracas secured |
| Constitution of Cucuta; Bolivar president, **Santander** vice-president and effective administrator | **1821** | ✅ Bolivar president of Gran Colombia; a centralised republic on paper |
| **Battle of Pichincha**, won by **Antonio Jose de Sucre** | **24 May 1822** | Secured Quito; incorporated into Gran Colombia; opened the route to Peru |

⚠️ **Evidentiary discipline:** no figure for the strength or losses of any force in this campaign, and no figure for mortality on the Andean crossing, is asserted here. The crossing was exceptionally costly in men and animals, and that qualitative statement is the limit of what is claimed.

### 4. Why the strategy worked: four mechanisms

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the campaign is easy to narrate and hard to explain, and the explanation is what earns marks. Four mechanisms did the work.

| Mechanism | How it operated | ⚠️ Why it mattered beyond 1819 |
|---|---|---|
| **A base beyond naval reach** | Spanish maritime superiority was decisive on the coast and irrelevant on an inland river system | Converted a war Spain could win by sea into a war it had to win by land, at the end of a very long logistical chain |
| **The social bargain with the interior** | Emancipation for service; land and rank for llanero soldiers; a llanero commander given standing in the new state | It changed the composition of the armies without a battle - and it created a class of armed regional commanders with claims on the republic |
| **External supply and credit** | Foreign volunteers, arms and money, with London the principal source | It anticipates Lesson 19: independence was financed from Britain, and the debts survived the war |
| **Surprise of axis** | Attacking the least-defended and most valuable territory rather than the most contested | Gave the patriots New Granada's revenue and manpower, which is what made Carabobo and Pichincha possible |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second row is the one to carry into the later lessons. The bargain that won the war built the caudillo problem into the state at its foundation. Paez is the clearest case: the commander whose cavalry made victory possible became the dominant power in Venezuela and the decisive figure in its separation from Gran Colombia. The connection between the winning method and the subsequent instability is not a coincidence; it is the mechanism of Lesson 18.

### 5. Gran Colombia: the design, and the three reasons it failed

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Gran Colombia deserves treatment as a *project* rather than as an episode, because it is the concrete form of the continental-scale republican project, and its collapse is the fragility in question.

✅ **Fact:** the union comprised the former viceroyalty of New Granada with Venezuela and, after 1822, Quito; its constitution of 1821 was centralist; its capital was Bogota; Bolivar was president and Santander vice-president. ✅ It dissolved in **1830** into Venezuela, New Granada and Ecuador.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three reasons, each of which generalises.

1. **Geography and communications.** The union joined territories separated by mountain ranges, by the Andes and by great distances with no integrated communication. ⚠️ Administrative unity in such a territory required an infrastructure that did not exist and a revenue that did not exist either.
2. **The regional commanders.** The war had been won by mobilising regional forces under commanders with local followings and local claims to reward. Those commanders were now provincial powers with armies, and the central government had to either satisfy them or fight them. ⚠️ Paez in Venezuela is the decisive instance.
3. **The constitutional quarrel.** The centralist design, and Bolivar's increasingly strong-executive proposals, collided with a federalist and liberal current associated with Santander and with regional elites who saw centralism as rule from Bogota. ⚠️ The quarrel was not abstract: it was about who controlled revenue, appointments and the army in each region.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the honest verdict is that Gran Colombia failed for the same reasons that it had been possible. It was created by a wartime command structure that could override regional interests while the war lasted, and it dissolved when that override expired. ✅ The settled formulation - his integrative vision did not endure politically - is exactly right and should be used.

### 6. The northern path in comparison

| Axis | ✅ Northern South America (Bolivar) | ✅ Southern South America (San Martin) |
|---|---|---|
| Base | An interior river system inside the contested territory | An external base in already-secured territory, west of the Andes after 1817 |
| Force | A coalition: regular units, llanero cavalry, foreign volunteers, emancipated recruits | A trained, disciplined, purpose-built regular army |
| Method | Mobile campaigning over enormous distances, with political construction in parallel | Deliberate preparation, then a single decisive projection of force |
| Political role | Head of state, constitution-maker, founder of a union | Liberator who declined to rule; Protector of Peru and then withdrawal |
| Legacy | A continental project that ✅ did not endure politically | Independent states in the south, and a disputed withdrawal |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** ✅ the standing correction - that the two men did not operate in the same theatre in the same way, and that their political roles differed - is exactly what this table demonstrates, and a comparison question is best answered from it rather than from two biographies laid side by side.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This lesson credits strategy with what was actually decided by Spanish weakness. After 1820 Spain was in revolution, its expeditionary force never sailed, its commanders in America were divided between constitutionalists and absolutists, and its treasury was empty. Carabobo and Pichincha were won against an enemy that had already been abandoned by its own government. Attributing the outcome to the Orinoco base and the llanero bargain overstates American agency in a collapse that was mainly metropolitan.

**Reply:** The objection identifies a genuine and important condition and an answer should include it, because a script that omits the effect of the 1820 revolt on Spanish forces in America is incomplete. But it proves less than it claims, for two reasons. First, chronology: ✅ Boyaca was fought on 7 August 1819 and ✅ New Granada was secured before the Spanish revolt of 1820, so the decisive strategic reversal preceded the metropolitan collapse rather than following it. Second, comparison: Spanish weakness after 1820 was available to every patriot force in America, and the outcomes still differed sharply by theatre - rapid in the north, protracted in Peru, and in Mexico producing a conservative settlement rather than a patriot victory. A condition that is common to all cases cannot explain the differences among them. The disciplined formulation is that the strategic and social reconstruction of 1816-19 made the northern cause survivable and then victorious, and that the metropolitan collapse after 1820 converted a winning position into a rapid conclusion.

**Residual:** It cannot be established here the scale of foreign volunteer participation, the terms on which llanero soldiers were actually rewarded, or how many emancipations the decrees produced. It also cannot settle the balance between Bolivar's constitutional preferences and regional resistance in the dissolution of Gran Colombia; the session records both as operative and weights neither.

### UPSC application

Three uses. First, for a "causes and course" question, this lesson supplies the course paragraph in four dated facts - ✅ Boyaca 1819, ✅ Gran Colombia 1819, ✅ Carabobo 1821, ✅ Pichincha 1822 - each attached to a function rather than narrated. Second, for a comparison question, section 6 is the answer structure; run one question through both theatres rather than describing each. Third, and most valuable, this lesson is where the post-independence instability of Lesson 18 becomes explicable: the armed regional commanders who made victory possible are the same men who made the union unworkable, and an answer that traces that line is giving a mechanism where most scripts give a lament.

### Where northern-campaign answers fail

- Narrating the Andes crossing as an adventure rather than explaining the strategic inversion that produced it.
- Dating the securing of New Granada or the creation of Gran Colombia to any year other than ✅ 1819.
- Writing that Bolivar united Spanish America permanently - an explicitly recorded prohibition.
- Omitting Sucre, and therefore attributing Pichincha and later Ayacucho to Bolivar personally.
- Supplying force numbers or crossing casualties; none is verified here.
- Treating Gran Colombia's collapse as a failure of will rather than as a structural consequence of how the war was won, or attributing the whole northern outcome to Spanish weakness after 1820, which the chronology of Boyaca contradicts.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **1819**: Bolivar secures New Granada and builds Gran Colombia; northern liberation gains momentum. ✅ Base at **Angostura** on the Orinoco, 1817-18; alliance with **Paez** and the llaneros, 1818; **British Legion** volunteers; **Congress of Angostura**, February 1819.
2. ✅ **Boyaca, 7 August 1819**; Bogota falls; New Granada secured.
3. ✅ **Gran Colombia** proclaimed December 1819; constitution of Cucuta 1821; ✅ Bolivar president, Santander vice-president. ✅ **Carabobo, 24 June 1821** decides Venezuela; **Pichincha, 24 May 1822**, won by **Sucre**, secures Quito.
4. ✅ Gran Colombia dissolves in **1830** into Venezuela, New Granada and Ecuador.
5. ⚠️ Four mechanisms: a base beyond naval reach; the social bargain with the interior; external supply and credit; surprise of axis.
6. ⚠️ The bargain that won the war created armed regional commanders with claims on the state - the caudillo problem built in at the foundation.
7. ⚠️ Three reasons Gran Colombia failed: geography and communications; regional commanders with local followings; the centralist-federalist quarrel over revenue, appointments and the army. ⚠️ It failed for the reasons that made it possible: a wartime command override that expired with the war.
8. ✅ Owners' verdict: a continental-scale republican project whose fragmentation shows how fragile it was; his integrative vision did not endure politically.
9. ⚠️ Comparison with the south: interior base against external base; coalition army against purpose-built regular army; head of state against liberator who withdrew.
10. ⚠️ Chronology control: Boyaca precedes the Spanish revolt of 1820, so the strategic reversal is not explained by metropolitan collapse. ⚠️ No force size, casualty or emancipation figure is held for this campaign.

### Concept check

**Question:** Account for the transformation of the northern theatre between 1816 and 1822, and explain why the method that produced victory also produced the instability that followed.

**Model answer:** The transformation followed from three connected decisions. The seat of the republic was moved inland to Angostura on the Orinoco, where Spanish naval superiority could not reach it and a river system supplied a large hinterland. The llaneros, whose mobilisation under Boves had destroyed the Second Republic in 1814, were recruited instead of fought: emancipation for military service, land and rank, and standing for their own commander Paez, which changed the composition of the armies without a battle. And external supply was obtained - foreign volunteers including the British Legion, arms and credit drawn principally from London - while the Congress of Angostura of February 1819 supplied a legislature and a constitutional argument. The operational expression of all three was the decision not to attack Venezuela but to march west across flooded plains and over the eastern Andes into New Granada, producing victory at Boyaca on 7 August 1819, the fall of Bogota, and the securing of a territory whose revenue and manpower funded everything afterwards: Gran Colombia proclaimed in December 1819, Carabobo on 24 June 1821 deciding Venezuela, and Sucre's victory at Pichincha on 24 May 1822 securing Quito. The instability follows from the same method. Victory was purchased by mobilising regional forces under commanders with local followings and unredeemed claims on the new state, so the republic was founded on a coalition of armed provincial powers. Once the wartime command override expired, those commanders became the decisive political actors, and a union joining territories separated by mountains and distance, with no integrated administration or revenue and a bitter quarrel between centralists and federalists over who controlled appointments and armies, dissolved in 1830 into three states. The verdict is exact: a continental-scale republican project whose fragmentation shows how fragile it was.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Andes crossing as the explanation. The crossing was the execution of a strategy, not the strategy; what decided the war was the prior reconstruction of the base, the coalition and the supply, and an answer that describes the march without those three things has described the most visible part of the least important question.

*The next lesson crosses to the other side of the continent, where a very different man was solving the same problem by the opposite method. Where the northern campaign improvised a coalition inside contested territory, the southern campaign spent three years building a professional army in a safe province and then projected it, in one operation, across the highest mountain barrier in the Americas.*

## Lesson 12 - San Martin: the continental plan, the Andes crossing and the liberation of Chile

Progress: 12/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the River Plate after 1810; why Upper Peru could not be taken from the south; the Cuyo base and the Army of the Andes; the crossing of 1817; Chacabuco and Maipu; O'Higgins and the Chilean settlement; sea power and the preparation for Peru

CA search: not sought - the single current linkage sits in Lesson 18
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: why the obvious route was the wrong one

```text
   THE PROBLEM AS SEEN FROM BUENOS AIRES AFTER 1810

   BUENOS AIRES is secure. The royalist centre of gravity in
   South America is LIMA and the Peruvian highlands.
   HOW DO YOU GET THERE?

   ROUTE 1 - THE OBVIOUS ONE: NORTH BY LAND THROUGH UPPER PERU
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   Buenos Aires -> Upper Peru -> Peru
   * enormous distances across high, poor, thin-aired terrain
   * every advance lengthens supply and shortens the enemy's
   * repeated expeditions sent; repeatedly defeated
   VERDICT: THE DIRECT ROUTE IS A TRAP. ABANDON IT.
   (what remained there was a local guerrilla war, which tied
    down royalist forces without being able to defeat them)

   ROUTE 2 - THE INDIRECT ONE: WEST, THEN NORTH BY SEA
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   STEP 1  build an army in a SAFE province: CUYO, at the foot
           of the Andes. Three years. Taxes, workshops,
           uniforms, cannon, discipline, intelligence.
   STEP 2  CROSS THE ANDES into CHILE by several passes at once
           -> CHACABUCO, 12 Feb 1817
   STEP 3  SECURE CHILE: government under O'HIGGINS;
           independence declared 1818; MAIPU, 5 Apr 1818
   STEP 4  USE CHILE'S COAST AND REVENUE TO BUILD A FLEET
   STEP 5  CARRY THE WAR TO PERU BY SEA, landing south of Lima
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   THE INSIGHT: Lima is reachable from the sea and unreachable
   from the southern highlands. The Andes crossing is not a
   heroic detour - IT IS THE ONLY WAY TO ACQUIRE A COASTLINE
   FROM WHICH PERU CAN BE ATTACKED.
```

*The diagram states the single most examinable fact about the southern campaign: it was a plan, made years in advance, whose whole purpose was to reach Peru by a route that worked. Chile was not the objective; Chile was the means. An answer that presents the Andes crossing as an act of daring has missed that this was the most carefully prepared operation in the entire topic.*

### 1. Buenos Aires is safe; Lima cannot be reached

The junta at Buenos Aires in 1810 succeeded where Caracas failed, for a simple reason: there was no significant royalist military power in the immediate vicinity, and the city's commercial elite had reasons of its own to welcome open trade. The River Plate therefore had, from very early, something no other patriot centre possessed - a secure territorial base with a revenue.

What it did not have was a way of finishing the war. The royalist centre in South America was the Peruvian highlands, with its viceregal government, its treasury and its veteran forces. Expeditions sent north by land into Upper Peru were defeated repeatedly, and the reason was structural rather than accidental: an army advancing hundreds of miles across high and impoverished country grows weaker with every mile while its opponent grows stronger.

San Martin's contribution was to see that this was not a problem to be solved by trying harder. If Peru could not be reached from the south by land, it had to be reached from the sea - and reaching it from the sea required a Pacific coastline, which meant Chile, which meant crossing the Andes. Everything else in the southern campaign follows from that chain of reasoning, including the three unglamorous years spent building an army in a provincial town before anything happened at all.

### 2. San Martin in the record: the crossing and the method

✅ **Fact:** **Jose de San Martin** is the **key southern liberator linked with Argentina, Chile and Peru**.

✅ **Fact:** **San Martin crosses the Andes into Chile** in **1817**, with the recorded significance that **southern liberation takes military shape**.

✅ **Fact:** the southern path's distinctive feature is **disciplined regular-army liberation from an external base**.

✅ **Fact:** ✅ **San Martin's career turns decisively after Napoleon's occupation of Spain in 1808**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that last fact is easy to pass over and is worth a sentence in an answer. San Martin was a professional officer in Spanish service who had fought in Europe; the imperial crisis converted a Spanish soldier into an American one. It is a reminder that the leadership of these movements was frequently formed inside the imperial system rather than outside it, which is part of why the movements were so militarily competent and so socially conservative.

### 3. From Cuyo to the Peruvian coast

✅ **Fact:** the sequence is as follows.

| Stage | ✅ Date | What it did |
|---|---|---|
| Junta formed at **Buenos Aires** | May **1810** | Established the secure southern base; ✅ part of the general wave of 1810 |
| Repeated expeditions north into **Upper Peru** defeated | 1811-1815 | Demonstrated that the land route to Peru was unusable |
| San Martin returns to the River Plate and takes command; appointed governor of **Cuyo** | 1812-1814 | Converted a frontier province into a military-industrial base |
| Formation and training of the **Army of the Andes** at **Mendoza** | 1814-1816 | Three years of preparation: taxation, workshops, powder, artillery, uniforms, discipline, deception operations |
| **Congress of Tucuman** declares the independence of the United Provinces | **1816** | Gave the campaign a sovereign authority to act for |
| **Crossing of the Andes** by multiple passes | January **1817** | ✅ The recorded marker; the operation that made everything else possible |
| **Battle of Chacabuco** | **12 February 1817** | Opened Santiago; patriot government established in Chile |
| **Bernardo O'Higgins** installed as **Supreme Director** of Chile | 1817 | Chilean government; ✅ the partner regime for the Peruvian phase |
| Chilean declaration of independence | **12 February 1818** | Formalised the settlement, on the anniversary of Chacabuco |
| **Battle of Maipu** | **5 April 1818** | Decided Chile after a dangerous royalist counter-offensive |
| Creation of a Chilean squadron, commanded by **Thomas Cochrane** | 1818-1820 | ✅ Gave the south the sea power that Peru required |
| Expedition sails for Peru | **1820** | The subject of Lesson 13 |

⚠️ **Evidentiary discipline:** no figure for the strength of the Army of the Andes, for the losses on the crossing, or for casualties at Chacabuco or Maipu, is verified here, and none is given.

### 4. What made the southern method different

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the comparison with the north is not a matter of temperament; it is a matter of the different problems the two theatres posed.

| Element | Northern method | Southern method | Why the difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Base** | Interior river system inside the contested territory | A secure province outside the contested territory | The north had no secure territory; the south did |
| **Force** | A coalition assembled from whoever could be recruited | A regular army raised, trained and equipped over three years | The north had to fight immediately; the south could afford to prepare |
| **Social bargaining** | Extensive and continuous: emancipation, land, rank | Present but narrower: enslaved men were enlisted with the promise of freedom, and the army's ranks drew heavily on them | The southern theatre had a smaller enslaved population and no llanero equivalent to win over |
| **Political construction** | Constitutions, congresses and a union built while fighting | Deliberately limited: liberate, install a local government, move on | A choice San Martin made and defended; it is the root of the Guayaquil question in Lesson 13 |
| **Decisive arm** | Cavalry and mobility over huge distances | Prepared infantry and artillery, then sea power | Terrain and objective |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third row deserves a note because it is often omitted entirely. The Army of the Andes recruited enslaved men in the River Plate provinces with the promise of freedom in exchange for service, and they formed a substantial part of its infantry. ⚠️ That is the same instrument used in the north, and it has the same double character: it was a genuine route out of bondage for those who survived, and it was a way of obtaining soldiers without a general emancipation. Lesson 17 audits what happened to such promises.

### 5. Chile: the settlement and its limits

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Chile is a small but analytically valuable case, and the evidence supports two points about it.

1. **It is the clearest case of liberation by external projection.** The territory was secured by an army raised elsewhere, and the government installed was headed by a Chilean commander who had fought with that army. ⚠️ That makes it the best illustration of disciplined regular-army liberation from an external base.
2. **Its subsequent political history is the region's partial exception.** ✅ In the region there **was no tradition of democracy, except in Chile**. ⚠️ That single exception is worth noticing in a comparative answer, because it shows that the region's later instability was not uniform and therefore not culturally determined; but this session does not attempt to explain the exception, because the material to do so is owned by the twentieth-century Latin America topic.

✅ **Fact:** O'Higgins's government abolished the formal titles of nobility and took steps toward ending slavery, and it fell in **1823** under pressure from landowning and conservative interests. ⚠️ The pattern - a liberator's government legislating against the old order and being displaced by the propertied interests whose support it needed - recurs across the region and is the domestic face of the caudillo problem.

### 6. Why the southern campaign stopped where it did

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the southern method had a limit built into it, and recognising it prepares Lesson 13. San Martin's principle was to liberate and hand over rather than to govern. That gave the southern campaign a clean operational logic and made it politically less threatening to local elites - but it meant that when the campaign reached Peru, where the royalist position was strongest and the local elite most reluctant, there was no political instrument to break the deadlock. A commander who will not seize the government has no answer when the government will not move.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that is the structural reason why the two campaigns had to meet, and why the meeting was difficult. The northern leader was a head of state with a constitution and an army; the southern leader was a general who declined to be one. They had complementary strengths and incompatible political methods, and Peru was where the incompatibility had to be resolved.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** The southern campaign is treated here as a plan executed, when in reality it was heavily contingent. The Buenos Aires government that financed it was unstable and frequently obstructive; the Chilean position was nearly lost in 1818 before Maipu; the fleet depended on hiring a foreign admiral and on borrowed money; and the Peruvian expedition ended in stalemate and withdrawal. Presenting this as the most carefully prepared operation in the topic reads the plan backwards from the parts that worked.

**Reply:** The contingency is real and should be stated, and the near-disaster before Maipu is exactly the sort of detail that improves an answer. But the objection conflates contingent execution with improvised design, and the design is documented in the sequence itself. Three years were spent building an army in Cuyo before any move was made; the crossing used multiple passes simultaneously with deception operations; a Chilean government was installed immediately and used to fund a squadron; and the squadron existed because Peru could only be attacked from the sea. That is a chain of means to an end, each step of which was undertaken before the next became necessary, and it is not reconstructable as luck. The stalemate in Peru, moreover, is not evidence against the plan; it is evidence for the limit identified in section 6 - that a method built on liberating and handing over could not resolve a situation in which the local elite declined to be liberated. The disciplined formulation is that the southern campaign was the best-planned operation in the topic and that its planning did not extend to the political problem it eventually met.

**Residual:** Nothing is established here on the finances of the Cuyo preparation, the composition of the Army of the Andes, the terms on which enslaved recruits were freed or the proportion who survived to claim freedom. Those gaps are recorded rather than filled, and Lesson 17 returns to the last of them as an open question.

### UPSC application

For a comparison question - and comparing the two liberators is a probable framing - this lesson supplies one half of the table in section 4, and the table is the answer. Run a single question through both theatres: what base, what force, what bargain, what political role, what legacy. For a "causes and course" answer, the southern campaign contributes exactly three dated facts - ✅ the crossing of 1817, ✅ Chacabuco and Maipu securing Chile, and the naval expedition to Peru in 1820 - and the crucial analytical sentence is that Chile was the means and Peru the objective. Avoid narrating the crossing; the marks are in the reason for it.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Treating the Andes crossing as an act of daring rather than as the acquisition of a Pacific coastline.
- Presenting Chile as the objective of the southern campaign rather than as the means to Peru.
- Omitting the failed expeditions to Upper Peru, which are what prove the land route was unusable.
- Forgetting the naval dimension, without which the Peruvian expedition is inexplicable.
- Omitting the enlistment of enslaved men in exchange for freedom, which is the southern instance of the region's recruitment bargain.
- Dating the crossing to any year other than ✅ 1817, or Chilean independence other than 1818.
- Supplying army strengths or casualty figures; none is verified here.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **San Martin**: the key southern liberator linked with Argentina, Chile and Peru; ✅ his career turns decisively after the occupation of Spain in 1808.
2. ✅ **1817**: crossing of the Andes into Chile; southern liberation takes military shape.
3. ✅ Owners' description of the southern path: disciplined regular-army liberation from an external base.
4. ✅ Buenos Aires junta May 1810; repeated defeats in Upper Peru 1811-15 prove the land route unusable.
5. ✅ Cuyo governorship; **Army of the Andes** built at **Mendoza**, 1814-16; **Congress of Tucuman**, 1816.
6. ✅ Crossing January 1817; **Chacabuco, 12 February 1817**; **O'Higgins** Supreme Director; Chilean independence declared **12 February 1818**; **Maipu, 5 April 1818**.
7. ✅ Chilean squadron under **Cochrane**, 1818-20; expedition sails for Peru in **1820**.
8. ⚠️ The strategic chain: Peru is unreachable by land from the south -> it must be attacked from the sea -> a Pacific coast is needed -> Chile -> the Andes.
9. ⚠️ Five axes of difference from the north: base, force, social bargaining, political construction, decisive arm.
10. ⚠️ Enslaved men were enlisted in the River Plate with the promise of freedom and formed a substantial part of the infantry - the southern form of the recruitment bargain.
11. ✅ O'Higgins abolished titles of nobility and moved against slavery; his government fell in 1823 under conservative and landowning pressure.
12. ✅ There was no tradition of democracy in the region **except in Chile** - an exception worth noting and not explained here.
13. ⚠️ The built-in limit: a commander who liberates and hands over has no instrument when the local elite will not move - which is why Peru deadlocked.
14. ⚠️ No army strength, crossing loss or battle casualty figure is held here.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why San Martin crossed the Andes rather than marching north, and show what this reveals about the nature of the southern campaign.

**Model answer:** He crossed because the direct route did not work and could not be made to work. The royalist centre of gravity in South America was the Peruvian highlands with their viceregal government, treasury and veteran forces, and the expeditions sent north from Buenos Aires into Upper Peru between 1811 and 1815 were repeatedly defeated for a structural reason: an army advancing hundreds of miles across high, poor and thinly supplied country weakens as it goes while its opponent, falling back on its own base, strengthens. If Peru could not be reached by land from the south, it had to be reached from the sea, which required a Pacific coastline, which required Chile, which required the Andes. The campaign was therefore built backwards from the objective: three years converting the frontier province of Cuyo into a military base at Mendoza, the formation and training of the Army of the Andes, the Congress of Tucuman's declaration in 1816 to supply a sovereign authority, the crossing by multiple passes in January 1817, Chacabuco on 12 February 1817, a Chilean government under O'Higgins, independence declared in February 1818 and confirmed at Maipu that April, and then the use of Chilean revenue and coastline to create the squadron that carried the expedition to Peru in 1820. What it reveals is that the southern campaign was the most deliberately planned operation in the topic - disciplined regular-army liberation projected from an external base - and that Chile was never the objective but the means.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the crossing as a display of courage comparable to a famous march. The crossing was costly and difficult, but its significance is strategic: it was the only way to obtain a coastline from which the royalist heartland could be attacked, and an answer that praises the feat without explaining the purpose has described the least analytical part of the campaign.

*The next lesson brings both campaigns to the same place. Peru was the seat of the viceroyalty, the base of the royalist army and the territory whose creole elite had the least enthusiasm for independence, because it had the most to lose. What happened when the southern expedition arrived, what the two liberators decided when they met, and how the war was finally ended are the subjects of the final military lesson in the Spanish American sequence.*

### Visual: two liberators, one bottleneck

```text
   WHY PERU WAS THE HARDEST CASE

   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | PERU IS NOT A COLONY LIKE THE OTHERS. IT IS THE SEAT OF        |

   | ARCHBISHOPRIC AND THE VETERAN ARMY. ITS CREOLE ELITE IS        |
   | RICH, TITLED, CLOSE TO THE ADMINISTRATION - AND SURROUNDED     |
   | BY A LARGE INDIGENOUS POPULATION IT REMEMBERS RISING IN        |
   | 1780. IT HAS LESS TO GAIN AND MORE TO FEAR THAN ANY OTHER      |
   | AMERICAN ELITE. IT DOES NOT WANT TO BE LIBERATED.              |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                                |
        SOUTH ARRIVES                            NORTH ARRIVES
        1820 by sea                              1823-24 by land

        negotiate, wait for Lima to              powers, reorganise, raise
        declare; ✅ independence                 an army, march INTO THE
        proclaimed 1821; Protector               HIGHLANDS and destroy the
             |                                   royalist field army
        DEADLOCK: the coast is held,                  |
        the highlands are not; the                    |
        royalist army is intact                       |
             |                                        |
             +---------------- GUAYAQUIL -------------+
                          26-27 JULY 1822
              two liberators meet; content not recorded
              publicly; SAN MARTIN WITHDRAWS FROM AMERICA
                                |
                     JUNIN, 6 AUGUST 1824 (Bolivar)
                     AYACUCHO, 9 DECEMBER 1824 (Sucre)
                     ✅ MAJOR ROYALIST POWER BROKEN
                                |
                     ✅ BOLIVIA EMERGES, 1825
                     mainland Spanish rule effectively ends
```

*The diagram identifies the single feature that makes Peru the exception: this was the one major territory where the local elite's interest lay with the empire. Everywhere else the patriot cause had a domestic constituency waiting for an opportunity. In Peru it had to be imported - first by sea from the south, then by land from the north - and it had to be imposed on a society whose wealthiest inhabitants preferred the arrangement it replaced.*

### 1. The viceroyalty that did not want to be liberated

Ask why the last royalist stronghold in South America was also its oldest and richest centre, and the answer is not military. It is the argument of Lesson 2 applied to one place with unusual force.

The Peruvian creole elite had more to lose from disorder than any other in the Americas: proximity to the viceregal government, access to office and honours that elites elsewhere were denied, a mining economy dependent on a labour system, and a very large Indigenous population whose great rising of the 1780s was within living memory. For that elite, the crown was not an obstacle; it was the guarantor of an order. Independence, especially independence achieved by armies raised elsewhere and promising freedom to enslaved recruits, looked less like an opportunity than like the beginning of the thing their grandparents had survived.

So the southern expedition arrived in 1820 to liberate a country that did not want to be liberated, and discovered that a strategy of landing, blockading and waiting for the capital to declare itself could take the coast and could not take the war. That deadlock is the context of the most discussed private conversation in the history of the region.

### 2. Ayacucho, Bolivia, and a hard date control

✅ **Fact:** **Ayacucho** in **1824** **breaks major royalist power in South America**, with the recorded significance that **the Spanish military position collapses**.

✅ **Fact:** **Bolivia emerges as an independent state** in **1825**, recorded as the point at which **Bolivar's northern campaign reaches symbolic completion**.

✅ **Fact:** **Peru** is central to San Martin's role, and ✅ **Peru and Bolivia are tied to Bolivar's campaigns** - both statements are recorded, and they are not in conflict; they describe two phases.

✅ **Fact:** one date control is absolute: ❌ **do not date Ayacucho other than 1824**, or **Bolivia's emergence other than 1825**.

✅ **Fact:** **Ayacucho, 1824, breaks major royalist power**, and it closes the southern sequence - while Peru and Bolivia are equally tied to Bolivar's campaigns. ⚠️ The disciplined formulation is therefore that Peru was **begun by the southern campaign and completed by the northern one**, which is exactly what the Guayaquil transfer accomplished.

### 3. From the landing of 1820 to Bolivia in 1825

✅ **Fact:** the sequence is as follows.

| Stage | ✅ Date | What it did |
|---|---|---|
| The southern expedition sails from Chile and lands on the Peruvian coast | **1820** | Brings the war to the viceroyalty by sea, as planned |
| Naval blockade; negotiations with the viceregal authorities; avoidance of a general engagement | 1820-21 | A strategy of pressure rather than assault, designed to induce Lima to declare |
| Royalist forces withdraw to the highlands; San Martin enters **Lima**; **independence of Peru proclaimed** | July **1821** | The coast is secured and a Peruvian state is declared |
| San Martin assumes the title of **Protector of Peru**; decrees include a **free-womb law** freeing children born to enslaved mothers, and the abolition of **Indigenous tribute** and of the labour draft | 1821 | A liberation government with a limited but real social programme |
| Deadlock: the royalist army remains intact in the highlands; the Peruvian elite does not rally | 1821-22 | Establishes that the southern method cannot finish the war here |
| **Meeting at Guayaquil** between San Martin and Bolivar | **26-27 July 1822** | The transfer point; ✅ its content was not made public |
| San Martin resigns his command and leaves Peru, then leaves America | 1822 | Removes the southern leadership from the theatre |
| Bolivar arrives in Peru and is granted extraordinary powers; the army is reorganised | 1823-24 | Supplies the political instrument the southern method lacked |
| **Battle of Junin** | **6 August 1824** | A cavalry action in the highlands opening the final campaign |
| **Battle of Ayacucho**, commanded by **Sucre** | **9 December 1824** | ✅ Breaks major royalist power; the viceroy is captured; capitulation follows |
| **Upper Peru** constituted as a separate republic, named for Bolivar | **1825** | ✅ Bolivia emerges as an independent state |
| Residual royalist garrisons surrender | 1826 | Mainland Spanish rule effectively ends |

⚠️ **Evidentiary discipline:** no force strength or casualty figure for Junin, Ayacucho or any other engagement is verified here, and none is given.

### 4. Guayaquil: what is known, and the discipline of not inventing the rest

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the episode on which candidates most often invent, and the invention is always detectable. State what is established and then state the candidate explanations as candidates.

✅ **Fact:** what is established is that the two men met at Guayaquil on 26-27 July 1822; that the port and province of Guayaquil were incorporated into Gran Colombia around that time; that San Martin left Peru shortly afterwards, resigned the protectorate and withdrew from America; and that no agreed public record of the conversation was issued by either man.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four explanations are standardly offered, and an answer should present them as competing rather than choose one confidently.

| Explanation | The case for it | The case against |
|---|---|---|
| **A disagreement over reinforcements** | San Martin needed troops that only Bolivar could supply, and did not get them on acceptable terms | It would explain resentment, but not a complete withdrawal from public life |
| **A disagreement over the future form of government** | San Martin is recorded as favouring constitutional monarchy for the new states; Bolivar was committed to republics | Their disagreement on this was known before the meeting, so it explains the atmosphere rather than the outcome |
| **A recognition that two commanders could not share one theatre** | Unified command was a military necessity, and one of them had to go | Persuasive, but it does not by itself explain why it was San Martin who went |
| **A deliberate withdrawal for the sake of the cause** | Consistent with his refusal to seek political power throughout the southern campaign | Depends on inferring motive from character, which the record available here cannot support |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the disciplined formulation, and the one to write, is that the meeting **resolved a command problem by removing one commander**, that its content is not established, and that the consequence is clear regardless of the cause: Peru was completed by the northern army under Bolivar and Sucre. ⚠️ Any answer that asserts what was said at Guayaquil is asserting something the record does not support.

### 5. Why the northern method finished what the southern could not

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the analytical payoff of the lesson, and it closes the comparison begun in Lesson 12.

| Requirement in Peru | Southern method | Northern method |
|---|---|---|
| A political authority able to tax, conscript and override local resistance | Declined by principle: liberate and hand over | Supplied: extraordinary powers assumed and used |
| Willingness to fight in the highlands rather than hold the coast | Avoided; the strategy was pressure and negotiation | Undertaken; Junin and Ayacucho were fought in the interior |
| An army that could be reinforced from outside Peru | Limited to what Chile and the River Plate could sustain | Available: the Gran Colombian army, fresh from Pichincha |
| Acceptance that the local elite would not rally | Strategy assumed it would | Strategy assumed it would not and proceeded anyway |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the lesson generalises, and stating the general form is what turns this into an argument. **Where a patriot cause had no domestic constituency, it could only be established by an external army with political powers.** That is true of Peru, and it is the reason Peru's independence was followed immediately by the instability of Lesson 18: a state created by an imported army, over the reservations of its own elite, begins with an unusually weak claim on the loyalty of the people who have to run it.

### 6. Bolivia, and the end of the mainland war

✅ **Fact:** **Upper Peru**, historically attached to the viceroyalty of Peru and later to the River Plate, was constituted after Ayacucho as a separate republic in **1825**, taking its name from Bolivar, with **Sucre** as its first effective head and a constitution drafted by Bolivar. ✅ **Bolivia emerges as an independent state** in **1825**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Bolivia is worth one sentence more than its size suggests, because it demonstrates the fragmentation mechanism at the moment of creation. Upper Peru could have joined Peru, could have returned to the River Plate, or could have stood alone; it stood alone because local elites preferred it, because the commanders on the spot accepted it, and because no authority strong enough to prevent it existed. ⚠️ That is exactly how the region's map was made - not by nationalities asserting themselves, but by colonial administrative units becoming states because nothing held them together.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Framing Peru as a country that "did not want to be liberated" is a caricature. There were Peruvian patriots, there had been conspiracies and risings in the viceroyalty before 1820, and the Peruvian declaration of 1821 was supported by a section of the Lima elite. Explaining Peru's late independence by elite reluctance understates both Peruvian patriot activity and the simple military fact that the royalist army there was the strongest in America.

**Reply:** The correction is fair and should be incorporated: Peruvian patriots existed, acted and suffered for it, and an answer that presents the whole society as royalist is wrong. But the explanation survives in a more precise form. The claim is not that no Peruvians wanted independence; it is that the *balance of elite interest* in the viceregal capital favoured the existing order more strongly than anywhere else, and the evidence for that is behavioural rather than rhetorical. The coast was taken in 1821 and the war did not end; a declaration of independence was issued and the royalist army was not deserted; the government of the country had to be assumed by an outsider with extraordinary powers before the highland campaign could be mounted; and the decisive battles were won by an army raised in Venezuela, New Granada and Quito. As for military strength, the objection is right that the royalist army was formidable - but that army was recruited and maintained substantially in Peru and Upper Peru, which returns the question to why so many Peruvians were available to it. The disciplined formulation is that Peru's independence was militarily hardest because it was socially least wanted by those with the means to decide.

**Residual:** The content of the Guayaquil meeting is not established and is not reconstructed here. Nor can the record available here establish the extent of Peruvian patriot support, the composition of the royalist forces in Peru, or the degree to which San Martin's free-womb and tribute decrees were enforced before or after his departure. All four are recorded as open.

### UPSC application

Two uses. First, Peru is the decisive case against the reading of these wars as national liberations: it is where the local elite's preference for the empire is clearest, and one sentence on it prevents a whole paragraph of romanticism. Second, this lesson closes the Bolivar-San Martin comparison with evidence rather than with characterisation - the two methods met a problem that only one of them could solve, and the reason is institutional, not personal. For the chronology, four dates carry everything: ✅ 1820 expedition, ✅ 1821 Lima and the protectorate, ✅ 1824 Ayacucho, ✅ 1825 Bolivia. The recorded date control on the last two should be treated as a hard constraint.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Asserting the content of the Guayaquil conversation; it is not established anywhere here.
- Dating Ayacucho or Bolivia to any year other than ✅ 1824 and ✅ 1825 - an explicit recorded prohibition.
- Attributing Ayacucho to Bolivar personally; ✅ it was commanded by Sucre.
- Treating Peru as simply the last territory to be reached, which misses that it was the hardest because of elite interest.
- Presenting Peru as uniformly royalist, which erases Peruvian patriots.
- Supplying casualty or force figures for Junin or Ayacucho; none is verified here.
- Treating Bolivia's separation as an expression of nationality rather than as the fragmentation mechanism operating at the moment of creation.
- Writing that San Martin was defeated in Peru; he took the coast, proclaimed independence and then withdrew from a war that was not yet won.
- Omitting the protectorate decrees of 1821, which are the southern campaign's only substantial social measures.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Ayacucho, 1824**, breaks major royalist power; the Spanish military position collapses. ✅ **Bolivia emerges, 1825**; the symbolic completion of Bolivar's campaign.
2. ✅ Recorded date control: never vary 1824 for Ayacucho or 1825 for Bolivia.
3. ⚠️ Peru is the exception because its elite's interest lay with the empire: proximity to office, a mining economy, and the memory of the Andean rising of the 1780s.
4. ✅ Expedition lands **1820**; blockade and negotiation; royalists withdraw to the highlands; **Lima entered and Peruvian independence proclaimed July 1821**; San Martin becomes **Protector**. ✅ Protectorate decrees: a **free-womb law**, abolition of **Indigenous tribute** and of the labour draft.
5. ✅ **Guayaquil, 26-27 July 1822**; content not made public; San Martin resigns and withdraws from America.
6. ⚠️ Four candidate explanations of Guayaquil: reinforcements; monarchy against republic; unified command; deliberate withdrawal. Present as candidates; assert none.
7. ✅ Bolivar granted extraordinary powers in Peru 1823-24; **Junin, 6 August 1824**; **Ayacucho, 9 December 1824**, commanded by **Sucre**; the viceroy captured.
8. ✅ **Upper Peru becomes Bolivia in 1825**, named for Bolivar, with Sucre as first effective head; residual garrisons surrender by 1826.
9. ⚠️ Four requirements Peru imposed, met by the northern method and not the southern: political authority, highland campaigning, external reinforcement, and acceptance that the elite would not rally.
10. ⚠️ General proposition: where a patriot cause had no domestic constituency, it could only be established by an external army with political powers - with predictable consequences for later legitimacy. ⚠️ Bolivia shows the fragmentation mechanism at the moment of creation: colonial administrative units became states because nothing held them together.
11. ⚠️ No force or casualty figure is held for any engagement in this campaign.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why Peru was the last major territory to be freed from Spanish rule, and what its case establishes about the character of the Spanish American wars.

**Model answer:** Peru was last because it was the one major territory whose dominant interests preferred the empire. It was the seat of the viceroyalty, the treasury, the high court and the archbishopric, so its creole elite enjoyed an access to office and honours denied elsewhere; its economy rested on mining and a labour system; and its wealthiest families lived among a large Indigenous population whose great rising of the 1780s was within living memory. Independence promised that elite little and threatened it with disorder. The consequences are visible in the sequence. The southern expedition landed in 1820, blockaded the coast, avoided a general engagement and entered Lima in July 1821, where independence was proclaimed and San Martin became Protector, decreeing a free-womb law and the abolition of tribute and the labour draft. The war did not end: the royalist army withdrew intact to the highlands and the Peruvian elite did not rally, producing a deadlock that a strategy of liberating and handing over could not break. After the Guayaquil meeting of 26-27 July 1822, whose content was never made public, San Martin withdrew and Bolivar took over with extraordinary powers, reorganised the army and carried the war into the interior, winning at Junin on 6 August 1824 and, under Sucre, at Ayacucho on 9 December 1824, which broke major royalist power; Upper Peru became Bolivia in 1825. What the case establishes is that these were not straightforward national liberations. Where independence had no domestic constituency it had to be imported by an external army exercising political powers, and the royalist forces that made Peru so hard to take were substantially Peruvian - which is the clearest single demonstration that the wars were civil conflicts over the internal order as much as struggles against an external ruler.

**Misconception to avoid:** Filling in what was said at Guayaquil. No record of the conversation was issued, four incompatible explanations circulate, and a confident account of it is a fabrication that an examiner can detect. State the establishable facts - the meeting, the date, San Martin's withdrawal, the transfer of the theatre - and present the explanations as competing.

*The next lesson leaves the Spanish empire entirely. While fifteen years of war were consuming Spanish America, Portuguese America took the same trigger and produced the opposite result: no junta crisis, no war of liberation, no republic, and no emancipation. The reason is a single structural difference, and the comparison it enables is the cleanest in the topic.*

## Lesson 14 - Brazil: the court that crossed the Atlantic, and the separation that needed no war

Progress: 14/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the transfer of the Portuguese court in 1807-08; the opening of the ports; the raising of Brazil to a kingdom; the Porto revolt and the recolonisation demand; the Fico and the Ipiranga declaration of 1822; Pedro I, the constitution of 1824, recognition in 1825 and abdication in 1831; slavery retained; why the Brazilian path diverged
