Book context: queried - the 1808 transfer of the court, independence in 1822 under Pedro I, and the persistence of slavery long afterwards
CA search: not sought - the one current linkage is held back for Lesson 18
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the same shock, the opposite mechanism

```text
   1807-08: THE FRENCH ADVANCE ON THE IBERIAN PENINSULA

   SPAIN                                 PORTUGAL
   -----                                 --------
   the royal family is TAKEN to          the royal family SAILS, under
   Bayonne; abdications extracted        British naval escort, from
        |                                Lisbon to RIO DE JANEIRO
   NO LEGITIMATE AUTHORITY                    |
   ANYWHERE IN THE EMPIRE                THE SOVEREIGN IS PHYSICALLY
        |                                PRESENT IN AMERICA
   juntas; refusal; 15 years of               |
   war; republics; caste abolished       ports OPENED to friendly
   in law; slavery ended piecemeal       nations, 1808
        |                                     |
        |                                Brazil RAISED to the status of
        |                                a KINGDOM, 1815 - legally equal
        |                                to Portugal within one monarchy
        |                                     |
        |                                1820-21: revolt in Porto; the
        |                                Cortes demands the king's return
        |                                AND the reduction of Brazil to
        |                                colonial subordination
        |                                     |
        |                                THE BRAZILIAN ELITE NOW FACES
        |                                THE SPANISH-AMERICAN PROBLEM -
        |                                BUT WITH A PRINCE ON THE SPOT
        |                                     |
        |                                1822: the heir REFUSES to leave
        |                                ("I stay"), then declares
        |                                independence and is crowned
        |                                EMPEROR PEDRO I
        v                                     v
   REPUBLICS AFTER WAR               A MONARCHY AFTER A DECLARATION
   caste abolished in law            ✅ SLAVERY PERSISTS LONG AFTER
   slavery ended piecemeal              INDEPENDENCE - to 1888
```

*The diagram isolates the variable with unusual precision. The trigger was identical and simultaneous; the political outcome was opposite; and the difference is a single structural fact - whether the legitimate sovereign was absent or present. That is why Brazil is the best natural experiment in this topic, and why an answer that uses it correctly can demonstrate causal reasoning rather than assertion.*

### 1. A monarchy that put to sea

In late 1807, with French forces advancing on Lisbon, the Portuguese monarchy made a decision no European dynasty had made before: instead of surrendering, abdicating or fighting, it embarked and sailed for its largest colony.

Think about what that does. Every constitutional question that tore Spanish America apart simply does not arise. Who governs in the king's absence? The king is not absent; he is in Rio de Janeiro. Are the American territories colonies or kingdoms? They are where the court is, and from 1815 Brazil is formally a kingdom. Should a junta be formed? There is no vacancy to fill.

The consequences ran further. A monarchy resident in Brazil could not keep Brazilian ports closed to the world - it had no metropolis to protect - so the ports were opened almost immediately. Institutions of government, courts, a bank, a press and a military establishment followed the court. By 1820 Brazil had spent twelve years as the centre of an empire rather than its periphery.

And then Portugal asked for it all back. That is the demand that produced Brazilian independence - not a colonial grievance accumulating toward revolution, but a colonial elite refusing a demotion it had already escaped.

### 2. Brazil in the record: 1822, Pedro I and a prohibition

✅ **Fact:** in **1808** the **Portuguese court shifts to Brazil**, contributing to the **Iberian imperial crisis** that **opens the door to colonial autonomy**.

✅ **Fact:** **Brazil becomes independent in 1822 under Pedro I**, with the recorded significance that **Portuguese America follows a different path**.

✅ **Fact:** the distinctive feature of the Brazilian path is that it was **monarchical and comparatively non-violent**, and that **slavery persisted long after independence**.

✅ **Fact:** one prohibition is explicit: ❌ **do not say Brazil followed the Spanish-American route**; ✅ **it became independent in 1822 under Pedro I through a monarchical path**. ❌ And it is an error to claim that **Brazil followed the same route as Spanish America**; ✅ **Brazil's path was more monarchical and less conventionally revolutionary**.

✅ **Fact:** a three-line summary should be reproduced in comparisons: **Haiti = anti-colonial + anti-slavery social revolution; mainland Spanish America = anti-colonial, often creole-led political revolution; Brazil = monarchical separation from Portugal.**

✅ **Fact:** these states gained independence from Spain - **in the case of Brazil, from Portugal** - in the early nineteenth century, and that **Portuguese is the main language** there.

### 3. From Lisbon in 1807 to abolition in 1888

✅ **Fact:** the sequence is as follows.

| Stage | ✅ Date | What it did |
|---|---|---|
| The Portuguese court leaves Lisbon under British naval escort and sails for Brazil | late **1807** | Avoids the constitutional vacuum that destroyed Spanish authority |
| The court arrives; **Rio de Janeiro** becomes the seat of the monarchy; **ports opened to friendly nations** | **1808** | Ends the commercial monopoly at a stroke; the central Spanish American grievance simply disappears in Brazil |
| Institutions established: courts, a treasury, a bank, a military academy, a printing press, a botanical garden | 1808 onward | Converts a colony into a capital |
| **Brazil raised to the status of a kingdom**, united with Portugal and the Algarves | **1815** | Removes colonial status in law - the concession Cadiz refused to Spanish America |
| Death of the queen; **Joao VI** reigns | 1816 | Continuity of the American-seated monarchy |
| Liberal revolt at **Porto**; a Portuguese Cortes convenes and demands the king's return and the restoration of Brazil's subordinate commercial and administrative status | **1820-21** | The recolonisation demand; the trigger of Brazilian independence |
| **Joao VI returns to Portugal**, leaving his son **Pedro** as regent | **1821** | Leaves a legitimate prince on American soil |
| Pedro refuses the Cortes's order to return - the episode known as the **Fico**, "I stay" | January **1822** | The decisive act; the heir aligns with the Brazilian elite |
| Declaration of independence, conventionally dated to the **cry of Ipiranga** | **7 September 1822** | ✅ Brazil independent under Pedro I |
| Pedro acclaimed and crowned **Emperor Pedro I** | December **1822** | ✅ A monarchical path |
| Constituent assembly dissolved; a constitution granted by the emperor, with a **moderating power** reserved to the crown | **1824** | Centralised imperial constitution; provincial revolt in the north-east suppressed |
| **Portugal recognises Brazilian independence**, under British mediation and with financial compensation | **1825** | Separation completed by treaty rather than by conquest |
| **Pedro I abdicates** in favour of his infant son and returns to Portugal | **1831** | Ends the first reign; a regency follows |
| **Slavery abolished in Brazil** | **1888** | ✅ Slavery persisted long after independence - by more than six decades |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two entries deserve particular attention. The **1815** elevation to kingdom is the concession that Spanish America asked for at Cadiz and did not get; Brazil received it without fighting, which is one reason it had no war. And the **1888** abolition is the single most economical fact for a comparison question, because it shows that the least violent independence in the region produced the longest survival of the most extreme form of the colonial social order.

⚠️ **Evidentiary discipline:** the description of Brazilian independence as **comparatively non-violent** is retained here strictly in that comparative form. ⚠️ There was armed conflict in several provinces, and the suppression of provincial opposition after 1822 was not bloodless; no casualty figure for any of it is asserted here.

### 4. Why the Brazilian path diverged: five mechanisms

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** stating the divergence is easy; explaining it is what earns marks. Five mechanisms, each of which can be checked against the Spanish case.

| Mechanism | In Brazil | In Spanish America |
|---|---|---|
| **Presence of legitimate authority** | The sovereign was in America; no vacancy, no junta question | The king was a prisoner; the vacancy was the whole crisis |
| **Early removal of the commercial grievance** | ✅ Ports opened in 1808 | Free trade with foreign nations refused at Cadiz |
| **Early removal of colonial status** | ✅ Kingdom in 1815 | Conceded in words at Cadiz and withheld in arithmetic |
| **An available domestic head of state** | The heir was present and chose Brazil | No equivalent; authority had to be constructed from congresses and commanders |
| **Elite fear of social upheaval** | Acute: a large enslaved population made any mobilisation dangerous, so the elite preferred a monarchy that guaranteed order | Equally present, but there was no monarch available to guarantee it, so republics were built instead |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the last row is the most important and the least often stated. The Brazilian elite's preference for monarchy was not sentiment about kings; it was a judgement about security. A slaveholding society facing a separation crisis chose the political form least likely to mobilise anyone, and it got exactly what it chose - continuity of the social order, including slavery, for a further two generations. The same calculation in Spanish America produced caution within republics; in Brazil it produced a monarchy.

### 5. What Brazil's path cost and what it saved

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a comparative answer should say both, because the case cuts in two directions.

| Saved | Cost |
|---|---|
| Fifteen years of destructive war, with its economic devastation | ✅ Slavery survived until 1888 - the longest in the hemisphere among the major states |
| The immediate fragmentation that followed Spanish American independence: Brazil remained a single state | A centralised imperial constitution granted rather than negotiated, with provincial revolt suppressed |
| The immediate collapse of administration: institutions established since 1808 continued | A political order whose stability depended on an unelected moderating power |
| Rapid international recognition, ✅ completed by treaty in 1825 | Recognition purchased with financial compensation and under British commercial influence |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sentence to carry is that **Brazil bought continuity, and paid for it in the currency of the people it kept enslaved**. That is a strong claim and it is defensible on the recorded evidence: ✅ the path was monarchical and comparatively non-violent, and ✅ slavery persisted long after independence.

### 6. The three-line comparison, and how to use it

✅ **Fact:** that three-line summary is the best available comparative device in the topic:

```text
Haiti                  = anti-colonial + anti-slavery social revolution
Mainland Spanish America = anti-colonial, often creole-led political revolution
Brazil                 = monarchical separation from Portugal
```

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** deploy it as the hinge of a comparative paragraph, not as decoration. Read downward, it ranks three cases by social depth; read across, it shows that the same imperial crisis produced three different relationships between political and social change. Adding Mexico's reversal to it - independence achieved as a conservative settlement - gives four cases and covers the whole region without a list.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Describing Brazilian independence as non-violent and consensual understates the conflict. There was fighting in Bahia and other provinces; the constituent assembly was dissolved by force in 1823; a major provincial revolt in the north-east was suppressed in 1824; and the regency period after 1831 saw serious regional rebellions. The contrast with Spanish America is one of degree, and presenting Brazil as a peaceful separation flatters the imperial state.

**Reply:** The objection is correct on the facts and the session states them: there was armed conflict, the assembly was dissolved, and provincial revolt was suppressed. The description used here is ✅ **comparatively** non-violent - and the comparative qualifier is doing real work rather than softening a claim. The contrast is a matter of degree, but the degree is very large: Spanish America fought for fifteen years across a continent, lost its administrative continuity and fragmented into many states, while Brazil separated within about eighteen months of the recolonisation demand, retained its institutions and remained a single state. And the objection strengthens rather than weakens the lesson's main argument, because the violence that did occur in Brazil was directed at provincial and constitutional opposition rather than at an imperial army - which is precisely the profile of a separation managed from above. The disciplined formulation is that Brazil's was a comparatively non-violent separation that nonetheless required force to impose a centralised imperial settlement on its own provinces.

**Residual:** Nothing is established here on the scale of provincial conflict, on the internal politics of the Brazilian elite in 1821-22, or on the terms of the 1825 compensation, and no figure for any of them is given. Nor does it establish why the Brazilian monarchy proved durable where Iturbide's did not; the comparison is noted in the register notes as an open question.

### UPSC application

Brazil is the best natural experiment available in this part of the syllabus, and a comparative answer should use it as such rather than as a paragraph of additional description. The construction is three sentences: the same trigger produced opposite mechanisms because the Portuguese sovereign was present and the Spanish sovereign was not; ✅ Brazil received in 1808 and 1815 the commercial opening and the end of colonial status that Spanish America was refused; and the result was a monarchical separation in 1822 under Pedro I in which ✅ slavery persisted long after independence. Used inside an answer on whether independence was revolutionary, the last clause is decisive: the least revolutionary path preserved the most extreme feature of the colonial order for the longest time.

### Brazil: the standing errors

- Saying Brazil followed the Spanish American route - an explicitly recorded prohibition.
- Dating Brazilian independence to any year other than ✅ 1822, or attributing it to anyone other than ✅ Pedro I.
- Omitting the transfer of the court in 1807-08, which makes the entire divergence inexplicable.
- Omitting the 1815 elevation to kingdom, which is the concession Cadiz refused.
- Forgetting the Porto revolt and the recolonisation demand, which is the actual trigger of 1822.
- Presenting the separation as entirely peaceful rather than ✅ comparatively non-violent.
- Omitting ✅ the persistence of slavery, which is the single most valuable comparative fact in the lesson.
- Treating the opening of the ports in 1808 as a minor administrative step, when it removed at a stroke the commercial grievance that Spanish America was still fighting over in 1820.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **1808**: the Portuguese court shifts to Brazil; part of the Iberian crisis opening the door to colonial autonomy.
2. ✅ **1822**: Brazil becomes independent under **Pedro I**; Portuguese America follows a different path.
3. ✅ Distinctive feature: monarchical and comparatively non-violent; ✅ slavery persisted long after independence.
4. ✅ Recorded prohibition: Brazil did not follow the Spanish American route; its path was more monarchical and less conventionally revolutionary.
5. ✅ Court sails late 1807 under British escort; arrives 1808; **ports opened to friendly nations**; institutions established at Rio.
6. ✅ **1815**: Brazil raised to the status of a kingdom united with Portugal - the concession Cadiz refused to Spanish America.
7. ✅ **1820-21**: Porto revolt; the Cortes demands the king's return and Brazil's recolonisation; **Joao VI returns in 1821** leaving **Pedro** as regent.
8. ✅ **January 1822**: the **Fico** - Pedro refuses to leave; **7 September 1822**: declaration at Ipiranga; **December 1822**: crowned Emperor Pedro I.
9. ✅ **1824** constitution granted with a **moderating power**; constituent assembly dissolved; north-eastern provincial revolt suppressed.
10. ✅ **1825**: Portuguese recognition under British mediation with compensation; **1831**: Pedro I abdicates; **1888**: slavery abolished.
11. ⚠️ Five divergence mechanisms: sovereign present; commercial grievance removed in 1808; colonial status removed in 1815; a domestic head of state available; elite fear of upheaval in a slaveholding society answered by monarchy.
12. ⚠️ What it saved and what it cost: no fifteen-year war, no fragmentation, institutional continuity, rapid recognition - against slavery to 1888, a granted constitution, a moderating power and provincial suppression.
13. ✅ The three-line comparison: Haiti = anti-colonial and anti-slavery social revolution; mainland Spanish America = anti-colonial, often creole-led political revolution; Brazil = monarchical separation from Portugal.
14. ⚠️ The carrying sentence: Brazil bought continuity and paid for it in the currency of the people it kept enslaved.

### Concept check

**Question:** The same European crisis struck the Spanish and Portuguese empires in the same years. Explain why it produced fifteen years of war and a set of republics in one and a monarchical separation without a comparable war in the other.

**Model answer:** The difference is a single structural fact with a long train of consequences: whether the legitimate sovereign was absent or present. In Spain the royal family was removed to Bayonne and abdications were extracted, so every official in the empire held a commission from a vacated authority, and the resulting constitutional question - in whose name does anyone govern? - produced juntas, Spanish refusal, war and, after the restoration of 1814 annulled the Cadiz settlement, independence. In Portugal the court sailed from Lisbon under British escort in late 1807 and reached Rio de Janeiro in 1808, so there was no vacancy, no junta question and no crisis of legitimacy. The consequences compounded. A monarchy resident in Brazil had no metropolis to protect and opened the ports to friendly nations in 1808, removing at a stroke the commercial grievance that Cadiz refused Spanish America; in 1815 Brazil was raised to the status of a kingdom, granting in law the end of colonial status that Spanish America was denied in arithmetic. Independence came only when Portugal tried to reverse all of this: the Porto revolt of 1820-21 produced a Cortes demanding the king's return and Brazil's recolonisation, Joao VI returned in 1821 leaving his son as regent, Pedro refused the order to follow in January 1822, declared independence in September and was crowned emperor in December. Because a legitimate prince was available to lead the separation, the Brazilian elite - acutely conscious that mobilisation in a slaveholding society was dangerous - could obtain independence without arming anyone. The outcome matched the method: a single state, a granted constitution with a moderating power, recognition by treaty in 1825, and slavery retained until 1888.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that Brazil's peaceful path shows that independence could be achieved without cost. The cost was paid, but not by the people who chose the method: the least violent separation in the region preserved slavery for more than six decades after independence, which is the longest survival of the colonial social order among the major states.

*The next lesson steps back from the five theatres and asks a question none of them can answer alone. Fifteen years of war on a continental scale required soldiers, money, supplies and local cooperation, and the people who supplied them were overwhelmingly not the creole notables whose names appear on the declarations. Who actually fought, on which side, and what were they promised?*

## Lesson 15 - Who actually fought, and what each side had to promise to get them

Progress: 15/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the recruitment market; creoles as leadership and finance; Indigenous communities and royal protection; enslaved and free coloured participation on both sides; women in the wars; the divided church; merchants and credit; foreign volunteers; why royalist America was large

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the group-by-group social table, and the surviving authority of church and army
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage sits in Lesson 18
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a market in allegiance

```text
   THE WAR AS A BIDDING PROCESS
   Both sides need the same populations. Neither can win without them.
   Each can offer different things - and what each CAN offer is
   determined by what it is trying to protect.

                      WHAT THE CROWN COULD OFFER
   +-----------------------------------------------------------------+
   | to INDIGENOUS COMMUNITIES  protection of community land and of   |
   |                            separate legal jurisdiction against   |
   |                            creole encroachment; a familiar       |
   |                            protector with a long record          |
   | to ENSLAVED MEN            freedom in return for royal service   |
   | to FREE PEOPLE OF COLOUR   militia rank, legal privileges, and   |
   |                            distance from a creole elite that had |
   |                            blocked their advancement             |
   | to the CHURCH HIERARCHY    establishment, privilege, order       |
   +-----------------------------------------------------------------+

                    WHAT THE PATRIOT SIDE COULD OFFER
   +-----------------------------------------------------------------+
   | to ENSLAVED MEN            freedom in return for military service|
   |                            (and, from 1816 in the north, decreed |
   |                            emancipation)                         |
   | to MIXED-DESCENT MEN       abolition of caste distinctions,      |
   |                            officer rank, land, plunder           |
   | to INDIGENOUS COMMUNITIES  abolition of tribute and labour draft |
   |                            - but NO guarantee on community land, |
   |                            and liberal property doctrine pointing|
   |                            the other way                         |
   | to PARISH CLERGY           a share in a national project         |
   | to MERCHANTS               open trade                            |
   +-----------------------------------------------------------------+

   ===================================================================
   THE ASYMMETRY THAT DECIDED ALLEGIANCE IN MANY REGIONS:
   the crown could promise PROTECTION OF WHAT EXISTED.
   the patriots could promise REMOVAL OF WHAT OPPRESSED.
   Which is more attractive depends on whether you have something
   to protect - which is why INDIGENOUS COMMUNITIES WITH LAND
   often chose the king, and LANDLESS MIXED-DESCENT AND ENSLAVED
   MEN often chose whoever offered freedom and rank FIRST.
   ===================================================================
```

*The diagram converts a moral question - why did the oppressed not all support liberation? - into an analytical one. Allegiance followed from what each side could credibly offer to a specific group in a specific region, and the offers were constrained by what each side existed to defend. Once that is understood, royalist America stops being a puzzle and becomes evidence.*

### 1. Who actually carried the muskets

Fifteen years of war across a continent cannot be fought by a few hundred notables. Somebody had to carry the muskets, drive the cattle, build the roads, nurse the wounded, lend the money and grow the food. Very few of those people were the men who signed the declarations.

The uncomfortable fact that follows is the one this lesson exists to handle. Large numbers of the people at the bottom of the colonial hierarchy fought for the king, and they had reasons. The crown was distant and the local landowner was not. The crown's law recognised Indigenous communities and protected their land because it taxed them; a republic of property-owners promised equality and had no reason to maintain either the protection or the separate jurisdiction. The crown could offer a free coloured artisan a militia commission; the creole elite of his own city had spent a century making sure he could not enter its guilds or its university.

Once this is said plainly, two things become visible. First, the wars were civil wars. Second, and more importantly for the topic's central question, the patriot side's inability to outbid the crown in many regions was not a failure of imagination - it was a consequence of what the patriot side was defending. You cannot promise to protect community land and simultaneously promise your financiers a free market in property.

### 2. The social table, group by group

✅ **Fact:** the position and role of each group can be set out exactly.

| Group | ✅ Position | ✅/⚠️ Role in independence | ✅/⚠️ What they gained |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Peninsulares** | ✅ Europeans born in Spain or Portugal held the top colonial offices | The target of creole resentment | Displaced |
| **Creoles** | ✅ American-born whites: wealthy but politically restricted | Supplied leadership, finance and officers | ✅ Became the ruling class; power remained with them |
| **Mestizos** | ✅ Socially and politically subordinate | Supplied much of the fighting strength | ⚠️ Formal legal change without social ascent |
| **Indigenous peoples** | ✅ Subordinate | Mobilised in Mexico's popular phase under Hidalgo | ⚠️ Land pressure often continued or worsened |
| **Enslaved Africans** | ✅ Subordinate | Decisive only in Haiti | ✅ Emancipation was immediate only in Haiti; elsewhere it came piecemeal and later |

✅ **Fact:** the analytical use of this table is direct: it alone answers the question why independence did not produce equality, because **the group that led the movement was the group that benefited from the existing social order**.

✅ **Fact:** **a mass base existed, but the benefits of independence were uneven**.

✅ **Fact:** **church-army influence** is a recorded reason why unity proved difficult: **old authority structures remained powerful** and **liberal constitutions faced resistance**.

### 3. Group by group: position, role and reward

**Creoles.** ✅ They supplied leadership, finance and officers. ⚠️ Their contribution was decisive in three respects that are easy to underrate: they had the credit networks to borrow, the literacy and legal training to construct governments, and the militia commissions created by the eighteenth-century military reforms, which gave them a trained officer class. ⚠️ Their limitation was equally structural: they could not finance a war and simultaneously promise to redistribute the assets that secured the loans.

**Mestizos and other mixed-descent populations.** ✅ They supplied much of the fighting strength. ✅ They served on both sides in very large numbers, and in Venezuela the decisive royalist force of 1814 was the llanero cavalry. ⚠️ What determined their allegiance was usually what was offered locally - rank, land, plunder, the abolition of caste restriction - rather than any regional programme. ⚠️ Their recorded gain was formal legal change without social ascent, which is the most precise summary available of what caste abolition delivered.

**Indigenous communities.** ✅ They were mobilised in Mexico's popular phase under Hidalgo. ✅ Elsewhere they divided sharply by region: substantial Indigenous support for the royalist cause is recorded in the Peruvian and Upper Peruvian highlands, while Indigenous participation on the patriot side is recorded in other regions and in the Upper Peruvian guerrilla war. ⚠️ The general consideration is the one stated in the visual: the crown's protection of community land and of separate jurisdiction was a concrete benefit that a liberal republic of individual property-holders could not match. ✅ The recorded outcome is that land pressure often continued or worsened.

**Enslaved and free people of African descent.** ✅ Enslaved Africans were decisive only in Haiti. ✅ On the mainland both sides recruited enslaved men with the promise of freedom for military service - the crown from early in the wars, the patriots systematically from 1816 in the north after the Haitian condition, and in the south through the enslaved recruits who formed a substantial part of the Army of the Andes. ✅ Free people of colour, legally free and legally restricted, served extensively on both sides and were among the most consistent royalist constituencies in parts of the Caribbean coast. ⚠️ The decisive point for Lesson 17 is that freedom obtained by service is conditional freedom, and its scale cannot be established from the record available here.

**Women.** ✅ Women participated across the entire range of wartime activity: as organisers of conspiracies and safe houses, as couriers and intelligence agents, as suppliers and financiers, as camp followers who sustained armies logistically, and in some recorded cases as combatants and commanders. Several were executed by royalist authorities for conspiracy and are among the founding figures of their national histories. ⚠️ Two disciplines apply. First, no figure of any kind is held here for women's participation, and none is given. Second, and more analytically, independence produced no change in women's legal or political status anywhere in the region in this period; the wars expanded what women did and not what they were permitted to be, and that gap is itself part of the topic's central finding about political change without social transformation.

**The church.** ✅ Church influence stood among the old authority structures that remained powerful and that liberal constitutions had to contend with. ✅ The institution divided by rank rather than acting as a bloc: the hierarchy was overwhelmingly royalist and in places excommunicated insurgents, while parish clergy were prominent among insurgent leaders - ✅ the first two leaders of the Mexican insurgency were both priests. ⚠️ That split matters because it shows that the conflict ran through institutions rather than between them, which is the defining feature of a civil war.

**Merchants and credit.** ✅ The commercial grievance was a principal creole grievance. ✅ Merchant houses of the old monopoly ports were generally royalist, since their position depended on the imperial system, while merchants oriented toward open trade and British connections favoured the patriot side; and patriot governments financed themselves increasingly through loans raised in London. ⚠️ This is the point at which the war and Lesson 19 connect: the debts contracted to win independence were among the first burdens of the states it created.

**Foreign volunteers.** ✅ Foreign volunteers, principally British and Irish veterans of the European wars, served in the northern campaigns in the formations known collectively as the British Legion, and foreign naval officers commanded the Chilean and Peruvian squadrons. ⚠️ Their significance is less numerical than technical: they supplied trained infantry, artillery and naval command at moments when those were unavailable locally.

### 4. Why royalist America was large: three reasons, stated without apology

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** an answer that cannot explain royalism cannot explain the wars, and three reasons cover the evidence.

1. **Interest.** For groups whose position depended on royal protection - Indigenous communities with land and legal standing, free coloured militiamen with privileges, the church hierarchy, monopoly merchants - the empire was not an oppressor but a guarantor.
2. **Legitimacy.** ⚠️ As Lesson 6 established, the royalist position was a constitutional position: the Regency and later the Cortes claimed to be the lawful government of the monarchy, and to obey them was not treason but legality. Many Americans held that view sincerely.
3. **Fear.** ⚠️ In societies stratified by caste and containing large enslaved populations, a war fought with promises of freedom and equality frightened everyone with something to lose - including people far below the creole elite who nonetheless had a place in the existing order.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and a fourth consideration, which is not a reason but a corrective: allegiance was frequently **local, pragmatic and changeable**. Communities and commanders shifted sides as armies approached, as promises were made or broken, and as conscription and requisition made one side more burdensome than the other. ⚠️ Treating allegiance as a fixed ideological property of a social group is the most common error in this part of the topic, and this session avoids it deliberately.

### 5. The recruitment bargain, and the debt it created

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this section is the bridge to Lesson 17 and should be read as the topic's central mechanism stated in its sharpest form.

| Promise made during the war | Who made it | ⚠️ What happened to it |
|---|---|---|
| Freedom for military service | Both sides, across the region | Honoured for individuals who served and survived; it is not general emancipation, and its scale is not established here |
| Abolition of caste distinctions | Patriot governments, and ✅ the Plan of Iguala in Mexico | Delivered in law across the region; ⚠️ formal legal change without social ascent |
| Abolition of Indigenous tribute and labour drafts | ✅ Cadiz, ✅ Hidalgo, ✅ San Martin's protectorate and several republics | Repeatedly enacted and repeatedly reversed where fiscal need returned |
| Land and rank for soldiers | Patriot commanders, especially in the north | Honoured for commanders; ⚠️ the record for ordinary soldiers is not established here |
| Protection of community land | The crown, implicitly through the colonial legal order | Not inherited by the republics; ✅ land pressure often continued or worsened |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the pattern. **Promises that cost the leading coalition nothing were kept; promises that would have transferred assets were not.** That is the same finding as Lesson 9's reading of the Plan of Iguala and Lesson 7's reading of Cadiz, arrived at from a third direction - which is what makes it a finding rather than an impression.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This lesson comes close to arguing that the colonial order was popular. It was not. The groups described as choosing the crown were choosing between two bad options under coercion, conscription and requisition by whichever army was nearest, and reading their allegiance as a considered preference imports a freedom of choice they did not have.

**Reply:** The objection is an important corrective and its substance is accepted in section 4's fourth point: allegiance was frequently local, pragmatic and changeable, and coercion was pervasive on both sides. But the argument does not depend on preference in a free sense. It depends on a narrower and better-evidenced claim: that each side could offer only what its own purposes permitted, and that this constrained which populations it could attract. ✅ The crown could protect community land and separate jurisdiction because it taxed them; the patriots could not promise the same and also promise a free market in property to their financiers. ✅ The crown could offer militia rank to free coloured men; the creole elite had built the exclusions that made such rank valuable. That is a structural claim about the supply of offers, not a claim about the contentment of the governed. And it is testable: ✅ where the patriot side changed its offer - decreed emancipation after 1816, rank and land for llanero commanders - the allegiance of the relevant populations changed with it, which would not occur if allegiance were purely coerced.

**Residual:** No figure can be established here for the composition of any army, the number of enslaved recruits on either side, the proportion of Indigenous communities supporting either cause in any region, or the extent of women's participation. Every quantitative question in this lesson is open, and the session records recorded roles and recorded outcomes only.

### UPSC application

This is the lesson that converts a script about leaders into a script about society, which is exactly what the syllabus phrase "effect on the society" invites. Two constructions are worth memorising. For a question on why independence did not produce equality, use the five-row group table and its conclusion: the group that led the movement was the group that benefited from the existing order. For a question on the character of the wars, use royalism: state - as a qualified claim - that royalist forces in most theatres drew very largely on American manpower, give the llanero case and the Indigenous-protection argument, and conclude that these were civil wars over the internal order. Both constructions are short, both are evidenced, and both pre-empt the generic paragraph about heroic liberation that most scripts supply.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Describing the wars as colonisers against colonised, which cannot accommodate royalist America.
- Treating any group as a bloc with one regional allegiance: Indigenous communities divided sharply by region, and the church hierarchy and the parish clergy frequently took opposite sides.
- Omitting women entirely, or including them only as a decorative sentence rather than noting that their legal status did not change.
- Presenting freedom-for-service as general emancipation; it was conditional and its scale is not established.
- Supplying any figure for army composition, enslaved recruitment or participation; none is verified here.
- Explaining allegiance by ideology alone and ignoring coercion, requisition and local circumstance.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Creoles supplied leadership, finance and officers, and ✅ became the ruling class. ✅ Mestizos supplied much of the fighting strength and gained ⚠️ formal legal change without social ascent.
2. ✅ Indigenous peoples were mobilised in Mexico under Hidalgo; ✅ land pressure often continued or worsened; ⚠️ allegiance divided sharply by region.
3. ✅ Enslaved Africans were decisive only in Haiti; ✅ emancipation was immediate only there. ✅ Both sides recruited enslaved men with the promise of freedom for service; free people of colour served extensively on both sides.
4. ✅ Women organised conspiracies, carried intelligence, financed, supplied, sustained armies and in cases fought and commanded; several were executed for conspiracy; ⚠️ their legal and political status did not change with independence.
5. ✅ The church remained a powerful old-authority structure; ✅ the hierarchy was largely royalist while parish clergy led the Mexican insurgency. ✅ Monopoly-port merchants were generally royalist; open-trade merchants and London credit financed the patriots.
6. ✅ Foreign volunteers, principally British and Irish, served in the north; foreign officers commanded the Pacific squadrons.
7. ⚠️ Three reasons royalist America was large: interest, legitimacy and fear - plus the corrective that allegiance was local, pragmatic and changeable. ⚠️ The asymmetry of offers: the crown could promise protection of what existed; the patriots could promise removal of what oppressed.
8. ⚠️ The pattern of promises: those costing the leading coalition nothing were kept; those transferring assets were not.
9. ✅ The conclusion: the group that led the movement was the group that benefited from the existing social order. ⚠️ No figure for composition, recruitment or participation is held here.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why large numbers of Indigenous, mixed-descent and enslaved people fought for the crown, and show why this fact is essential rather than embarrassing for the analysis of Latin American independence.

**Model answer:** They fought for the crown because of what each side was able to offer them. The colonial state recognised Indigenous communities as a separate jurisdiction, protected their community land and enforced their obligations, and it did so because that arrangement produced tribute revenue and labour - so for a community with land to lose, the crown was a guarantor and a republic of individual property-holders led by the local landowners was a threat. Free people of colour could obtain militia rank and legal privileges from the crown that the creole elite of their own cities had spent a century denying them. Enslaved men were offered freedom for royal service from early in the wars. The church hierarchy and the monopoly merchants had corresponding institutional interests. Two further reasons operated: the royalist position was a constitutional one, since the Regency and the Cortes claimed to be the lawful government of the monarchy, so obedience was legality rather than treason; and in societies stratified by caste with large enslaved populations, a war fought with promises of liberty frightened everyone with a place to lose. Allegiance was also local, pragmatic and changeable under coercion and requisition. The fact is essential rather than embarrassing because it supplies the mechanism for the topic's central conclusion. The patriot side could not outbid the crown for these populations without attacking the property and the labour systems that its own financiers depended on. Its inability to do so was therefore not an oversight but a structural feature of what it was defending - and that is precisely why the movement that won transferred sovereignty to creole elites while leaving caste practice, landholding and, outside Haiti, slavery substantially intact.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that the subordinated groups misunderstood their own interests. They were choosing between a distant authority that protected specific things they possessed and a nearby elite that had built the restrictions they lived under. That is a rational calculation, and treating it as false consciousness removes the only evidence that explains why these wars were so long and so bitter.

*The next lesson turns outward. Two European powers and one American one had strong interests in the outcome, and one of them issued a statement in 1823 that has been misread ever since. What outside actors actually did - and, just as importantly, what they did not do - is the last piece of the explanatory picture before the outcomes are assessed.*

## Lesson 16 - What the outside world did: Britain, the United States, Monroe and the Holy Alliance

Progress: 16/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: British commercial and naval interest; recognition in the 1820s; the United States and early recognition; the Monroe message of 1823 and what it could and could not enforce; the Holy Alliance intervention that never sailed; Haiti's isolation; the boundary with the Vienna and Concert of Europe topic

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the trade grievance and the external examples; the diplomacy of recognition is reconstructed from standard chronology
CA search: not sought - no current anchor belongs on the diplomacy of the 1820s
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four outside actors, four different interests

```text
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   | BRITAIN - the decisive external actor                         |
   | WANTS: markets for industrial exports; an end to the Spanish  |
   |        commercial monopoly; no French or Spanish reconquest   |
   | HAS:   the world's dominant navy; capital; manufactured goods |
   | DOES:  supplies arms, credit, volunteers and shipping;        |
   |        blocks the prospect of armed European intervention;    |
   |        recognises the new states in the mid-1820s             |
   | RESULT: the monopoly is replaced by British preponderance     |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   | THE UNITED STATES                                             |
   | WANTS: no European recolonisation on its continent; trade     |
   | HAS:   a small navy; a large interest; no capacity to enforce |
   | DOES:  recognises the new states early; issues the MONROE     |
   |        MESSAGE of 2 December 1823                             |
   | RESULT: a doctrine whose enforcement in this period rested    |
   |         on BRITISH sea power, not on American arms            |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   | THE CONTINENTAL POWERS / HOLY ALLIANCE                        |
   | WANTS: legitimacy restored; revolution reversed; France       |
   |        restores Ferdinand VII in Spain in 1823                |
   | HAS:   armies, and no means of crossing the Atlantic against  |
   |        British objection                                      |
   | DOES:  discusses intervention in America; never launches it   |
   | RESULT: the threat shapes the diplomacy and produces the      |
   |         Monroe message -> the EUROPEAN story is owned by the  |
   |         VIENNA topic and is handed over here                  |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   | HAITI                                                         |
   | GIVES: asylum, arms and ships to Bolivar in 1816, on the      |
   |        condition of EMANCIPATION                              |
   | GETS:  diplomatic isolation; recognition from France only in  |
   |        1825, in exchange for an indemnity                     |
   | RESULT: the one external actor whose assistance carried a     |
   |         social condition - and the one penalised for it       |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
```

*The diagram corrects the single most persistent error on this part of the topic. The power that made European reconquest impracticable was Britain, acting on commercial interest with the world's dominant navy. The Monroe message stated a principle that the United States could not yet enforce. Both facts are true simultaneously, and an answer that gets their relationship right is doing diplomatic history rather than repeating a slogan.*

### 1. Could Europe take the colonies back?

By 1820 a question hung over the whole American settlement: could Spain, or Europe on Spain's behalf, get the colonies back? Spain alone could not - it had lost its fleets, its treasury and, after 1820, its internal cohesion. But Europe in these years was a system committed to reversing revolutions. A French army restored Ferdinand VII to absolute power in Spain itself in 1823. If it could be done in Madrid, why not in Mexico or Buenos Aires?

The answer is that America is across an ocean, and the ocean belonged to Britain. British interest was unambiguous: the Spanish American market had been closed by the imperial monopoly and was now open, British manufactures were flowing into it, British capital was lending to it, and no British government was going to permit its reclosure by a restored Spanish empire or by a French expedition.

That is the context in which the United States issued its famous message in December 1823. The message expressed a genuine American interest and stated a principle of enduring importance. It also, in this decade, described a situation that British sea power was already producing.

### 2. Britain: the decisive external actor

✅ **Fact:** British involvement took four forms and all four are examinable.

| Form | Content |
|---|---|
| **Commercial** | British merchants had traded with Spanish America in wartime and through contraband before independence; after the ports opened, British manufactures entered the region on a large scale |
| **Military and naval** | Surplus arms and equipment from the European wars were sold to patriot governments; British and Irish veterans served as volunteers in the northern campaigns; British naval officers commanded the Pacific squadrons |
| **Financial** | Loans were raised for the new states on the London market during the 1820s, followed by a speculative boom and widespread default - the beginning of the region's external debt history |
| **Diplomatic** | Britain resisted proposals for European intervention in America and extended commercial and then diplomatic recognition to the new states in the mid-1820s |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the analytical point is that British support was **interested, not benevolent**, and saying so converts a descriptive paragraph into an argument. Britain opposed reconquest because reconquest would have reclosed a market. It recognised the new states when recognition served trade. And the same commercial relationship that helped secure independence began, within a decade, to produce the structure that Lesson 19 examines: open economies exporting primary products, importing manufactures and servicing debts contracted in London. ⚠️ The disciplined formulation is that Spanish America exchanged a **political monopoly** for a **commercial preponderance**, and that the second was in most respects more profitable for the region's export elites and more constraining for its manufacturers.

### 3. The United States and the Monroe message

✅ **Fact:** the United States recognised several of the new states from **1822**, ahead of the European powers. On **2 December 1823**, in the president's annual message to Congress, **James Monroe** set out the principles that came to be called the **Monroe Doctrine**: that the American continents were no longer open to future European colonisation; that the United States would regard any attempt by European powers to extend their political system to the Americas as dangerous to its peace and safety; that it would not interfere with existing European colonies; and that it would not take part in European wars.

✅ **Fact:** a joint Anglo-American declaration had been proposed by the British foreign secretary, **Canning**, and the United States chose instead to make a unilateral statement.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four observations are required, and together they form the whole of a good answer on this point.

1. **It was a declaration of policy, not a treaty or a law.** It created no obligation on anyone and established no machinery.
2. **It could not be enforced by the United States in 1823.** The capacity to prevent a European expedition crossing the Atlantic lay with the British navy, and the practical guarantee of non-intervention was British commercial interest.
3. **It was not solicited by, or agreed with, the new republics**, and it did not bind the United States to defend them. ⚠️ Bolivar's own project for regional cooperation, expressed in the Congress of Panama of 1826, was a separate Spanish American initiative, not an American-hemisphere alliance under United States leadership.
4. **Its long-run significance is real but belongs elsewhere.** ⚠️ The doctrine's later use as a justification for United States primacy and intervention in the region is a twentieth-century subject belonging to the twentieth-century Latin America topic, together with the whole account of United States economic, political and military influence and of its interventions. This session establishes the 1823 statement and hands the rest over.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sentence that scores is therefore: **the Monroe message of 1823 announced a principle that British sea power was already enforcing for reasons of its own, and acquired its coercive meaning only much later.** That is a graded, dated judgement, and it avoids both the error of dismissing the message as empty and the error of treating it as the shield of Latin American independence.

### 4. The Holy Alliance intervention that never sailed

✅ **Fact:** after the congresses of the post-Napoleonic European settlement, a French army acting with the approval of the continental powers restored **Ferdinand VII** to absolute authority in Spain in **1823**. Proposals for a comparable intervention to restore Spanish authority in America were discussed among the continental powers and were not executed. British objection and the practical difficulty of transatlantic operations against British naval opposition are the standard explanations.

⚠️ **Boundary:** the Congress of Vienna, the Concert of Europe, the congress system, the principle of legitimacy and the diplomacy of the 1820s congresses belong to the Vienna and Concert of Europe topic. This session records only the American consequence: that the threat was real enough to shape diplomacy, that the expedition did not sail, and that the reason was in the last analysis maritime. Do not narrate the European congresses here.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the non-event is worth a sentence in an answer because of what it proves about the nature of the independence settlement. The new states were secured not by a guarantee they had obtained but by a convergence between their survival and the commercial interest of the strongest naval power. ⚠️ That is a weak foundation for sovereignty, and it is one of the structural reasons why the nineteenth-century relationship between the region and the great powers took the form it did.

### 5. Haiti's exception, and what it shows about the international order

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the treatment of Haiti is the control case for the whole of this lesson, exactly as it was for Lesson 5.

- ✅ Haiti supplied Bolivar with asylum, arms and ships in **1816** on the express condition of emancipation, making it the only state in the region whose assistance carried a social condition.
- ✅ Haiti was excluded from diplomatic recognition for a generation, and obtained French recognition in **1825** only in exchange for an indemnity of **150 million francs**, reduced in **1838** to **90 million**.
- ⚠️ The contrast is exact and should be stated in one sentence: the creole republics were recognised because trade followed recognition; the Black republic was recognised only when it agreed to pay for the privilege. ⚠️ The international system of the 1820s was not neutral between social outcomes, and Haiti is the evidence.

### 6. What external actors explain, and what they do not

| Question | Do external actors explain it? |
|---|---|
| Why the movements began | ⚠️ No. ✅ The trigger was the Iberian crisis; no external power instigated the risings |
| Why reconquest did not succeed | ⚠️ Substantially yes - British naval and commercial interest made a European expedition impracticable |
| Why the new states survived as states | ⚠️ Partly - recognition and trade stabilised them internationally while they remained unstable internally |
| Why the social outcome was conservative | ⚠️ No. That followed from the composition of the domestic winning coalitions, and Haiti's treatment shows only that the international order penalised the alternative rather than preventing it |
| Why export dependence developed | ⚠️ Partly; this is the subject of Lesson 19, where the argument is stated and limited |

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This lesson over-credits Britain. British recognition came late, after the wars were effectively won; the loans of the 1820s were speculative ventures that ended in default and did more harm than good; the volunteers were a small fraction of the forces engaged; and there is no evidence that any European power seriously intended to send an army across the Atlantic. Building the independence of a continent on the calculations of the London stock market and the Royal Navy repeats a nineteenth-century British self-image.

**Reply:** The objection is right on two specifics and wrong on the structure. It is right that recognition was late and consequential mainly for finance and trade rather than for the fighting, and right that the loan boom of the 1820s ended badly - a point this session makes in its own right in Lesson 19. On the volunteers it is also right that their importance was technical rather than numerical, which is how they are described here. But the central claim does not rest on any of those. It rests on a counterfactual that is not speculative: a European expedition to restore Spanish authority in America was discussed in the same years in which a French army actually restored absolutism in Spain, and the reason it could be contemplated in Madrid and not in Mexico is the Atlantic. Naval control of the Atlantic in the 1820s lay with Britain, and British commercial interest was unambiguously against reconquest. That is not a British self-image; it is a statement about where the capacity to move armies across an ocean lay. The disciplined formulation is that Britain did not win Latin American independence and did make its reversal impracticable, which are different claims and only the second is asserted here.

**Residual:** No figures can be established here for British trade with the region, for the size or terms of the loans, for the number of foreign volunteers, or for the deliberations of the continental powers on intervention. The session therefore states the forms of involvement and the recorded outcomes, and records that the intention behind the discussed intervention cannot be assessed from what is held here.

### UPSC application

Two precise uses and one boundary. First, whenever the Monroe message appears in a question or a script, the four observations of section 3 are the answer, and the graded sentence - a principle announced in 1823 that British sea power was already enforcing for its own reasons - is worth more than a paragraph of description. Second, the Haitian contrast in section 5 is the most efficient evidence available for any claim about the international order's treatment of social revolution. The boundary is that the Congress of Vienna, the Concert of Europe and the congress system belong to their own topic, and the twentieth-century United States relationship with the region belongs to its own topic; name both hand-overs in a clause each and do not narrate either.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Treating the Monroe Doctrine as the reason Latin American independence survived; in 1823 the United States could not have enforced it.
- Treating it as empty rhetoric, which misses that it stated a principle of long-run significance and was chosen over a joint declaration with Britain.
- Narrating the Congress of Vienna or the congress system, which trespasses on another topic.
- Importing twentieth-century United States interventions into a nineteenth-century answer; they belong to the twentieth-century Latin America topic.
- Presenting British support as disinterested rather than commercial.
- Omitting Haiti's exclusion and indemnity, which is the sharpest evidence about the international order.
- Supplying trade, loan or troop figures; none is verified here.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Four British forms of involvement: commercial, military and naval, financial, diplomatic.
2. ⚠️ British support was interested, not benevolent: reconquest would have reclosed a market.
3. ⚠️ The exchange: a political monopoly replaced by a commercial preponderance.
4. ✅ United States recognition of several new states from **1822**.
5. ✅ The **Monroe message of 2 December 1823**: no future European colonisation; extension of the European political system to the Americas dangerous to United States peace and safety; no interference with existing European colonies; no participation in European wars.
6. ✅ **Canning** proposed a joint Anglo-American declaration; the United States acted unilaterally.
7. ⚠️ Four observations: policy not law; unenforceable by the United States in 1823; not solicited by or binding toward the new republics; its coercive meaning is later and owned elsewhere.
8. ⚠️ The graded sentence: the message announced a principle that British sea power was already enforcing for its own reasons.
9. ✅ A French army restored Ferdinand VII's absolute authority in Spain in **1823**; a comparable American intervention was discussed and never executed.
10. ⚠️ Boundary: the Vienna settlement, the Concert of Europe and the congress system belong to their own topic; only the American consequence is used here.
11. ⚠️ The non-event matters: the new states were secured by a convergence with British commercial interest rather than by any guarantee of their own.
12. ✅ Haiti supplied Bolivar in 1816 on condition of emancipation, and was recognised by France only in 1825 in exchange for an indemnity of 150 million francs, reduced to 90 million in 1838.
13. ⚠️ The contrast: creole republics recognised because trade followed; the Black republic recognised only on payment.
14. ⚠️ External actors explain the failure of reconquest and the international survival of the new states; they do not explain the risings or the conservative social outcome.

### Concept check

**Question:** Assess the role of the Monroe Doctrine in securing Latin American independence.

**Model answer:** Its role in this period was declaratory rather than protective, and the accurate assessment separates the statement from its enforcement. The message of 2 December 1823 announced that the American continents were no longer open to future European colonisation, that any attempt by European powers to extend their political system to the Americas would be regarded as dangerous to United States peace and safety, that existing European colonies would not be interfered with and that the United States would not take part in European wars; it followed United States recognition of several new states from 1822, and it was chosen in preference to the joint declaration proposed by Canning. But it was a statement of policy, not a treaty; it created no obligation and no machinery; it was not solicited by the new republics and did not bind the United States to defend them; and in 1823 the United States lacked the naval capacity to prevent a European expedition crossing the Atlantic. The power that actually made reconquest impracticable was Britain, whose manufactures and capital had entered the region once the imperial monopoly ended and whose navy controlled the ocean any expedition would have to cross - which is why a French army could restore Ferdinand VII in Spain in 1823 while no comparable force was sent to America. The disciplined verdict is that the Monroe message announced a principle that British commercial and naval interest was already enforcing for reasons of its own, and that its coercive significance for the region belongs to a later period and a different topic. The contrasting treatment of Haiti, isolated for a generation and recognised by France in 1825 only in exchange for an indemnity, shows what the international order of these years was actually protecting.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the doctrine as either the shield of Latin American independence or as empty words. It was neither: a genuine statement of an enduring principle, issued by a state that could not yet enforce it, in a situation already being secured by another power's interest.

*The next lesson audits the promises. The wars were won partly by offering freedom, the end of tribute, legal equality and land. Independence arrived. The question now is what was actually delivered - and the answer, examined instrument by instrument, is the strongest evidence available for the topic's central finding.*

## Lesson 17 - Abolition by instalments, tribute and community land: what legal equality did and did not do

Progress: 17/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: caste abolished in law; the free-womb device and the long road to emancipation; the abolition dates; Brazil and Cuba as the outliers; tribute abolished and reimposed; community land and liberal property doctrine; what the audit establishes

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - emancipation immediate only in Haiti, surviving social and racial hierarchy, and worsening Indigenous land pressure
CA search: not sought - the one current linkage is held back for Lesson 18
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the audit, instrument by instrument

```text

   ------------------------       -------------------     -------
```
