## What to rehearse, given a nil return

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a topic with no owned question is prepared differently from one with a bank of past papers, and the difference is a matter of method rather than of effort.

1. **Rehearse architectures, not answers.** The eight directive rows in Lesson 22 exhaust what can realistically be asked, and each has a structure. Practise producing the structure from the stem in under ninety seconds.
2. **Rehearse the controls, because they are what a first attempt gets wrong.** Twelve items, of which the four date controls and the five-path count discipline are the most frequently breached.
3. **Rehearse the social-outcome paragraph separately.** ✅ The syllabus clause names **effect on the society**, and the audit of Lesson 17 is the part most likely to be thin under time pressure because it requires a calendar rather than a sentiment.
4. **Rehearse the hand-offs.** Seven boundaries, one clause each. A script that trespasses into the American Revolution, French Revolution, Vienna or twentieth-century owners loses time and gains nothing.
5. **Use the recorded probable framings as the practice bank.** ✅ Three are standard - explain the causes and course; compare Bolivar and San Martin; why political independence did not automatically produce social equality - and ✅ three more are equally well established - why independence did not produce stable democracies in the nineteenth century; compare Haiti with the mainland movements; and the proposition that independence was politically revolutionary but socially incomplete. The six original questions below are built to cover all six framings.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - Starting conditions and the opening, Lessons 1 to 7

### Cumulative check A1 - from a standing hierarchy to an active grievance

**Question:** Show how a social order that had been stable for two centuries became, within a single generation, the basis of a continental political grievance - and identify precisely what still had to happen before that grievance could produce a break.

**Model answer:** The order itself supplied the grievance's content. Four unaligned hierarchies structured colonial society: office, in which peninsular-born Europeans held the viceregal, judicial and senior ecclesiastical posts; legal caste, in which mestizo, pardo, Indigenous and enslaved populations held graded and inferior statuses; land and labour, organised through the hacienda, the plantation, Indigenous tribute and labour drafts; and trade, routed and taxed through the imperial system. American-born creoles sat at the top of the social and economic ladders and were blocked on the political one, and that mismatch gave them both a motive and the means to act. What converted the standing condition into an active grievance was the eighteenth-century reform programme. New viceroyalties, the intendancy system, fiscal intensification, state monopolies, the expulsion of the Jesuits in 1767 and a deliberate reduction of the creole share of senior appointments made exclusion a matter of policy rather than custom, while the 1778 commercial decree opened trade only within the empire and left the substantive demand unmet. The same measures provoked the mass risings of 1780-83 in the Andes and of 1781 in New Granada, which proved that grievance reached far below the elite and simultaneously taught that elite to fear mass mobilisation. What still had to happen was the removal of the authority everyone was obeying. The grievances were decades old and produced no independence movement; the imperial crisis of 1808, in which legitimacy collapsed at the centre before it was attacked in the colonies, supplied the opening, and the revolts began in 1810 once the Junta Central had given way to a Regency whose claim was no stronger than a local junta's.

**Misconception to avoid:** Collapsing the causal ladder into a list. Background condition, predisposing cause, enabling condition and precipitant are four different kinds of cause, and an answer that places the colonial hierarchy, Bourbon taxation, the Enlightenment and Napoleon side by side as four items of the same kind has not explained the timing of anything.

### Cumulative check A2 - the road not taken

**Question:** Using the Cortes of Cadiz and the Haitian Revolution together, explain what each demonstrates about what was and was not possible in this period.

**Model answer:** They are the topic's two tests, and they test opposite things. Cadiz tests whether the imperial relationship could have been reformed instead of broken. The Cortes summoned in 1810 seated American deputies, declared the Spanish nation to comprise Spaniards of both hemispheres and thereby abolished colonial status in principle, promulgated the Constitution of 1812 with national sovereignty and equality before the law, and legislated away Indigenous tribute and compulsory labour drafts. It then refused the two concessions that would have transferred power or revenue: trade with foreign nations, and proportionate representation - the latter withheld by excluding those of African descent from citizenship and from the population base on which seats were calculated, which shows that the representation question and the race question were a single question. When the restoration of 1814 annulled the whole settlement by decree, it demonstrated that no Spanish concession could bind a future Spanish government, which is the point at which autonomy ceased to be a rational objective. Haiti tests whether social transformation was possible. The revolution that began in 1791 was made by the enslaved majority, defeated an attempted restoration in 1802, produced independence on 1 January 1804 and ended slavery immediately and irreversibly, destroying the plantation order as a system of ownership and replacing the ruling group outright. Together they establish a precise conclusion: a liberal empire was constructible in principle and was refused for structural reasons, and social revolution was achievable in this region in this period and was not attempted by the creole leadership. Neither outcome was foreclosed by circumstance; both were decided by interest.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Cadiz as irrelevant because it was short-lived, or Haiti as exceptional because it was French. Their value is as tests: the first is the only occasion when the alternative to independence was actually constructed, and the second is the only case where independence and social revolution coincided. Remove either and the topic's central claims become assertions.

## Block B - The five paths, Lessons 5 and 8 to 14

### Cumulative check B1 - one crisis, five outcomes

**Question:** Explain why a single imperial collapse produced five different kinds of outcome, and identify the variable that predicts which outcome occurred where.

**Model answer:** The crisis supplied a common opening and nothing else, so the outcome in each territory was determined by who was able to use the opening and what that group wanted. In Haiti the revolution preceded the crisis and was made by the enslaved majority, so independence in 1804 was also social revolution: slavery ended at once, the plantation order was destroyed and the ruling group was replaced. In Mexico the crisis produced a popular and Indigenous rising under Hidalgo from 1810 and a constitutional social programme under Morelos at Chilpancingo in 1813 and Apatzingan in 1814, which the creole elite, the church hierarchy and the royalist army combined to defeat; independence came in 1821 through the Plan of Iguala, proclaimed by the royalist commander Iturbide in alliance with the insurgent survivor Guerrero, guaranteeing religion, a monarchy and legal equality of peninsulares and Americans while saying nothing about land. In northern South America the opening was used by a creole leadership that had to buy the interior after two lost republics - emancipation for service, land and rank for the llaneros - and produced Boyaca in 1819, Gran Colombia, Carabobo in 1821 and Pichincha in 1822. In the south a professional army built in a secure province crossed the Andes in 1817, took Chile at Chacabuco and Maipu, and carried the war to Peru by sea in 1820; Peru, whose elite preferred the empire, was finished by the northern army at Junin and Ayacucho in 1824, and Bolivia emerged in 1825. In Brazil there was no vacancy at all, because the Portuguese court had relocated in 1808; ports were opened, the colony was raised to a kingdom in 1815, and when Portugal demanded recolonisation the heir declared independence in 1822 and was crowned emperor, with slavery retained until 1888. The variable that predicts the outcome is the social composition of the group that used the opening: bottom-led produced social revolution, elite-led produced political transfer, dynasty-led produced continuity.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the five outcomes as regional idiosyncrasies. They are the same variable producing different values, and an answer that can name the variable is explaining the region while an answer that describes five stories is only reporting it.

### Cumulative check B2 - the two liberators, compared as methods

**Question:** Compare Bolivar and San Martin as methods rather than as men, and explain why their theatres had to meet and why the meeting ended as it did.

**Model answer:** They were solving different problems and therefore used different methods. Bolivar operated inside contested territory with no secure base, so after the loss of two republics in 1812 and 1814 he rebuilt on three principles: a base at Angostura on the Orinoco beyond naval reach, a social bargain that converted the llaneros from enemies into cavalry through emancipation decrees, land and rank and standing for Paez, and external supply in the form of foreign volunteers and London credit. He also built political institutions while fighting - the Congress of Angostura in 1819, Gran Colombia in December 1819, the presidency under the constitution of 1821. San Martin operated from a secure base outside the contested territory and could therefore prepare: three years converting Cuyo into a military base, the Army of the Andes, the crossing of 1817, Chacabuco and Maipu, a Chilean government under O'Higgins, and a squadron to carry the war to Peru in 1820. His principle was to liberate and hand over rather than to govern. The theatres had to meet because Peru was the objective of both and could not be finished by either alone: the southern expedition took the coast and proclaimed independence in July 1821 but could not break a royalist army that remained intact in the highlands while the Peruvian elite declined to rally. The meeting at Guayaquil on 26-27 July 1822 resolved a command problem by removing one commander; its content was never made public and four incompatible explanations circulate, so none should be asserted. What followed is clear: San Martin withdrew, Bolivar assumed extraordinary powers in Peru, and the highland campaign produced Junin on 6 August 1824 and Sucre's victory at Ayacucho on 9 December 1824. The deeper reason is institutional: Peru required a political authority willing to tax, conscript and override local resistance, and only one of the two methods supplied one.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining Guayaquil by the characters of the two men. No record of the conversation exists, and an answer that invents one is inventing evidence. State the establishable facts and the competing explanations, and then use the institutional difference, which is evidenced.

## Block C - Outcomes and consequences, Lessons 15 to 19

### Cumulative check C1 - the audit of promises

**Question:** Take three promises made during the wars and trace what happened to each, then state the pattern the three together establish.

**Model answer:** Take caste abolition, emancipation and the abolition of Indigenous tribute. Caste abolition was delivered quickly and across the region, including in the Plan of Iguala of 1821, because legal status transfers no assets; the recorded outcome is nonetheless that social and racial hierarchies survived and that mixed-descent populations obtained formal legal change without social ascent. Emancipation was promised in two forms. Freedom for military service was honoured for individuals who served and survived, and was used by both sides - the crown from early in the wars, the patriots systematically in the north after Petion of Haiti made emancipation the condition of assistance in 1816, and in the south through the enslaved recruits who formed a substantial part of the Army of the Andes. General emancipation was another matter: it was immediate only in Haiti, and elsewhere proceeded through free-womb laws that freed nobody already enslaved - the River Plate in 1813, Gran Colombia and Peru in 1821, Brazil as late as 1871 - before final abolition in Chile in 1823, Mexico in 1829, Colombia in 1851, Argentina in 1853, Peru and Venezuela in 1854, Cuba in 1886 and Brazil in 1888. The abolition of Indigenous tribute was enacted repeatedly - at Cadiz, by Hidalgo in 1810, by San Martin's Peruvian protectorate in 1821 and by several republics - and then restored by several of those republics, because a state that would not tax land or income needed the revenue. The pattern the three establish is that promises divided by cost: what cost the ruling coalition nothing was delivered at once, and what would have transferred labour, revenue or land was delayed, reversed or never attempted. The same pattern appears in a fourth case that was not even promised - the colonial protection of Indigenous community land, which the republics did not inherit and which liberal individual-property reform actively undermined, so that land pressure often continued or worsened.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding from the pattern that the promises were insincere. The tribute sequence shows something more precise: the republics abolished the head tax because they believed in equality and restored it because they needed the revenue and would not tax themselves. A structural constraint operating on people who meant what they said is both more accurate and more persuasive than an accusation of bad faith.

### Cumulative check C2 - why the states were weak

**Question:** Explain the connection between how the wars were won and the character of the states that resulted, drawing on both the political and the economic evidence.

**Model answer:** The connection is direct in both domains. Politically, the patriot side had no national treasury and no means of central direction across the Andes and the great river basins, so it raised forces regionally under commanders with local followings and paid for them with promises of land, rank and a share in the new order. At the end of the wars the only functioning organisations in most territories were those regional armed forces; the colonial inheritance provided no tradition of legislative self-government comparable to the North American colonial assemblies and therefore no established civilian political class; regional landowners funded and commanded personal armed followings; and personal loyalty substituted for institutional legitimacy. That chain is caudillismo, defined as personalist rule by a military strongman resting on armed clientage rather than on institutions. The same forces fragmented the map: geography, colonial administrative boundaries that became state boundaries, provincial elites preferring local control, and armed commanders with regional bases. Gran Colombia, proclaimed in 1819, dissolved in 1830; the Central American union of 1823 broke up between 1838 and 1841; Bolivar's league at the Congress of Panama in 1826 was attended by few and ratified by almost none. Economically, the wars destroyed mining, herds, internal trade and the colonial revenue systems; direct taxation was politically impossible; and the new states therefore financed themselves from customs duties, making state income a direct function of external trade at exactly the moment when exports were concentrating in a narrow range of products whose prices were set elsewhere. Loans floated in London in the 1820s ended in widespread default and closed external credit for years. The result is a state that is solvent only while trade flows, has every incentive to expand exports whatever the social cost, and has little reason to build the administrative capacity that direct taxation requires - which is the fiscal face of the same weakness the caudillo mechanism describes politically.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating instability as evidence of a regional incapacity for self-government. That is circular, and a tradition of democracy did develop in Chile. A variable that does not vary cannot explain an outcome that does.

## Block D - Judgement, comparison and conversion, Lessons 20 to 22

### Cumulative check D1 - the whole topic in one judgement

**Question:** State the single most defensible overall judgement on Latin American independence, and set out the three pieces of evidence that carry it and the one qualification it must include.

**Model answer:** The most defensible judgement is that political decolonisation outran social democratisation: between about 1808 and 1826 sovereignty was transferred from two European empires to American states, and social power was confirmed in the hands of the groups that already held it. Three pieces of evidence carry it, each from a different domain. Politically, the men who led the movements were creoles who were wealthy but politically restricted, who supplied leadership, finance and officers, and who became the ruling class; in Mexico independence was achieved in 1821 by the royalist commander who had helped defeat the socially radical insurgency of 1810-15, on terms guaranteeing religion, property and legal equality of status with nothing on land. Socially, the audit of instruments divides by cost: caste distinctions abolished in law at once and hierarchy surviving in practice; emancipation immediate only in Haiti and reaching Colombia in 1851, Peru and Venezuela in 1854 and Brazil in 1888; Indigenous tribute abolished and then reimposed; and community land losing its legal protection at the moment Indigenous legal separateness was abolished, so that land pressure continued or worsened. Economically, the commercial monopoly ended and was replaced by a structure of narrow primary exports, foreign credit and customs-financed states, in which cheap abundant labour removed the incentive to modernise agriculture. The qualification the judgement must include is that the alternative was historically available and was defeated rather than absent: Haiti achieved independence as social revolution in the same region and period, and the Mexican insurgency proposed the same programme in constitutional form in 1813-14. That is what makes the outcome a choice made by the winning coalitions rather than a limit imposed by the age - and it is also why the legal revolution that did occur, the abolition of caste categories across a whole region, should be conceded as genuine rather than dismissed.

**Misconception to avoid:** Turning the judgement into a verdict on the character of the leaders. The argument is structural: a coalition financed by landowners, merchants and creditors could not promise to redistribute the assets securing its credit, and its inability to outbid the crown for the loyalty of the interior followed from what it was defending. That is an explanation; moral disappointment is not.

### Cumulative check D2 - writing under an unfamiliar stem

**Question:** An unfamiliar 15-mark stem reads: "Assess the significance of the Haitian Revolution for the wider history of Latin American independence." Produce the structure, assign the evidence and state the verdict.

**Model answer:** Thesis, about thirty words: Haiti matters in three distinct ways - as the region's only case of independence as social revolution, as a material actor in the mainland wars, and as the warning that shaped creole strategy everywhere else. Body one, the case itself, about seventy words: the revolution began in 1791 in a four-sided conflict among wealthy planters, poorer whites, free people of colour and the enslaved majority, with Spanish and British intervention; French revolutionary abolition converted insurgents into commanders; Toussaint Louverture governed the colony and prohibited bondage; the French expedition of 1802 attempted restoration, seized and deported him, and was defeated; independence was proclaimed on 1 January 1804 under Dessalines; Britannica records the result as the first state founded by former slaves. Body two, the material role, about fifty words: in 1816 President Petion supplied Bolivar with asylum, arms and ships on the express condition that he proclaim emancipation, which Bolivar did - making Haiti the only external supporter whose assistance carried a social condition, and connecting abolition to the recruitment logic that changed the military balance in the north. Body three, the deterrent effect and the penalty, about sixty words: for mainland creole elites Haiti answered the question of what happens when revolutionary language reaches the people they owned, which is one reason mainland movements avoided general emancipation, why royalist recruitment among enslaved and free coloured populations often succeeded, and why the new republics were slow to recognise Haiti; France recognised it only in 1825 and only in exchange for an indemnity of 150 million francs, reduced to 90 million in 1838. Verdict, about twenty-five words: Haiti is the region's control case - it proves social revolution was possible here, explains why the creole movements took care that it would not recur, and shows what the international order charged for it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Answering a significance question with a narrative. Significance means consequences for other things, so the structure must run outward from the case to the mainland movements, to the recruitment bargains, to the pattern of recognition and to the analytical use of the case as a control - not simply through the revolution's own chronology.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

These six questions are original to this session and are not verified questions; ✅ the topic owns none, and six probable framings are well established, all of which are covered below. Attempt each under timed conditions before reading the model answer. Each model sits at or below its stated word ceiling - 150 words at 10 marks and 250 words at 15 and 20 marks, the established repository convention - and each carries a scoring note identifying the single element that most often separates a good script from an average one on that demand.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

> Explain the causes and course of the Latin American independence movements. (Answer in 150 words)

### Model answer

Causes operated at three levels. The colonial order placed peninsular-born officeholders above wealthy but politically restricted creoles, with caste, tribute, hacienda labour and a commercial monopoly beneath them. Eighteenth-century reform sharpened this: creole appointments were cut, taxes intensified, and the 1778 decree opened trade only within the empire. The precipitant was external - Napoleon's intervention in Spain in 1808, where legitimacy collapsed at the centre before it was attacked in the colonies.

The course followed from that opening. Juntas formed in the captive king's name, revolts began in 1810, and Spain's refusal of equality, confirmed by the annulment of 1814, turned autonomy into independence. Hidalgo's rising in Mexico began in 1810 and ended in Iturbide's settlement of 1821. Bolivar secured New Granada in 1819 and built Gran Colombia; San Martin crossed the Andes in 1817; Ayacucho broke royalist power in 1824; Bolivia emerged in 1825. Brazil separated monarchically in 1822.

*Scoring note: the separator is the word "opening". Scripts that treat the grievances as causes cannot explain why nothing happened for decades; 1808 is what converts a standing condition into an event.*

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

> Compare the roles of Simon Bolivar and Jose de San Martin in the liberation of South America. (Answer in 150 words)

### Model answer

They were complementary rather than parallel, and the difference is one of method rather than of stature.

San Martin liberated the south with a disciplined regular army projected from an external base. Three years of preparation in Cuyo produced the Army of the Andes, which crossed in 1817 and secured Chile at Chacabuco and Maipu; Chilean revenue funded the squadron that carried the war to Peru in 1820. His principle was to liberate and hand over, and he declined political power.

Bolivar built a political project as well as an army, inside contested territory. After losing two republics he based the cause on the Orinoco, converted the llaneros by emancipation and rank, drew on foreign volunteers, and won at Boyaca in 1819 and Carabobo in 1821; he was president of Gran Colombia.

Their theatres met in Peru, which only a liberator willing to govern could finish.

*Scoring note: the separator is refusing biography. A script that narrates two lives earns description marks; one that runs a single question - base, force, bargain, political role - through both earns comparison marks.*

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

> Why did political independence in Latin America not automatically produce social equality? (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

Because the group that made the revolution was the group that benefited from the existing social order, and the settlement it produced answered its own grievance and nobody else's.

Creoles were wealthy but politically restricted. They supplied leadership, finance and officers, and their demands were access to office and commercial freedom - neither of which required disturbing caste, land or slavery. They became the ruling class, and power remained with them.

The audit of promises shows the pattern. Caste distinctions were abolished in law quickly and across the region, because legal status transfers no assets; hierarchy nonetheless survived in practice and mestizos obtained formal legal change without social ascent. Emancipation was immediate only in Haiti; elsewhere it came through freedom granted for military service and through free-womb laws that freed nobody already enslaved, before final abolition in Chile in 1823, Mexico in 1829, Colombia in 1851, Peru and Venezuela in 1854 and Brazil in 1888. Indigenous tribute was abolished at Cadiz, by Hidalgo and by San Martin's protectorate, and then restored by republics that needed revenue and would not tax land. Community land lost its protection when Indigenous legal separateness was abolished, so equality and dispossession arrived together; land pressure often continued or worsened.

The limits were chosen rather than imposed. Haiti achieved independence as social revolution in the same region and period, and Mexico's insurgency constitutionalised the same programme in 1813-14 before being defeated. Political decolonisation outran social democratisation because the winning coalitions decided it should.

*Scoring note: the separator is the abolition calendar. Scripts that assert continuity in general terms read as opinion; the dated sequence from 1823 to 1888 converts the same claim into evidence.*

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

> Compare the Haitian Revolution with the mainland Latin American independence movements. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

The comparison isolates a single variable - who made the revolution - and shows that the social outcome tracked it exactly.

Haiti's revolution began in 1791 among the enslaved majority of a French sugar colony, in a four-sided conflict also involving planters, poorer whites and free people of colour, with Spanish and British intervention. Slavery was abolished by the metropolis during the revolutionary decade; a French expedition attempted restoration in 1802, seized Toussaint Louverture, and was defeated; independence was proclaimed on 1 January 1804 under Dessalines. Britannica records the result as the first state founded by former slaves. Slavery ended immediately and irreversibly, the plantation order was destroyed as a system of ownership, and the ruling group was replaced.

The mainland movements were led by creoles - American-born whites, wealthy but politically restricted - whose demands were office and commercial freedom. Sovereignty was transferred to them; hierarchy survived; emancipation came piecemeal and decades later. Brazil, led by its own dynasty, retained slavery until 1888.

The two cases are also connected. In 1816 Petion of Haiti supplied Bolivar with asylum, arms and ships on the condition that he proclaim emancipation, which is why abolition entered the northern patriot programme when it did. And Haiti operated on creole elites chiefly as a warning, which helps explain their avoidance of general emancipation and the success of royalist recruitment below them.

The international order registered the difference: creole republics were recognised as trade followed; France recognised Haiti only in 1825, for an indemnity.

*Scoring note: the separator is treating Haiti as an actor rather than a symbol. The 1816 assistance and its abolitionist condition show the two cases materially connected, which is worth more than any number of adjectives about radicalism.*

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

> "Latin American independence was politically revolutionary but socially incomplete." Discuss. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

Both halves are true at different levels, and the answer lies in naming them.

Politically it was revolutionary. Two empires lost almost all of mainland America between about 1808 and 1826; colonial status ended; viceregal government was replaced across Spanish America by republics with written constitutions, elected congresses and declared rights; and caste distinctions were abolished in law throughout the region. Measured against 1800, that is a transformation of the legal and political order.

Socially it was not. Creoles supplied leadership, finance and officers and became the ruling class. Hierarchy survived in practice. Emancipation was immediate only in Haiti and reached Colombia in 1851, Peru and Venezuela in 1854 and Brazil in 1888. Tribute was abolished and reimposed. Community land lost its protection as Indigenous legal separateness ended, and land pressure often continued or worsened. Social power remained concentrated in oligarchic landholding.

The region's own variation shows the limit was chosen. Haiti, made from below, abolished slavery at once. Mexico's insurgency under Hidalgo and Morelos constitutionalised caste abolition, the end of tribute and limits on great estates in 1813-14, and was defeated by a coalition including the creole elite; independence arrived in 1821 through a royalist commander's settlement guaranteeing religion and property. Brazil, led by its dynasty, kept slavery for sixty-six years after independence.

The legacy followed the method: states before nations and armies before institutions, with Gran Colombia dissolving in 1830.

The verdict: a real revolution in sovereignty, a deliberate conservation in society.

*Scoring note: the separator is the internal variation. A script that argues continuity from the mainland alone invites the reply that nothing more was possible; Haiti and Mexico's first phase prove the alternative was proposed within the same region and decade.*

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

> Why did the Latin American republics fail to establish stable democratic government in the nineteenth century? (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

Because of how the wars were won, what colonial rule failed to build, and how the new states were financed - not from regional incapacity, which the evidence refutes.

The wars produced authority. With no national treasury and no means of central direction across the Andes and the great river basins, the patriot side raised forces regionally under commanders with local followings, paid in land, rank and a share of the new order. At the end, the only functioning organisations were those armed regional powers.

Colonial rule had produced no alternative. The Spanish empire left no tradition of self-government comparable to the North American colonial assemblies, so independence created states with armies but without an established civilian political class. Regional landowners funded personal armed followings, and personal loyalty substituted for institutional legitimacy - caudillismo, the region's characteristic nineteenth-century form, reinforced by oligarchic landholding and by surviving church and army influence.

The same inheritance fragmented the map. Distance, weak communications and regional elite interests defeated continental federation: Gran Colombia dissolved in 1830, the Central American union broke up between 1838 and 1841, and the Panama congress of 1826 was ratified by almost none.

Finance completed the problem. War destroyed mining, herds and the colonial revenue systems; direct taxation was politically impossible; and customs duties on narrow primary exports became the fiscal base, leaving states solvent only while trade flowed.

That the explanation is structural rather than cultural is confirmed by the exception: a democratic tradition did develop in Chile.

*Scoring note: the separator is the exception. Naming Chile converts a structural argument from assertion into a tested claim, because it shows the candidate knows that an explanation which cannot accommodate variation is not an explanation.*

# REMEDIATION

## Error map

| # | The error | Why it costs marks | The correction | Taught in |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Treating "Latin American independence" as one event with one cause | Removes the variation that is the topic's own evidence | ✅ One trigger and five distinct paths | Lesson 1 |
| 2 | Using "independence" and "revolution" as synonyms | Makes the most probable exam question unanswerable | Independence transfers sovereignty; revolution transforms social power | Lessons 1, 21 |
| 3 | Describing the paths as four or six, or letting the 1808 trigger cover Haiti | ✅ Explicitly prohibited by the count discipline | Five paths - Haiti, Mexico, northern South America, southern South America, Brazil - and one trigger that opened four of them | Lessons 1, 20 |
| 4 | Saying Spain lost the Americas by 1826 without qualification | ✅ Cuba is inside the region and outside this period | Add "mainland" | Lesson 1 |
| 5 | Giving a number for the new states created | ✅ No such count is reliably established here | Describe fragmentation without counting | Lessons 1, 18 |
| 6 | Writing that creoles were "oppressed" | Imprecise, and invites the reply that they owned the country | ✅ Socially dominant and politically restricted | Lesson 2 |
| 7 | Presenting the caste order as prejudice alone | Loses the tribute and labour revenue that made it durable | The categories were fiscally and administratively productive | Lesson 2 |
| 8 | Treating Indigenous communities as one actor with one interest | Cannot accommodate their division by region | State the region and the recorded action | Lessons 2, 15 |
| 9 | Forgetting that colonial protection of community land was fiscal | Makes the later dispossession look accidental | Protection existed because the community had to pay tribute | Lessons 2, 17 |
| 10 | Translating *comercio libre* as free trade | Dissolves the grievance the reforms are supposed to have caused | ✅ The 1778 decree opened trade **within** the empire | Lesson 3 |
| 11 | Treating the Bourbon reforms as the cause of independence | Leaves the timing unexplained | They are a predisposing cause; ✅ the precipitant is 1808 | Lesson 3 |
| 12 | Omitting the risings of 1780-83 and 1781 | Loses both the depth of grievance and the source of creole caution | Andean rising under Tupac Amaru II; Comuneros of New Granada | Lesson 3 |
| 13 | Presenting the reforms as uniformly resented | Cannot account for royalist America | Militia reform and wider imperial trade benefited some groups | Lessons 3, 15 |
| 14 | Asserting that the Atlantic revolutions caused the movements | Leaves a gap of decades unexplained | Ideas supplied form; 1808 supplied the opening | Lesson 4 |
| 15 | Writing "Enlightenment ideas" without specifying | Contributes nothing assessable | Name the doctrine: sovereignty in the nation, not the monarch's person | Lesson 4 |
| 16 | Treating French influence as purely encouraging | Misses the deterrent effect that produced creole caution | It operated as programme **and** warning | Lesson 4 |
| 17 | Treating Haiti as an inspiration to creole leaders | Inverts the recorded relationship | It was a warning to them and an inspiration to those they ruled | Lessons 4, 5 |
| 18 | Dating Haitian independence to 1791 | ✅ Explicitly prohibited | Revolution begins 1791; independence 1804 | Lesson 5 |
| 19 | Describing Haiti as a two-sided conflict | Loses the free-coloured claim, the white division and the interventions | Four internal parties plus Spanish and British intervention | Lesson 5 |
| 20 | Omitting the attempted restoration of 1802 | Removes the step that makes the event a revolution | A restoration was attempted and defeated | Lesson 5 |
| 21 | Explaining Haiti's later poverty by the radicalism of its revolution | Assumes the conclusion and ignores the penalties | Isolation, and recognition bought in 1825 for an indemnity | Lesson 5 |
| 22 | Writing that the colonies rebelled in 1808 | ✅ Revolts are dated 1810; the gap is evidence | The Regency of 1810 removed the last stronger claim | Lesson 6 |
| 23 | Presenting the juntas as independence movements from the outset | Makes the oaths to Ferdinand VII inexplicable | They claimed kingdom status and governed in the king's name | Lesson 6 |
| 24 | Treating royalism as a Spanish imposition | Cannot explain fifteen years of war | It was an American constitutional position held by Americans | Lessons 6, 15 |
| 25 | Making the topic an accident of European war | Loses fifteen years of American decisions | Europe supplied the opening; America supplied the war and the settlement | Lesson 6 |
| 26 | Omitting Cadiz | Forces an unargued assertion of inevitability | It is the only occasion the alternative was constructed | Lesson 7 |
| 27 | Missing the link between racial exclusion and representation | Loses the strongest evidence that the refusal was structural | Excluding those of African descent preserved a peninsular majority | Lesson 7 |
| 28 | Dating the shift to independence at 1810 | Confuses the opening with the hardening | ✅ 1814 closed the autonomy option | Lessons 6, 7 |
| 29 | Forgetting the 1820 Riego revolt | Makes the Mexican reversal of 1821 inexplicable | It restored the Constitution of 1812 and inverted Mexican alignments | Lessons 7, 9 |
| 30 | Describing Hidalgo's rising as a creole independence movement | Reverses the actual alignment | ✅ Creoles fought on the royalist side against it | Lesson 8 |
| 31 | Omitting Morelos | Reduces the phase to a jacquerie | ✅ Chilpancingo 1813; ✅ Apatzingan 1814; a constitutional social programme | Lesson 8 |
| 32 | Dating Mexican independence to 1810 | ✅ 1810 is the Grito; ✅ 1821 is independence | Keep the eleven-year sequence visible | Lessons 8, 9 |
| 33 | Presenting Mexican independence as Hidalgo's culmination | Inverts the relationship between the phases | ✅ The sharpest reversal in the region | Lesson 9 |
| 34 | Forgetting that Iturbide had fought the insurgency | Removes the whole point of the reversal | The royalist commander proclaimed independence | Lesson 9 |
| 35 | Reading the Plan of Iguala's caste abolition as radicalism | It was the cheapest available concession | Religion and property guaranteed; nothing on land | Lesson 9 |
| 36 | Omitting the empire of 1822 and the republic of 1824 | Loses the settlement's instability | Monarchy to republic in two years | Lesson 9 |
| 37 | Beginning the northern story in 1819 | Removes the explanation of the winning strategy | Two lost republics produced every element of it | Lesson 10 |
| 38 | Treating royalist forces in Venezuela as Spanish | Destroys the mechanism | ✅ The decisive royalist force was the American llaneros | Lesson 10 |
| 39 | Presenting Bolivar's emancipation decrees as purely ideological | Loses the Haitian condition and the recruitment function | ✅ Petion made emancipation the price of assistance in 1816 | Lesson 10 |
| 40 | Presenting them as purely cynical | Cannot explain acceptance of a condition he could have refused | Conviction and constraint operated together | Lessons 10, 17 |
| 41 | Narrating the Andes crossing of 1819 as an adventure | Misses the strategic inversion | The base, the coalition and the axis were all changed | Lesson 11 |
| 42 | Dating New Granada or Gran Colombia other than 1819 | ✅ Fixed by the chronology | Boyaca 7 August 1819; Gran Colombia December 1819 | Lesson 11 |
| 43 | Omitting Sucre | Misattributes Pichincha and Ayacucho | ✅ Sucre commanded at Pichincha 1822 and Ayacucho 1824 | Lessons 11, 13 |
| 44 | Treating Gran Colombia's collapse as a failure of will | Removes the structural explanation | It failed for the reasons that made it possible | Lesson 11 |
| 45 | Writing that Bolivar permanently united Spanish America | ✅ Explicitly prohibited | ✅ His integrative vision did not endure politically | Lessons 11, 18 |
| 46 | Presenting the Andes crossing as a display of courage | Misses that it acquired a Pacific coastline | Chile was the means; Peru was the objective | Lesson 12 |
| 47 | Omitting the failed expeditions into Upper Peru | Removes the proof that the land route was unusable | Repeated defeats, 1811-15 | Lesson 12 |
| 48 | Forgetting the naval dimension in the south | Makes the Peruvian expedition inexplicable | Chilean revenue funded the squadron | Lesson 12 |
| 49 | Omitting enslaved recruitment in the Army of the Andes | Loses the southern form of the recruitment bargain | Enlistment in exchange for freedom | Lessons 12, 15 |
| 50 | Asserting the content of the Guayaquil conversation | No record of it was ever issued | State the meeting, the date, the withdrawal and the competing explanations | Lesson 13 |
| 51 | Dating Ayacucho or Bolivia other than 1824 and 1825 | ✅ Explicitly prohibited | Ayacucho 9 December 1824; Bolivia 1825 | Lesson 13 |
| 52 | Treating Peru as simply the last territory reached | Misses that its elite preferred the empire | Independence had to be imported and imposed | Lesson 13 |
| 53 | Presenting Peru as uniformly royalist | Erases Peruvian patriots | The balance of **elite** interest favoured the existing order | Lesson 13 |
| 54 | Saying Brazil followed the Spanish American route | ✅ Explicitly prohibited | ✅ Monarchical path under Pedro I in 1822 | Lesson 14 |
| 55 | Omitting the transfer of the court in 1807-08 | Makes the whole divergence inexplicable | The sovereign was present, so there was no vacancy | Lesson 14 |
| 56 | Omitting the elevation to kingdom in 1815 | Loses the concession Cadiz refused | Colonial status ended in law without a war | Lessons 7, 14 |
| 57 | Forgetting the Porto revolt and the recolonisation demand | Removes the actual trigger of 1822 | Portugal tried to reverse 1808 and 1815 | Lesson 14 |
| 58 | Calling Brazilian independence simply peaceful | ✅ The recorded description is **comparatively** non-violent | Provincial revolt was suppressed and the assembly dissolved | Lesson 14 |
| 59 | Omitting that slavery persisted in Brazil | Loses the most valuable comparative fact available | ✅ Abolition in 1888, sixty-six years after independence | Lessons 14, 17 |
| 60 | Describing the wars as colonisers against colonised | Cannot accommodate royalist America | They were civil wars within American society | Lesson 15 |
| 61 | Omitting women, or including them decoratively | Loses the point that their legal status did not change | The wars expanded what women did, not what they were permitted to be | Lesson 15 |
| 62 | Treating the church as a bloc | The hierarchy and the parish clergy frequently opposed each other | ✅ Two priests led the Mexican insurgency | Lesson 15 |
| 63 | Presenting freedom-for-service as general emancipation | It was conditional, individual and of unestablished scale | Distinguish it from free-womb laws and from abolition statutes | Lessons 15, 17 |
| 64 | Treating the Monroe message as the shield of independence | The United States could not enforce it in 1823 | British naval and commercial interest made reconquest impracticable | Lesson 16 |
| 65 | Treating the Monroe message as empty | Misses a principle of long-run significance chosen over a joint declaration | ✅ Canning proposed a joint statement and was declined | Lesson 16 |
| 66 | Narrating the Congress of Vienna or the congress system | Trespasses on another owner | Use only the American consequence | Lesson 16 |
| 67 | Importing twentieth-century United States interventions | Belongs to the twentieth-century owner | Establish the inheritance and hand over | Lessons 16, 19 |
| 68 | Writing that independence abolished slavery | ✅ Explicitly prohibited | ✅ Immediate only in Haiti | Lesson 17 |
| 69 | Treating free-womb laws as emancipation | They freed nobody already enslaved | Children freed from a date, often bound to serve for years | Lesson 17 |
| 70 | Omitting the tribute reversal | Loses the strongest single item in the audit | Abolished at Cadiz and by republics, then restored | Lesson 17 |
| 71 | Presenting community-land loss as neglect | Misses that a liberal reform produced a regressive effect | Equality of status and loss of collective tenure arrived together | Lesson 17 |
| 72 | Dismissing caste abolition as worthless | Overstates the case and weakens the answer | It was a real legal revolution; the qualification improves the argument | Lessons 17, 21 |
| 73 | Naming individual caudillos with dates or careers | ✅ Explicitly prohibited by the source control | Name the phenomenon and state the mechanism | Lesson 18 |
| 74 | Explaining instability by regional or cultural incapacity | ✅ Explicitly prohibited, circular, and refuted by the Chilean exception | The explanation is structural and historical | Lessons 18, 21 |
| 75 | Treating fragmentation and caudillismo as separate misfortunes | Loses the common cause | Both are outputs of how the wars were won | Lesson 18 |
| 76 | Omitting the Panama congress of 1826 | Loses the concrete form of the federation project | Attended by few, ratified by almost none | Lesson 18 |
| 77 | Presenting the commercial opening as imposed on the region | ✅ It was a central objective of the movements | Elites demanded it and benefited from it | Lesson 19 |
| 78 | Stating the dependency argument without its limits | Reads as a slogan | State it in five steps, then give the four limits | Lesson 19 |
| 79 | Omitting the customs-revenue mechanism | Loses the link between the economy and the weak state | States solvent only while trade flows | Lessons 18, 19 |
| 80 | Writing parallel descriptions and calling them a comparison | Leaves the comparison to the examiner | Run one question through several cases | Lesson 20 |
| 81 | Treating decolonisation as one process | ✅ The precise error the six-axis table exists to prevent | A settler-elite secession in 1820 and a mass movement in 1960 | Lesson 20 |
| 82 | Forcing a one-to-one analogy with Indian nationalism | ✅ Explicitly prohibited | Three clauses only: social base, chronology, mobilisation, institutional outcome | Lesson 20 |
| 83 | Naming historians from recollection | An invented attribution is an error in the most visible sentence | State positions by content and evidence | Lesson 21 |
| 84 | Choosing a side in a debate whose sides measure different things | Discards half the evidence | Name the level at which each side is right | Lesson 21 |
| 85 | Using counterfactuals that insert capacities nobody had | Speculation rather than causal analysis | Remove one documented decision and test what remains | Lesson 21 |
| 86 | Supplying casualty figures for any campaign or repression | None is verified for any of them | State the event, the policy and the recorded consequence | Every lesson |
| 87 | Supplying population, army, trade, loan or price figures | None is verified for this period here | Argue the mechanism without the number | Lessons 2, 15, 19 |
| 88 | Expanding narrative rather than adding contrast at 20 marks | Produces an inflated 15-mark script | ✅ Add Haiti, Brazil, caudillismo and a comparison | Lesson 22 |
| 89 | Ending with a summary rather than a verdict | A verdict decides and concedes; a summary repeats | If it could be written before the body, replace it | Lesson 22 |
| 90 | Writing about leaders when the question is about society | Answers a different question from the one set | ✅ The clause says "effect on the society" | Lessons 15, 17, 22 |

## Corrective drills

Attempt each in two or three sentences, from memory, before checking the lesson named in the error map.

### Drill 1 - one label, five operations

Name the five paths, state which four the 1808 crisis opened, and attach one distinguishing feature to each.

### Drill 2 - independence against revolution

Define the two words in one sentence each, and say which applies to mainland Spanish America and why.

### Drill 3 - the four ladders

Name the four hierarchies of colonial society and identify the one position where a group is simultaneously at the top and blocked.

### Drill 4 - the fiscal logic of caste

Explain in two sentences why a colonial state maintained legally distinct Indigenous status, and what that implies for post-independence reform.

### Drill 5 - reform as a predisposing cause

State the four rungs of the causal ladder and assign the Bourbon reforms and the crisis of 1808 to the correct rungs.

### Drill 6 - the false friend

Say what *comercio libre* actually permitted and what it did not, and why the distinction matters for the grievance.

### Drill 7 - the risings of the 1780s

Name the two revolts, their years and their fiscal origin, and give the three uses they have in an answer.

### Drill 8 - inspiration and causation

Write the three-clause sentence that assigns form, timing and limits to their respective causes.

### Drill 9 - four examples, four messages

State what the Enlightenment, 1776, 1789 and Haiti each supplied to a creole reader, distinguishing the encouraging from the deterrent.

### Drill 10 - the two Haitian dates

State what happened in 1791 and in 1804 and explain why conflating them is the most penalised factual error in the lesson.

### Drill 11 - why the restoration failed

Explain in three sentences why the attempted reversal of 1802 matters more analytically than the rising of 1791.

### Drill 12 - the price of recognition

State the year of French recognition of Haiti, the indemnity and its later reduction, and the one analytical use of the transaction.

### Drill 13 - the legitimacy chain

Draw the pre-1808 chain of authority and identify exactly where it was cut and what question that raised.

### Drill 14 - the four-part junta claim

State the four elements of the American junta claim and explain why they were conservative in form and revolutionary in effect.

### Drill 15 - why 1810 and not 1808

Explain the two-year gap using the Junta Central and the Regency.

### Drill 16 - the arithmetic trap

Explain how the exclusion of those of African descent from citizenship at Cadiz was also a device for controlling representation.

### Drill 17 - the hinge of 1814

State what the restoration did and why it converted autonomists into separatists.

### Drill 18 - the inversion of 1820

Explain how a liberal revolt in Spain made independence the conservative option in Mexico.

### Drill 19 - the Mexican first phase

Name the two insurgent leaders, their dates, and the four elements of the Morelos programme.

### Drill 20 - four reasons the insurgency lost

State them, and identify which one is structural rather than circumstantial.

### Drill 21 - the three guarantees

Name them, name the constituency each addressed, and name the omission each contains.

### Drill 22 - the curriculum of failure

For each of the two lost Venezuelan republics, state the diagnosis and the change adopted.

### Drill 23 - the llanero question

Explain why the plainsmen fought for the king in 1814 and for the republic afterwards.

### Drill 24 - the Haitian condition

State what Petion supplied in 1816, on what condition, and give the four analytical uses of the episode.

### Drill 25 - the strategic inversion

Explain in three sentences why the 1819 campaign went west rather than into Venezuela.

### Drill 26 - four dates of the northern war

Give Boyaca, Gran Colombia, Carabobo and Pichincha with their years and their functions.

### Drill 27 - why Gran Colombia failed

Name the three reasons and state the sentence that connects the failure to the method of victory.

### Drill 28 - the southern chain of reasoning

Set out the five-step argument that leads from the failure in Upper Peru to the crossing of the Andes.

### Drill 29 - the Chilean settlement

State what O'Higgins's government did, when it fell and to whom, and what the pattern illustrates.

### Drill 30 - the Peruvian exception

Give three reasons why the Peruvian elite preferred the empire.

### Drill 31 - Guayaquil discipline

State what is established about the meeting, and list the four competing explanations without choosing.

### Drill 32 - the Brazilian divergence

Name the five mechanisms that produced a monarchical separation rather than a war.

### Drill 33 - 1815 and 1888

Explain why these two Brazilian dates, taken together, make the best comparative point in the topic.

### Drill 34 - the market in allegiance

State what each side could offer to Indigenous communities, to free people of colour and to the enslaved, and why the offers differed.

### Drill 35 - three reasons for royalism

Name them, and add the corrective about local and changeable allegiance.

### Drill 36 - the Monroe grading

Write the one sentence that assigns the 1823 message its correct weight.

### Drill 37 - the abolition calendar

Recite the principal free-womb dates and the seven representative final abolition dates in order, and say why the list is illustrative rather than complete.

### Drill 38 - the tribute sequence

State who abolished tribute, who restored it and why, and what the sequence proves.

### Drill 39 - equality and dispossession

Explain in three sentences how one liberal reform produced both.

### Drill 40 - the caudillo mechanism

State the five-step chain without naming any individual.

### Drill 41 - four forces of fragmentation

Name them and attach one piece of evidence to each.

### Drill 42 - the Panama congress

Give the year, the purpose, the outcome and the three reasons it failed.

### Drill 43 - dependency in five steps

State the argument at its strongest, then give the four limits.

### Drill 44 - the customs state

Explain the three properties of a state financed by customs duties and their political consequences.

### Drill 45 - the six comparative axes

Recite the six-axis decolonisation comparison and state the purpose it serves.

### Drill 46 - the three-line device

Write the Haiti, Spanish America and Brazil lines, then add the Mexican fourth line.

### Drill 47 - the three syntheses

State the three debates and the synthesis recorded for each.

### Drill 48 - permitted and forbidden counterfactuals

Give two of each and state the rule that separates them.

### Drill 49 - the twelve controls

Recite the factual-risk controls and identify the four that are date controls.

### Drill 50 - the verdict test

Write a closing sentence for a social-outcome question, then test whether it could have been written before the body.

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Master chronology

```text
   BEFORE THE CRISIS - CONDITIONS AND WARNINGS
   1759  Jesuits expelled from Portuguese territories
   1767  Jesuits expelled from the Spanish empire
   1778  comercio libre decree - freer trade INSIDE the empire only
   1780-83  Andean rising under Jose Gabriel Condorcanqui, Tupac Amaru II;
            parallel Upper Peru movement under Tupac Katari; suppressed
   1781  Comuneros of New Granada - fiscal grievance, concessions repudiated

   THE HAITIAN SEQUENCE - THE REGION'S FIRST AND ONLY SOCIAL REVOLUTION
   1790-91  free-coloured claim for political rights, violently suppressed
   1791  ✅ HAITIAN REVOLUTION BEGINS - rising in the northern plain
   1793-94  Spanish and British intervention; French revolutionary abolition
   1801  autonomous constitution under Toussaint Louverture
   1802  French expedition attempts restoration; Toussaint seized
   1803  Toussaint dies in captivity in France
   1804  ✅ HAITI INDEPENDENT, 1 January, under Dessalines
   1816  Petion supplies Bolivar - on the condition of EMANCIPATION
   1825  French recognition purchased: 150 million francs (90 million, 1838)

   THE IBERIAN CRISIS AND THE CONSTITUTIONAL PHASE
   1807  Portuguese court sails from Lisbon under British escort
   1808  ✅ NAPOLEON INVADES SPAIN; Bayonne abdications; Joseph enthroned
         ✅ PORTUGUESE COURT REACHES RIO; Brazilian ports opened
         Spanish provincial juntas; Junta Central
   1810  Regency replaces the Junta Central
         ✅ REVOLTS BEGIN IN SPANISH AMERICA; American juntas form
         Cortes summoned at Cadiz
   1811  Venezuelan independence declared, 5 July
   1812  CONSTITUTION OF 1812 - Spaniards of both hemispheres; tribute
         abolished; proportionate representation REFUSED
         First Venezuelan Republic falls; Cartagena Manifesto
   1813  Admirable Campaign; Second Republic; war to the death
         Mexican congress of Chilpancingo declares independence
   1814  ✅ FERDINAND VII RESTORED; constitution annulled; reconquest ordered
         Second Venezuelan Republic falls to llanero forces under Boves
         Mexican charter of Apatzingan
   1815  Jamaica Letter; Morelos executed
         Brazil raised to the status of a KINGDOM

   THE MILITARY DECISION
   1817  ✅ SAN MARTIN CROSSES THE ANDES; Chacabuco, 12 February
         Bolivar establishes the base at Angostura
   1818  Chilean independence declared, 12 February; Maipu, 5 April
         Bolivar allies with Paez and the llaneros
   1819  Congress of Angostura, February
         ✅ BOYACA, 7 AUGUST - NEW GRANADA SECURED
         ✅ GRAN COLOMBIA PROCLAIMED, December
   1820  ✅ RIEGO'S REVOLT restores the Constitution of 1812
         Southern expedition sails from Chile for Peru
         Porto revolt; Portuguese Cortes demands recolonisation
   1821  ✅ CARABOBO, 24 June - Venezuela decided
         ✅ PLAN OF IGUALA, 24 February; ✅ TREATY OF CORDOBA, August
         ✅ MEXICO INDEPENDENT; Central America separates
         Lima entered July; Peruvian independence proclaimed; Protectorate
         Joao VI returns to Portugal, leaving Pedro as regent
   1822  ✅ PICHINCHA, 24 May - Sucre secures Quito

            crowned Emperor, December

         ✅ MONROE MESSAGE, 2 December

         Mexican federal republic constituted
   1825  ✅ BOLIVIA EMERGES; Portugal recognises Brazil

         Last royalist garrisons surrender

   THE AFTERMATH

   1838-41 Central American union breaks up
```
