## The causal engine

```text
   THE QUESTION: why did two empires lose a hemisphere in one generation,
   and why did the societies that emerged look so much like the ones
   that went in?

   [1] BACKGROUND CONDITION - present for two centuries
       office reserved for peninsulares | legal caste order | hacienda,
       plantation, tribute and labour drafts | commercial monopoly
       => produces resentment WITHOUT an exit
                     |
   [2] PREDISPOSING CAUSE - c. 1750-1808
       Bourbon and Pombaline reform: more tax, more control, fewer creole
       offices, freer trade inside the empire only, Jesuits expelled
       => converts resentment into a SPECIFIC, DOCUMENTED grievance
       AND, through 1780-83 and 1781, teaches the elite to FEAR the mass
                     |
   [3] ENABLING CONDITION - available from 1776 onward
       Enlightenment vocabulary | 1776 demonstration | 1789 programme and
       warning | 1791-1804 Haiti as the decisive deterrent
       => determines the FORM the demands will take, not that they occur
                     |
   [4] PRECIPITANT - 1808
       legitimacy collapses AT THE CENTRE before it is attacked in the
       colonies => THE OPENING
                     |
   [5] THE CONSTITUTIONAL PHASE - 1808-1814
       juntas claim kingdom status -> Spain concedes words and refuses
       arithmetic at Cadiz -> 1814 annulment by decree
       => AUTONOMY BECOMES UNAVAILABLE; INDEPENDENCE IS WHAT IS LEFT
                     |
   [6] THE WARS - 1810-1826, fought as CIVIL wars
       both sides bid for the same populations
       the crown can offer PROTECTION OF WHAT EXISTS
       the patriots can offer REMOVAL OF WHAT OPPRESSES
       -> the patriot side cannot outbid without attacking its own base
       -> it therefore offers freedom FOR SERVICE, rank and caste abolition
          and NOT land, tribute security or general emancipation
                     |
   [7] THE SETTLEMENT - the outcome follows the coalition
       bottom-led (Haiti)        -> slavery destroyed, order replaced
       elite-led (Spanish main)  -> sovereignty transferred, order kept
       dynasty-led (Brazil)      -> monarchy and slavery both kept
       reversed (Mexico)         -> radical phase defeated, then a
                                    conservative independence
                     |
   [8] THE LEGACY - the method determines the state
       regional armed commanders -> caudillismo
       colonial administrative units -> separate states; federation fails
       destroyed revenue + no direct tax -> customs-financed states
       narrow primary exports -> policy hostage to world prices
                     |
   ==> POLITICAL DECOLONISATION OUTRAN SOCIAL DEMOCRATISATION
```

## The five paths, compared on seven axes

| Axis | Haiti | Mexico | Northern South America | Southern South America | Brazil |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Dates** | ✅ 1791-1804 | ✅ 1810-1821 | ✅ 1810-1825 | ✅ 1810-1824 | ✅ 1807-1822 |
| **Who led** | The enslaved majority | A parish priest, then a royalist officer | Creole leadership with a llanero alliance | A professional officer and a purpose-built army | The heir to the Portuguese throne |
| **Relation to the 1808 trigger** | Preceded it | Followed it | Followed it | Followed it | Inverted it - the court relocated |
| **Method** | Revolutionary war against an attempted restoration | Popular rising defeated, then an elite settlement | Coalition warfare from an interior base | Prepared projection from an external base, then sea power | Declaration by the resident dynasty |
| **Constitutional outcome** | Republic | Monarchy 1822, republic 1824 | Republics; a failed union | Republics | ✅ Monarchy retained |
| **Effect on slavery** | ✅ Abolished immediately | Abolished 1829 | Free-womb 1821; abolition 1851-54 | Free-womb 1813 and 1821; abolition 1853-54 | ✅ Retained to 1888 |
| **Effect on the ruling group** | Replaced | Confirmed | ✅ Confirmed | Confirmed | Confirmed |

## Argument map: was it a revolution?

```text
                    WAS LATIN AMERICAN INDEPENDENCE A REVOLUTION?
                                      |
        +-----------------------------+-----------------------------+
        |                                                           |
   YES - AT THE LEVEL OF SOVEREIGNTY              NO - AT THE LEVEL OF SOCIAL POWER
   two empires lost a hemisphere                  creoles led, financed and officered,
   colonial status ended                          and became the ruling class
   republics with written constitutions,          hierarchy survived in practice
   elected congresses, declared rights            emancipation immediate only in Haiti
   caste distinctions abolished in law            tribute abolished and reimposed
        |                                         community land lost its protection
        |                                         social power stayed in land
        |                                                           |
        +-----------------------------+-----------------------------+
                                      |
                        THE TEST THAT DECIDES THE QUESTION
             WAS THE SOCIAL LIMIT A NECESSITY OR A CHOICE?
                                      |
        +-----------------------------+-----------------------------+
        |                                                           |
   NECESSITY                                              CHOICE
   no state anywhere redistributed                 HAITI did it, in this region,
   land or abolished slavery instantly             in this period, from below
   in the 1820s                                    MEXICO'S FIRST PHASE proposed it
                                                   in constitutional form, 1813-14,
                                                   and was DEFEATED by a coalition
                                                   including the creole elite
        |                                                           |
        +-----------------------------+-----------------------------+
                                      |
                      ✅ SYNTHESIS: POLITICAL DECOLONISATION
                         OUTRAN SOCIAL DEMOCRATISATION
             a real revolution in sovereignty; a deliberate
             conservation in society - and the alternative
             was defeated, not absent
```

## Argument map: why the states were weak

```text
   CLAIM: the new states were unstable because of a regional
          incapacity for self-government
                          |
                          X  REJECTED - ✅ explicitly prohibited;
                             circular; and refuted by the recorded
                             exception of Chile
                          |
   CLAIM: the new states were unstable because the wars bankrupted them
                          |
                          ~  PARTLY - explains the DEPTH of the initial
                             collapse; does not explain PERSISTENCE, since
                             ✅ cheap abundant labour, not war damage, is
                             the recorded reason agriculture did not
                             modernise; and does not explain the FORM
                          |
   CLAIM: the new states were unstable because of how the wars were won
          and what colonial rule had failed to build
                          |
                          >  ACCEPTED, with the fiscal factor attached
                             ✅ long wars -> authority held by commanders
                             ✅ no tradition of colonial self-government
                                comparable to the North American assemblies
                             ✅ regional landowners fund personal armed
                                followings
                             ✅ personal loyalty replaces institutional
                                legitimacy => caudillismo
                             + revenue rebuilt on customs, so the state is
                               solvent only while trade flows
                          |
   ==> STATES BEFORE NATIONS, ARMIES BEFORE INSTITUTIONS
```

## Answer-writing spine

```text
   OPEN      span (c. 1808-1826) + trigger (legitimacy collapsed at the
             centre) + count (FIVE paths; 1808 opened FOUR) + THESIS
                          |
   GROUND    the order that had to be broken: office, caste, land and
             labour, trade - and the mismatch that made creoles act
                          |
   EXPLAIN   the causal ladder: background -> predisposing -> enabling
             -> precipitant. Never four items of the same kind.
                          |
   EVIDENCE  the paths, compressed to functions and dates, not narrated:
             Haiti 1804 | Mexico 1810 and 1821 | Boyaca 1819 and Carabobo
             1821 | Andes 1817 and Ayacucho 1824 | Brazil 1822
                          |
   TURN      what did NOT change - the audit: caste abolished in law;
             emancipation 1823 to 1888; tribute abolished and reimposed;
             community land lost; power with creole elites
                          |
   DEEPEN    (15 and 20 marks) the contrast that proves the limit was a
             choice: Haiti and Mexico's first phase; then caudillismo,
             fragmentation and export dependence as legacy
                          |
   COMPARE   (20 marks) one disciplined comparison - the six axes against
             post-1945 decolonisation, or Brazil against Spanish America
                          |
   VERDICT   decide and concede in one sentence. If it could have been
             written before the body, it is a summary - replace it.
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. What the phrase names, and the count discipline

- ✅ Region: South America, Central America including Mexico, Caribbean islands including Hispaniola - divided into Haiti and the Dominican Republic.
- ✅ Independence from Spain; ✅ from Portugal in the case of Brazil; early nineteenth century.
- ✅ Span c. 1808-1826; most mainland Spanish colonies independent by the mid-1820s. Cuba is inside the region and outside this period.
- ⚠️ **Five paths, one trigger that opened four of them:** Haiti, Mexico, northern South America, southern South America, Brazil; the Iberian crisis of 1808 opened all but Haiti, whose revolution began in 1791 from a different trigger. ❌ Never four; ❌ never six.
- ⚠️ Independence = transfer of sovereignty. Revolution = transformation of social power. Secession = withdrawal of a territory and its existing ruling group. Decolonisation = the general process, covering both a settler-elite secession and a mass anti-colonial movement.
- ✅ Owners' verdict: political decolonisation outran social democratisation; creole-led states retained major social inequalities.
- ⚠️ The wars were substantially civil wars inside American society.

## 2. The ten-marker chronological spine

- ✅ 1791 Haitian Revolution begins · 1804 Haiti independent · 1808 Napoleon invades Spain and the Portuguese court shifts to Brazil · 1810 revolts begin and Hidalgo issues the Grito de Dolores · 1817 San Martin crosses the Andes · 1819 Bolivar secures New Granada and builds Gran Colombia · 1821 Mexico independent · 1822 Brazil independent under Pedro I · 1824 Ayacucho breaks royalist power · 1825 Bolivia emerges.
- ⚠️ The spine already argues: the social revolution is first and outside the trigger; the trigger is external; Mexico and Brazil arrive in consecutive years by opposite methods.

## 3. The colonial order that had to be broken

- ✅ Peninsulares: Europeans born in Spain or Portugal; held the top offices; resented by creoles.
- ✅ Creoles: American-born whites; wealthy but politically restricted; became the leading independence elites and ✅ the ruling class.
- ✅ Mestizos, Indigenous peoples and enslaved Africans: socially and politically subordinate; a mass base existed; benefits uneven.
- ✅ Vocabulary: peninsular, creole, mestizo, mulatto/pardo, Indigenous, enslaved, free person of colour - legal and fiscal statuses created by a colonial state, defined on first use, never used to impute motive without a recorded action.
- ✅ Institutions: encomienda, hacienda, mita, tribute, community land, plantation.
- ⚠️ Four unaligned ladders - office, caste, land and labour, trade - and the creole mismatch of social supremacy with political exclusion is the engine.
- ⚠️ The caste order was fiscally productive; colonial protection of community land was revenue policy, not benevolence.
- ✅ Trade restriction: monopoly and regulation bred resentment; economic grievance mattered.
- ⚠️ The creole programme answered only the creole grievance, so participation from below had to be purchased.

## 4. Imperial reform and the two warnings of the 1780s

- ✅ Bourbon reforms, most intensive under Charles III: new viceroyalties of New Granada and the River Plate; intendancies; reduced creole share of senior and *audiencia* office; fiscal intensification with tobacco and spirit monopolies; militia expansion; ✅ expulsion of the Jesuits, **1759** in the Portuguese empire and **1767** in the Spanish; ✅ *comercio libre* decree of **1778** - freer trade **inside** the empire, ❌ not free trade.
- ✅ Pombaline counterpart in the Portuguese empire; capital moved to Rio de Janeiro - the precondition for 1808.
- ✅ **1780-83** Andean rising under Jose Gabriel Condorcanqui, taking the name **Tupac Amaru II**, with a parallel Upper Peru movement under **Tupac Katari**; suppressed with great severity. ✅ **1781** Comuneros of New Granada; concessions granted and repudiated.
- ⚠️ Three uses of the revolts: grievance reached below the elite; creole fear of mass mobilisation; concession-then-repudiation as a recurring imperial method.
- ⚠️ Causal ladder: background condition -> predisposing cause -> enabling condition -> precipitant (✅ 1808).

## 5. Ideas and examples, under the inspiration-is-not-causation rule

- ✅ Enlightenment, the American Revolution, the French Revolution and Haiti are recorded influences supplying intellectual and political inspiration.
- ⚠️ Decisive borrowed doctrine: sovereignty residing in the nation rather than in the monarch's person - fused with an older Spanish tradition that authority reverts to the kingdoms in the king's absence.
- ⚠️ 1776 = demonstration effect for a settler-descended propertied elite; three limits - no comparable tradition of legislative self-government, a legally stratified majority non-white society, and Spain's own earlier support for the North American rebels.
- ⚠️ 1789 has two faces - a universal programme and a warning about uncontrollable mobilisation - and it produced the Haitian crisis, which for creole readers was one story.
- ⚠️ Haiti was chiefly a deterrent to mainland elites and an inspiration to those they ruled.
- ⚠️ The memorisable line: the Atlantic revolutions gave the region its political language and constitutional models; 1808 gave it its opportunity; colonial social structure gave it its limits.
- ⚠️ No Enlightenment author or revolutionary document is quoted anywhere in this session.

## 6. Haiti: the control case

- ✅ Revolution begins **1791**; independence **1804**. ❌ Never conflate.
- ✅ First major anti-slavery revolutionary break in the region; ✅ first free Black republic of the modern era; ✅ first state founded by former slaves; ✅ the most radical social revolution in the region; ✅ the only case where independence *was* social revolution.
- ✅ Leaders recorded: Toussaint Louverture, Dessalines and others.
- ✅ Saint-Domingue, western third of Hispaniola; free-coloured claim 1790-91; northern rising August 1791; Spanish and British intervention from 1793; French revolutionary abolition 1793-94; Toussaint dominant in the later 1790s; autonomous constitution 1801; French expedition 1802; Toussaint dies in captivity 1803; independence 1 January 1804 under Dessalines.
- ⚠️ The attempted restoration of 1802 is what converts revolt into revolution; independence became the demand only after reversal was attempted.
- ✅ French recognition **1825** for an indemnity of **150 million francs**, reduced to **90 million** in **1838**.
- ⚠️ Three prohibited moves: footnoting Haiti; dating independence to 1791; reading later poverty backwards as a verdict on radicalism.

## 7. The imperial crisis of 1808 and the junta claim

- ✅ Britannica links Latin American independence directly to the shock of Napoleon's intervention in Spain; ✅ the colonies began to form juntas and challenge old authority; ✅ San Martin's career turns decisively after the occupation of 1808.
- ✅ Owners' formulation: **legitimacy collapsed at the centre before it was attacked in the colonies**.
- ✅ Bayonne abdications; Joseph enthroned; Spanish provincial juntas for Ferdinand VII; Junta Central 1808-09; Regency 1810; Cortes at Cadiz 1810; restoration 1814; Riego's revolt 1820.
- ✅ The Portuguese court sailed late 1807 under British escort and reached Rio in 1808; Brazilian ports opened.
- ⚠️ The four-part junta claim: sovereignty reverts to the communities; the Americas are kingdoms, not possessions; they may form juntas as Spanish provinces did; they hold authority for Ferdinand VII.
- ⚠️ Legally conservative and politically revolutionary at once; the origin of royalist America as a **constitutional** position.
- ⚠️ Central mechanism: crisis -> autonomy claim -> refusal -> war -> hardening -> independence.
- ✅ Why 1810 and not 1808: the Regency removed the last authority whose claim beat a local junta's.

## 8. Cadiz, and the road not taken

- ✅ Cortes summoned 1810; **Constitution of 1812**; national sovereignty; limited monarchy; single legislature; press freedom; the nation comprises **Spaniards of both hemispheres**; ✅ Indigenous tribute and labour drafts abolished.
- ✅ Proportionate representation **refused**; those of African descent excluded from citizenship and from the population base used to calculate seats; ✅ free trade with foreign nations refused.
- ⚠️ The representation question and the race question were one question; the pattern is that everything costless was conceded and everything that transferred power or revenue was refused.
- ✅ **1814** restoration annuls the constitution and orders reconquest - the hinge at which autonomy ceases to be rational. ✅ **1820** Riego's revolt restores it, diverting reconquest, producing armistices, and inverting Mexican alignments.
- ⚠️ Inevitability verdict: not inevitable in 1810; close to inevitable after 1814. The variable is guarantee, not sentiment.

## 9. Mexico: rising, defeat, reversal

- ✅ Hidalgo issued the **Grito de Dolores** in **1810**; sequence Hidalgo -> Morelos -> Iturbide; ✅ independence **1821**; ✅ the sharpest reversal in the region.
- ✅ Hidalgo, parish priest at Dolores, 16 September 1810; Guanajuato stormed; executed 1811; decrees abolishing slavery and tribute and restoring community land.
- ✅ **Morelos**; ✅ congress of **Chilpancingo 1813** declaring independence; ✅ charter of **Apatzingan 1814**; executed 1815; guerrilla continuation under **Guerrero**. Programme: independence, popular sovereignty, abolition of slavery, abolition of caste, abolition of tribute, limits on great estates.
- ⚠️ The only mainland movement combining independence with a constitutional social programme - a second control case beside Haiti, which lost. Four reasons: elite opposition produced by its own programme; royalist command of treasury, silver and regular forces; geographical confinement; no external supply.
- ✅ **Plan of Iguala, 24 February 1821** - religion, independence under a monarchy, union with caste abolition; **Army of the Three Guarantees**; ✅ **Treaty of Cordoba**, August 1821; Iturbide emperor 1822, deposed 1823; federal republic 1824; Central America separates, is annexed, federates and fragments.
- ⚠️ Caste abolition was cheap and land reform was not. The formulation: in Mexico, independence was not the victory of the revolution but the terms on which the revolution was ended.

## 10. The northern campaigns

- ✅ Bolivar is the leading figure of northern South American independence; Venezuela, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru and Bolivia are tied to his campaigns; ✅ president of Gran Colombia.
- ✅ Caracas junta April 1810; independence declared 5 July 1811; **First Republic falls 1812**; **Cartagena Manifesto 1812**; **Admirable Campaign 1813**; **war to the death**; **Second Republic falls 1814** to llanero forces under **Boves**.
- ⚠️ The llanero mobilisation is decisive evidence of civil war: a large mixed-descent population chose the monarchy over a creole republic.
- ✅ **Jamaica Letter 1815**; **1816** Petion of Haiti supplies asylum, arms and ships on the condition of **emancipation**.
- ✅ **Angostura** base 1817-18; alliance with **Paez**; **British Legion**; **Congress of Angostura** February 1819; ✅ **Boyaca 7 August 1819**; ✅ **Gran Colombia** December 1819; constitution of Cucuta 1821 with **Santander** as vice-president; ✅ **Carabobo 24 June 1821**; ✅ **Pichincha 24 May 1822** under **Sucre**; ✅ Gran Colombia dissolves **1830**.
- ⚠️ Four mechanisms of victory: a base beyond naval reach; the social bargain with the interior; external supply and credit; surprise of axis. The second built the caudillo problem into the state.
- ❌ Do not write that Bolivar permanently united Spanish America; ✅ his integrative vision did not endure politically.

## 11. The southern campaign and Chile

- ✅ San Martin is the key southern liberator linked with Argentina, Chile and Peru; ✅ crossing of the Andes **1817**; ✅ disciplined regular-army liberation from an external base.
- ✅ Buenos Aires junta May 1810; expeditions into Upper Peru defeated 1811-15; Cuyo governorship; **Army of the Andes** at **Mendoza** 1814-16; **Congress of Tucuman** 1816; **Chacabuco 12 February 1817**; **O'Higgins** Supreme Director; Chilean independence **12 February 1818**; **Maipu 5 April 1818**; squadron under **Cochrane**; expedition sails **1820**.
- ⚠️ The strategic chain: Peru is unreachable by land from the south -> attack from the sea -> a Pacific coast is needed -> Chile -> the Andes. Chile was the means, Peru the objective.
- ⚠️ Enslaved men enlisted in the River Plate with the promise of freedom formed a substantial part of the infantry.
- ✅ O'Higgins abolished titles of nobility and moved against slavery; his government fell in 1823 to conservative and landowning pressure.
- ✅ Core source: no tradition of democracy in the region **except in Chile**.

## 12. Peru, Guayaquil and the end of the mainland war

- ⚠️ Peru is the exception because its elite's interest lay with the empire: proximity to office and honours, a mining economy, and the memory of the Andean rising.
- ✅ Expedition lands **1820**; blockade and negotiation; **Lima entered and independence proclaimed July 1821**; San Martin becomes **Protector**; decrees a **free-womb law** and abolishes **tribute** and the labour draft; deadlock.
- ✅ **Guayaquil, 26-27 July 1822**; content not made public; San Martin resigns and leaves America. ❌ Never assert what was said; four candidate explanations - reinforcements, monarchy against republic, unified command, deliberate withdrawal.
- ✅ Bolivar granted extraordinary powers 1823-24; **Junin 6 August 1824**; ✅ **Ayacucho 9 December 1824** under **Sucre**; viceroy captured; ✅ **Bolivia 1825**; garrisons surrender by 1826.
- ✅ Date control: ❌ never vary 1824 for Ayacucho or 1825 for Bolivia.
- ⚠️ General proposition: where a patriot cause had no domestic constituency it could only be established by an external army with political powers.

## 13. Brazil: the divergent path

- ✅ 1808 the Portuguese court shifts to Brazil; ✅ **1822** Brazil independent under **Pedro I**; ✅ monarchical and comparatively non-violent; ✅ slavery persisted long after independence. ❌ Do not say Brazil followed the Spanish American route.
- ✅ Court sails late 1807; arrives 1808; **ports opened to friendly nations**; institutions established at Rio; ✅ **1815** Brazil raised to the status of a **kingdom**; **1820-21** Porto revolt and the recolonisation demand; **Joao VI returns 1821** leaving **Pedro** as regent; **January 1822** the **Fico**; **7 September 1822** Ipiranga; **December 1822** crowned emperor; **1824** granted constitution with a **moderating power** and provincial revolt suppressed; **1825** Portuguese recognition under British mediation with compensation; **1831** abdication; ✅ **1888** abolition.
- ⚠️ Five divergence mechanisms: sovereign present; commercial grievance removed in 1808; colonial status removed in 1815; a domestic head of state available; elite fear of upheaval in a slaveholding society answered by monarchy.
- ⚠️ Brazil bought continuity and paid for it in the currency of the people it kept enslaved.
- ✅ The three-line device: Haiti = anti-colonial and anti-slavery social revolution; mainland Spanish America = anti-colonial, often creole-led political revolution; Brazil = monarchical separation from Portugal. ⚠️ Add Mexico as the fourth line.

## 14. Participants and the market in allegiance

- ✅ Creoles supplied leadership, finance and officers and became the ruling class; ✅ mestizos supplied much of the fighting strength and gained formal legal change without social ascent; ✅ Indigenous peoples were mobilised in Mexico under Hidalgo and faced continuing or worsening land pressure; ✅ enslaved Africans were decisive only in Haiti.
- ✅ Both sides recruited enslaved men with the promise of freedom for service; free people of colour served extensively on both sides; women organised conspiracies, carried intelligence, financed, supplied, sustained armies and in cases fought and commanded, with several executed for conspiracy - ⚠️ and their legal and political status did not change with independence.
- ✅ The church remained a powerful old-authority structure and divided by rank: hierarchy largely royalist, parish clergy leading the Mexican insurgency.
- ✅ Monopoly-port merchants generally royalist; open-trade merchants and London credit financed the patriots; foreign volunteers supplied trained infantry, artillery and naval command.
- ⚠️ Three reasons royalist America was large - interest, legitimacy, fear - with the corrective that allegiance was local, pragmatic and changeable.
- ⚠️ The asymmetry: the crown could promise protection of what existed; the patriots could promise removal of what oppressed.

## 15. The outside world

- ✅ Four British forms of involvement: commercial, military and naval, financial, diplomatic; recognition in the mid-1820s. ⚠️ Interested, not benevolent; a political monopoly replaced by a commercial preponderance.
- ✅ United States recognition from **1822**; **Monroe message, 2 December 1823** - no future European colonisation; extension of the European political system to the Americas dangerous to United States peace and safety; no interference with existing colonies; no part in European wars. ✅ **Canning** proposed a joint declaration and was declined.
- ⚠️ Four observations: policy not law; unenforceable by the United States in 1823; not solicited by or binding toward the new republics; its coercive meaning is later and owned elsewhere. The graded sentence: it announced a principle that British sea power was already enforcing for its own reasons.
- ✅ A French army restored Ferdinand VII's absolute authority in Spain in **1823**; a comparable American intervention was discussed and never executed. ⚠️ Boundary: the European congress system belongs to the Vienna owner.
- ⚠️ Haiti's exception: assistance on an abolitionist condition; recognition only on payment. The international order was not neutral between social outcomes.

## 16. The social audit

- ✅ Emancipation was immediate only in Haiti; elsewhere piecemeal and later. ❌ Do not say independence abolished slavery.
- ✅ Three instruments: freedom for military service; **free-womb laws** - River Plate 1813, Gran Colombia 1821, Peru 1821, Brazil 1871; final abolition - **Chile 1823, Mexico 1829, Colombia 1851, Argentina 1853, Peru and Venezuela 1854, Cuba 1886, Brazil 1888**.
- ✅ Tribute abolished by Cadiz, by Hidalgo, by San Martin's protectorate and by several republics - and restored by several of the same republics for fiscal reasons.
- ⚠️ Community land: abolishing Indigenous legal separateness removed the legal basis of collective tenure; ✅ land pressure continued or worsened. The instrument was liberal and the effect regressive.
- ⚠️ The pattern divides by cost: costless promises kept, asset-transferring promises delayed, reversed or never attempted.
- ⚠️ Verdict: a genuine legal revolution and a negligible social one - independence changed what people were called and not what they owned. ⚠️ Qualification: by the standards of the 1820s the legal settlement was advanced, and the declared equality became the standard for later movements.

## 17. States before nations

- ✅ **Caudillismo** = personalist rule by a military strongman resting on armed clientage rather than institutions; the region's characteristic nineteenth-century political form. ✅ The chain: long wars -> authority held by commanders -> weak inherited institutions -> regional landowners fund personal armed followings -> personal loyalty replaces institutional legitimacy.
- ✅ Source control: the term is sourced and may be named with its mechanism; ❌ no individual caudillo, country, date or career.
- ✅ Why here and not in British North America: no tradition of colonial self-government comparable to the colonial assemblies; states with armies but without an established civilian political class.
- ✅ Fragmentation: Gran Colombia dissolved **1830**; the Central American union of **1823** broke up **1838-41**; ✅ distance, weak communications and regional elite interests defeated continental federation.
- ✅ **Congress of Panama, 1826**, proposed in the Jamaica Letter of 1815; limited attendance; effectively unratified.
- 📰 ✅ Current linkage: Panama designated **2026** the Year of the Bicentennial of the Amphictyonic Congress, with a high-level week of 20-27 June 2026, more than eighty delegations, the 56th General Assembly of the Organization of American States and an exhibition tracing the 1826 congress to the inter-American system. ⚠️ Carries no historical fact.
- ⚠️ Four forces of fragmentation: geography; colonial administrative inheritance; regional elites; armed commanders. The map was drawn by administrative boundaries, not by nations.
- ✅ Honest limit: no tradition of democracy **except in Chile**. ❌ Cultural explanation is prohibited.

## 18. The economy after independence

- ✅ Four developments: war damage to mines, herds, trade and revenue; commercial opening; the London loan boom of the 1820s and widespread default; fiscal reconstruction on customs duties.
- ⚠️ Customs-financed states are solvent only while trade flows, interested in export expansion whatever the social cost, and under-incentivised to build administrative capacity.
- ✅ Core source: heavy dependence on a limited range of products, sometimes a single commodity, so that a fall in its world price would be a major disaster; ✅ agriculture remained backward because peasant labour was plentiful and cheap; ✅ the new states were underdeveloped industrially and agriculturally with massive poverty and illiteracy and unstable political systems.
- ⚠️ The dependency argument in five steps, then four limits - elite agency, variation, chronology and an unspecified counterfactual.
- ⚠️ Verdict: the argument identifies the structure correctly and overreaches as a complete explanation. ⚠️ Boundary: the twentieth-century sequel is owned elsewhere.

## 19. Comparison

- ⚠️ Comparison is one question run through several cases; two axes - depth of social change and social composition of the leadership - and they are not independent.
- ✅ Against 1776: the same type - settler-elite secession - with a different institutional inheritance; fragmentation against union; caudillismo against institutional politics.
- ⚠️ Against 1789: a revolution changes who owns; a war of independence changes who governs.
- ✅ The six-axis decolonisation table: trigger, who led, colonial type, method, social outcome, post-independence problem; ✅ its stated purpose is to prevent treating decolonisation as one process.
- ⚠️ The parallel that holds: inherited administrative borders kept because revision meant universal conflict. ⚠️ The divergence that matters: later movements mobilised mass constituencies and carried social programmes.
- ✅ Indian contrast in three clauses only - social base, chronology, mode of mobilisation, institutional outcome. ❌ No one-to-one analogy.

## 20. Debates, attribution and counterfactuals

- ✅ Debate 1 nature: liberation of peoples against transfer of elite power; ✅ synthesis - political decolonisation outran social democratisation.
- ✅ Debate 2 social depth: Haiti proves transformation was possible against mainland oligarchy; ✅ synthesis - distinguish Haiti from the creole-led mainland cases.
- ✅ Debate 3 legacy: republican constitutions spread against stable democracy remaining weak; ✅ synthesis - institutions were fragile and society remained unequal.
- ⚠️ Elite transfer is an accurate description of the outcome and an inadequate account of the process - it understates the legal transformation, ignores internal variation, reads outcome back as intention and cannot explain American royalism.
- ⚠️ Attribution limit: this session names no historian; state positions by content and evidence.
- ✅ Permitted counterfactuals: no 1808 collapse; a genuinely equal Cadiz; no annulment in 1814. ⚠️ Restricted: federal Gran Colombia; early general emancipation. ❌ Forbidden: nineteenth-century industrialisation; a successful Holy Alliance intervention asserted as fact.

## 21. Answer architecture

- ✅ Clause: colonization, decolonization, **forms** and **effect on the society**.
- ✅ Directive map: causes and course; why not equality; compare the liberators; compare with Asian and African decolonisation; assess Haiti; caudillismo and instability - plus stable democracy and the "politically revolutionary, socially incomplete" framing.
- ✅ Thesis templates: core paradox; causation; Haiti contrast; instability.
- ✅ Mark-scaled structures with word budgets - 150 words at 10 marks, 250 at 15 and 20; at 20 add contrast, not narrative.
- ✅ Verdict scaffolds for causes and course, social equality, and the comparison of the liberators, plus two built here for "was it a revolution" and "why no stable democracy".
- ⚠️ Verdict test: if it could have been written before the body, it is a summary.

## 23. Prohibited formulations

1. ❌ Haitian independence dated to 1791.
2. ❌ Brazil following the Spanish American route.
3. ❌ Independence abolishing slavery across the region.
4. ❌ A number for the new states, population figures or army sizes.
5. ❌ Bolivar and San Martin operating in the same theatre in the same way.
6. ❌ Naming an individual caudillo with his country, dates or career.
7. ❌ Describing the five paths as four or six, or extending the 1808 trigger to Haiti.
8. ❌ Ayacucho other than 1824, or Bolivia other than 1825.
9. ❌ Bolivar permanently uniting Spanish America.
10. ❌ Latin American independence "completing democracy".
11. ❌ A cultural explanation of the region's instability.
12. ❌ A named historian, a casualty figure for any campaign or repression, or any trade, loan, revenue or price figure.
13. ❌ Asserting the content of the Guayaquil conversation.
14. ❌ A one-to-one analogy with Indian nationalism.

## Construction record for this edition

| Item | Status |
|---|---|
| Lesson count | Derived from the topic's own dependency chain and both owners rather than from a template; twenty-two lessons, with the colonial order and the reform programme taught before any cause, Haiti taught in its own right before the mainland crisis, the five paths taught separately before comparison, and the outcomes taught as consequences of method |
| Compiled four-option MCQ corpus | Not constructed, in accordance with the concept-check rule for this generation; one answer-free concept check, model answer and misconception note per lesson, plus eight cumulative checks across four blocks and fifty corrective drills |
| Workbook operations | None performed; no MCQ corpus was created, copied or relocated, and no solved-practice workbook was generated or migrated |
| Solved PYQ model answers | Not included; ✅ the topic owns no verified question, no objective item touching its names was found in any ledger, and none is displayed, reconstructed or keyed |
| Bounded History research rule | Applied. The substantive base is the complete canonical basic file, the complete canonical advanced file and one core OCR history source. External work was confined to exact question verification and routing, one bounded verification of a pre-declared list of standard connecting names and dates absent from those two files, and one current linkage; the provenance of that verification is recorded once, in the source ledger. Exactly one linkage is carried, in Lesson 18; the other twenty-one checklists record truthfully that no current affairs were sought. No per-lesson searches and no optional corroboration were performed |
| Connecting names and dates | Supplied and taught as ordinary verified history, without provenance labels in the lessons. Their provenance is recorded once, in the single table in the source ledger |
| Material disputed or unestablished points | Handled openly rather than silently resolved: the content of the Guayaquil meeting is not established and four candidate explanations are presented without choice; the relative weight of resistance and disease in defeating the French expedition in Haiti is not apportioned; the classification of Haiti as inside or outside this topic is recorded as a historian's convenience; and the Chilean exception is recorded and left to its owner |
| Chronology and numeric verification | Every date in this session was checked against the canonical file that holds it, or independently verified where those files carry no date. **The only quantitative figures used anywhere as historical evidence are the Haitian indemnity of 150 million francs and its reduction to 90 million in 1838.** That narrower claim deliberately excludes the quantities reported inside the single current linkage - the more than eighty delegations and the 56th General Assembly of the Organization of American States in June 2026 - which are present-day reporting, carry no historical claim, and are used for nothing beyond describing that commemoration |
| Named historians | None. The two owners record debate positions without attaching them to scholars, and the core OCR source names none for this period; the attribution limit is stated in Lesson 21 and enforced throughout |
| Figures deliberately refused | No casualty figure for any rising, campaign, massacre or repression; no force strength or order of battle; no population, enslaved-population, manumission, compensation or land figure; no trade, loan, default, customs, revenue or commodity-price figure; no count of new states; no participation figure for women, Indigenous communities or foreign volunteers |
| Sensitive-identity and slavery controls | Colonial racial categories are taught as legal and fiscal statuses created by a colonial state, defined on first use, with regional variation stated rather than averaged, and with group motives asserted only from recorded actions, decrees or petitions. Enslaved populations are described by what they did and what was done to them under named policies, never by collective characterisation |
| Boundary discipline | Six boundaries stated in the opening, with the seventh - Indian nationalism - introduced in Lesson 22; applied in Lessons 4, 16, 19, 20 and 22 and in the question index, and restated in the register notes; the American Revolution, the French Revolution, the Vienna settlement and Concert of Europe, New Imperialism, twentieth-century Latin America, post-1945 decolonisation and Indian nationalism are routed rather than annexed |
| Lesson architecture | Measured rather than asserted. Every lesson carries the four mandatory blocks - progress line, pre-teach checklist, concept-appropriate visual, and a single concept check with model answer and misconception note - so those four are deliberately identical by requirement. Outside them the shells differ: the twenty-two opening section titles are all distinct, no section-2 or section-3 title is repeated across lessons, numbered section counts range from five to eight, examiner-trap counts range from five to nine, revision-note counts range from nine to fifteen, the trap-block heading varies across nine wordings, and the visual grammar differs across all twenty-two - container diagrams, stacked ladders, feedback loops, message panels, four-party conflict maps, chain breaks, arithmetic panels, branching diagrams, side-switch inversions, curriculum rails, strategic contrasts, decision routes, bottleneck maps, natural-experiment panels, market panels, actor cards, audit ledgers, mechanism chains, structure panels, two-axis plots, debate panels and clause-to-script rails |
| Mains word ceilings | 150 words for 10 marks and 250 for 15 and 20 marks, the established repository convention |
| Final Mains model lengths | Q1 150, Q2 146, Q3 247, Q4 249, Q5 244, Q6 248 - each within its stated ceiling, with every named evidence item and every unique scoring note retained |

## Book evidence and its controls

1. `books\WORLD HISTORY -- NORMAN LOWE -- ENGLISH  ##.pdf`, read through the locally held full-text extraction at `attempts\_gs_source_text\WORLD_HISTORY_NORMAN_LOWE_ENGLISH_pdf.txt` - the folder's designated world-history spine and the **one core OCR history source** used for this topic under the bounded History research rule. It supplied: the definition of the region as the countries of South America, Central America including Mexico, and Caribbean islands such as Cuba, Jamaica and Hispaniola, the last divided into Haiti and the Dominican Republic; the record that these states gained independence from Spain, and Brazil from Portugal, in the early nineteenth century, that Portuguese is the main language in Brazil, and that they shared underdevelopment industrially and agriculturally with massive problems of poverty and illiteracy and unstable political systems, with revolutions, coups and assassinations commonplace and progress slow and uneven; the statement that there was no tradition of democracy except in Chile and that states were dominated by groups of wealthy landowners and run by military dictators (caudillos); the statement that many countries were heavily dependent for exports on a limited range of products, sometimes a single commodity, and that a fall in the world price of that commodity would be a major disaster; and the statement that agriculture remained backward because peasant labour was so plentiful and cheap that wealthy estate owners had no need to modernise, with estate owners ruling their peasants like feudal monarchs.
2. **Control result for the nineteenth-century core.** The same extracted text was searched term by term for every central name of this topic. The searches returned **zero** occurrences of "San Martin", "Toussaint", "Dessalines", "Hidalgo", "Morelos", "Iturbide", "Gran Colombia", "Ayacucho", "creole" and "Pedro I"; the only occurrences of "Bolivar" are twentieth-century references to Bolivarian politics; the only occurrences of "Haiti" are twentieth-century; the only occurrence of "caudillo" in a Latin American sense is the passage cited above, with one unrelated European use; and "Monroe" appears only as the person for whom a West African capital is named. This confirms the folder README's statement that the book begins in 1914 and is structurally insufficient for Topics 01-08, and the book is therefore **not cited anywhere in this session for the narrative of any independence movement**. The zero-return is recorded as evidence of a check performed, and the items listed above are the entirety of what the book legitimately supplies.
3. **Period control on the book.** The chapter from which the usable material is drawn concerns twentieth-century Latin America and is owned by another topic. Only its structural statements - the regional definition, the post-independence condition of the states, the caudillo category, single-commodity export vulnerability and the labour-abundance explanation of agrarian stagnation - are used. None of its twentieth-century narrative, cases, interventions, investment detail, debt figures or named episodes is imported, and Lesson 19 states this restriction on its face.
4. **Bounded-research control.** No second book was read for this topic. Under the bounded History research rule the substantive base is the complete basic owner, the complete advanced owner and this one core OCR history source, and no optional corroboration, alternative textbook or supplementary dossier was consulted. Where the bounded base leaves a point unresolved, it is recorded below as open uncertainty rather than filled from outside.
5. **Quotation control.** Every quoted phrase in this session is taken verbatim from the extracted text of the book named above or from the canonical files named above. No participant in these events is quoted, because no authenticated text of any statement by any of them is held in this evidence base; documents such as the Cartagena Manifesto, the Jamaica Letter, the Plan of Iguala, the Sentiments of the Nation and the Monroe message are described by their content and never rendered as quotation.

## The single bounded material-fact verification pass

⚠️ **Why the pass was necessary.** The two canonical files supply a ten-marker timeline, the five paths, the actors, the social table, the comparison and the complete answer architecture, and they deliberately do not narrate the connecting events. Several of those events are standard essentials without which the teaching would have to use vague formulations - "a reform programme was undertaken", "a parliament met in Spain", "a plan was proclaimed", "the two liberators met" - where the standard account has exact names, dates and instruments. The governing bounded-research rule permits exactly one bounded pass for a short pre-declared list of standard connecting names and dates whose omission would create a real syllabus gap, and requires the provenance to be recorded here rather than in learner-facing labels. One such pass was run, confined to the list below; the facts it settled are taught as ordinary verified history and carry no pass label anywhere in the session.

| Cluster verified | Items carried |
|---|---|
| Colonial institutions and vocabulary | Peninsular, creole, mestizo, mulatto and pardo, Indigenous, enslaved and free person of colour as legal and fiscal statuses; encomienda, hacienda, mita, tribute, community land and plantation; viceroy, captain-general and *audiencia* as the offices filled from Spain |
| Imperial reform | The Bourbon programme and its most intensive phase under Charles III; new viceroyalties of New Granada and the River Plate; the intendancy system; reduced creole officeholding; fiscal intensification and the tobacco and spirits monopolies; militia expansion; the expulsion of the Jesuits from Portuguese territories in 1759 and from the Spanish empire in 1767; the *comercio libre* decree of 1778 and its restriction to trade within the empire; the Pombaline counterpart and the move of the Brazilian capital to Rio de Janeiro |
| The risings of the 1780s | The Andean rising of 1780-83 under Jose Gabriel Condorcanqui taking the name Tupac Amaru II; the parallel Upper Peru movement under Tupac Katari; suppression by 1783; the Comuneros of New Granada in 1781, their fiscal origin, the negotiated concessions and their repudiation |
| The Haitian sequence | Saint-Domingue as the western third of Hispaniola; the free-coloured claim of 1790-91; the northern rising of August 1791; Spanish and British intervention from 1793; French revolutionary abolition in 1793-94; Toussaint Louverture's ascendancy and the autonomous constitution of 1801; the French expedition of 1802; Toussaint's deportation and death in captivity in 1803; independence on 1 January 1804 under Dessalines; the adoption of an Indigenous name for the island; French recognition in 1825 in exchange for an indemnity of 150 million francs, reduced to 90 million in 1838; Petion's supply of asylum, arms and ships to Bolivar in 1816 on the condition of emancipation |
| The Iberian crisis | French entry into Spain in 1807-08; the Bayonne abdications and Joseph's enthronement; Spanish provincial juntas for Ferdinand VII; the Junta Central of 1808-09 and its invitation to American representation; the Regency of 1810; the departure of the Portuguese court from Lisbon in late 1807 under British escort, its arrival at Rio in 1808 and the opening of Brazilian ports |
| Cadiz | The Cortes summoned in 1810; the Constitution of 1812 with national sovereignty, a limited hereditary monarchy, a single legislature, press freedom, abolition of seigneurial jurisdiction and the formula of Spaniards of both hemispheres; the abolition of Indigenous tribute and labour drafts; the refusal of proportionate representation through the exclusion of those of African descent from citizenship and from the representation base; the refusal of trade with foreign nations; the restoration of 1814 with the annulment of the constitution and the order for reconquest; Riego's revolt of 1820 among troops assembled for America and the restoration of the constitution |
| Mexico | Hidalgo as parish priest at Dolores; the Grito of 16 September 1810 invoking religion and Ferdinand VII; the storming of Guanajuato; Hidalgo's execution in 1811; his decrees abolishing slavery and tribute and restoring community land; Morelos's reorganisation of the insurgency; the congress of Chilpancingo of 1813 and its declaration of independence; the charter of Apatzingan of 1814; the content of the Sentiments of the Nation; Morelos's execution in 1815; Guerrero's continuing guerrilla resistance; Iturbide's command in 1820 and his negotiation with Guerrero; the Plan of Iguala of 24 February 1821 and its three guarantees; the Army of the Three Guarantees; the Treaty of Cordoba of August 1821 signed by O'Donoju and later repudiated by Spain; Iturbide's proclamation as emperor in 1822 and deposition in 1823; the federal republic of 1824; the separation of Central America in 1821, its brief annexation, its federation of 1823 and its break-up between 1838 and 1841 |
| Northern South America | The Caracas junta of April 1810; the declaration of 5 July 1811 and the federal constitution; the fall of the First Republic in 1812 and the earthquake used in royalist preaching; the Cartagena Manifesto of 1812 and its diagnosis; the Admirable Campaign of 1813 and the Second Republic; the war-to-the-death policy and its distinction between Spaniards and Americans; the fall of the Second Republic in 1814 to llanero forces under Boves; the Jamaica Letter of 1815 and its arguments including the proposal of a congress at the isthmus of Panama; the Angostura base of 1817-18; the alliance with Paez; the British Legion; the Congress of Angostura of February 1819; Boyaca on 7 August 1819 and the fall of Bogota; the proclamation of Gran Colombia in December 1819; the constitution of Cucuta of 1821 with Santander as vice-president; Carabobo on 24 June 1821; Pichincha on 24 May 1822 under Sucre; the dissolution of Gran Colombia in 1830 into Venezuela, New Granada and Ecuador |
| Southern South America | The Buenos Aires junta of May 1810; the repeated defeats of expeditions into Upper Peru between 1811 and 1815; San Martin's return and the Cuyo governorship; the formation and training of the Army of the Andes at Mendoza between 1814 and 1816; the Congress of Tucuman of 1816; the crossing of January 1817 by multiple passes; Chacabuco on 12 February 1817; O'Higgins as Supreme Director; the Chilean declaration of independence of 12 February 1818; Maipu on 5 April 1818; the Chilean squadron under Cochrane; the sailing of the Peruvian expedition in 1820; O'Higgins's abolition of titles of nobility and measures against slavery and the fall of his government in 1823 |
| Peru and the end of the war | The landing of 1820, the blockade and the negotiations; the royalist withdrawal to the highlands; the entry into Lima and the proclamation of Peruvian independence in July 1821; San Martin as Protector and his free-womb law and abolition of tribute and the labour draft; the Guayaquil meeting of 26-27 July 1822 and the absence of any public record of its content; San Martin's resignation and withdrawal; Bolivar's extraordinary powers in Peru in 1823-24; Junin on 6 August 1824; Ayacucho on 9 December 1824 under Sucre with the capture of the viceroy; the constitution of Upper Peru as Bolivia in 1825 with Sucre as its first effective head; the surrender of residual garrisons by 1826; the Congress of Panama of 1826, its purpose, its limited attendance and its effective non-ratification |
| Brazil | The opening of the ports in 1808 and the establishment of courts, a treasury, a bank, a military academy, a press and a botanical garden at Rio; the elevation of Brazil to a kingdom united with Portugal and the Algarves in 1815; the reign of Joao VI from 1816; the Porto revolt of 1820-21 and the Cortes's demand for the king's return and Brazil's subordination; the king's return in 1821 leaving Pedro as regent; the Fico of January 1822; the declaration of 7 September 1822; the coronation as Emperor Pedro I in December 1822; the dissolution of the constituent assembly and the granted constitution of 1824 with a moderating power; the suppression of north-eastern provincial revolt; Portuguese recognition in 1825 under British mediation with compensation; the abdication of 1831 |
| Participants | The service of enslaved men on both sides in exchange for freedom; the extensive service of free people of colour on both sides; the division of the church between a largely royalist hierarchy and insurgent parish clergy; the royalism of monopoly-port merchant houses and the patriot orientation of open-trade merchants; the London financing of patriot governments; the service of British and Irish volunteers and of foreign naval officers; the participation of women as conspirators, couriers, financiers, suppliers, camp sustainers and in cases as combatants and commanders, with several executed for conspiracy |
| External actors | British commercial, naval, financial and diplomatic involvement and recognition in the mid-1820s; United States recognition from 1822; the Monroe message of 2 December 1823 and its four principles; Canning's proposal of a joint declaration and its rejection; the French restoration of Ferdinand VII's absolute authority in Spain in 1823 and the discussion, without execution, of a comparable American intervention |
| Abolition and its instruments | Freedom for military service, free-womb laws and final abolition statutes as three distinct instruments; free-womb laws in the River Plate in 1813, in Gran Colombia in 1821, in Peru in 1821 and in Brazil in 1871; final abolition in Chile in 1823, Mexico in 1829, Colombia in 1851, Argentina in 1853, Peru and Venezuela in 1854, Cuba in 1886 and Brazil in 1888; the repeated abolition and restoration of Indigenous tribute; the post-independence division of community holdings into individual parcels and the later disentailment of corporate landholdings |
| Post-independence economy | War damage to mining, livestock, internal trade and colonial revenue systems; the opening of ports and the establishment of foreign merchant houses; the London loan boom of the 1820s, the accompanying mining speculation and the widespread default; the reconstruction of state finance on customs duties |

- No content from the excluded derived or generated package tree was read, cited or used, in accordance with the permanent live-session source exclusion; no learner-v2 artifact was used either, including this subject's own v2 learning sessions and solved workbooks and the complete-topic-package file that duplicates this topic.

- No casualty figure appears anywhere in this session - not for the Andean risings, the Haitian war, the French expedition, the storming of Guanajuato, the war to the death, the llanero campaigns, the Andes crossing, Boyaca, Carabobo, Pichincha, Junin, Ayacucho, the Brazilian provincial suppressions or any repression by either side. Mortality was heavy in several of these; that qualitative statement is asserted and no number is given, estimated or implied.
- No population, enslaved-population, manumission, compensation, landholding, output, trade, loan, default, customs, revenue or commodity-price figure appears anywhere. No count of the new states is given. No force strength or order of battle is given for any engagement. No participation figure is given for women, for Indigenous communities, for free people of colour or for foreign volunteers.
- **The only quantitative figures used as historical evidence anywhere in this session are the Haitian indemnity of 150 million francs and its reduction to 90 million in 1838.** ⚠️ The claim is deliberately narrow: the numbers reported within the single current linkage - more than eighty delegations, and the 56th General Assembly of the Organization of American States - are twenty-first-century reporting about a commemoration, are explicitly excluded from that claim, and support no historical statement anywhere in the teaching.
- Colonial racial categories are handled under a stated discipline: they are taught as legal and fiscal statuses created and enforced by a colonial state, defined on first use, with their regional variation stated rather than averaged, and with claims about what any group "wanted" made only from a recorded action, decree or petition in a named region. Enslaved populations are described by what they did and what was done to them under named policies, never by collective characterisation. Atrocity and coercion are attributed to identified regimes, commanders, decrees and policies, never to a nation or a people as such.
- No participant in these events is quoted. Documents are described by content and never rendered as quotation, because no authenticated text of any of them is held here.
- No historian is named anywhere in this session. The two owners record debate positions without attaching them to scholars and the core OCR source names none for this period; the attribution limit is stated in Lesson 21 and enforced throughout.
- Objective-question neutrality is preserved absolutely: no verified objective item touching this topic exists in any audited ledger, none is displayed, none is reconstructed, and no key, elimination clue or corrective mapping appears anywhere.
- The American Revolution, the French Revolution, the Congress of Vienna and the Concert of Europe, New Imperialism and the partition of Africa, twentieth-century Latin America, post-1945 Asian and African decolonisation, and Indian nationalism are all routed to their owners; each is named where it arises and none is narrated here.
- Remaining open uncertainty, recorded rather than filled: the content of the Guayaquil meeting of July 1822; the relative weight of resistance and disease in defeating the French expedition in Haiti; the internal social and labour settlement of Haiti after 1804; the scale of enslaved enlistment and of emancipation actually effected under the wartime decrees on either side; the extent to which promises of land and rank to ordinary soldiers were honoured; the distribution of opinion inside American societies in 1808-10 and the extent of Peruvian patriot support; the practical application of the Constitution of 1812 in American territories; the operation of caste abolition in practice after independence; the scale of community-land transfer and its regional variation; the intentions behind the discussed Holy Alliance intervention; the counterfactual question of what alternative economic strategy was available in the 1820s; and the explanation of the Chilean exception, which is recorded and left to its owner.
